Part: Linear functions, polyhedra and linear programming problems (60)

1. Linear functions (12)

lp-f-01-01 · level facile · notes
What is the gradient of the linear function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}$ at a point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n$?
  1. The vector $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$
  2. The scalar ${\boldsymbol c}'\bar{\boldsymbol x}$
  3. The zero vector, since the function is linear
  4. The vector ${\boldsymbol c}$, the same at every point
Explanation. The partial derivative with respect to $x_j$ is $c_j$ at every point: $\nabla f = {\boldsymbol c}$. It is the Hessian, not the gradient, that is zero.
lp-f-01-02 · level facile · notes
What is the Hessian of the linear function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}$, with ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n$?
  1. The $n \times n$ identity matrix
  2. The matrix ${\boldsymbol c}\,{\boldsymbol c}'$
  3. The diagonal matrix with entries $c_1, \dots, c_n$
  4. The $n \times n$ zero matrix
Explanation. The first derivatives $c_j$ are constant, so all the second derivatives are zero.
lp-f-01-03 · level facile · notes
What are the level sets of a linear function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol c} \ne {\boldsymbol 0}$?
  1. Half-lines leaving the origin in the direction of ${\boldsymbol c}$
  2. Hyperplanes parallel to the gradient ${\boldsymbol c}$
  3. Hyperplanes perpendicular to the gradient ${\boldsymbol c}$
  4. Half-spaces bounded by the hyperplane ${\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} = 0$
Explanation. The set $\{{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} = k\}$ is a hyperplane, and ${\boldsymbol c}$ is perpendicular to it.
lp-f-01-04 · level facile · notes
Which of the following functions $f : \R^3 \to \R$ is a linear function?
  1. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = |x_1| + x_2 - x_3$
  2. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 - x_2 + x_3$
  3. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1 x_2 + x_3$
  4. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 3x_1 - 2x_2 + x_3$
Explanation. A linear function has the form $\sum_j c_j x_j = {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}$: products, powers and absolute values of the variables are not allowed.
lp-f-01-05 · level facile · notes
For $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 8x_1 + 4x_2$, which point belongs to the level set $8x_1 + 4x_2 = 16$?
  1. $(0, 2)$
  2. $(2, 0)$
  3. $(1, 1)$
  4. $(4, 0)$
Explanation. $8 \cdot 2 + 4 \cdot 0 = 16$; at the other points the function takes the values $8$, $32$ and $12$.
lp-f-01-06 · level facile · notes
For $f({\boldsymbol x}) = -8x_1 - 4x_2$, in which direction does the function increase most rapidly?
  1. In the direction of $(8, 4)$
  2. In the direction of $(-8, -4)$
  3. In no direction, since the coefficients are negative
  4. In the direction of $(4, -8)$
Explanation. The function increases in the direction of the gradient ${\boldsymbol c} = (-8,-4)$; along $(4,-8)$, perpendicular to the gradient, it is constant.
lp-f-01-07 · level facile · notes
What is the graph of the function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 8x_1 + 4x_2$ in $\R^3$?
  1. A paraboloid
  2. A plane through the origin
  3. A half-space
  4. A line through the origin
Explanation. The graph $\{({\boldsymbol x}, f({\boldsymbol x}))\}$ of a linear function of two variables is the plane $x_3 = 8x_1 + 4x_2$, which contains the origin.
lp-f-01-08 · level facile · notes
For the linear function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 3x_1 - 2x_2$ and any point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$, what is $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol d}) - f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$ with ${\boldsymbol d} = (1, 1)$?
  1. $5$, whatever $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is
  2. $1$, whatever $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is
  3. $-1$, whatever $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is
  4. It depends on the point $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$
Explanation. By linearity $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol d}) - f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol d} = 3 \cdot 1 - 2 \cdot 1 = 1$: the change does not depend on the starting point.
lp-f-01-09 · level facile · notes
Let $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol c} \in \R^n$, ${\boldsymbol c} \ne {\boldsymbol 0}$. For which directions ${\boldsymbol d}$ do we have $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x} + t\,{\boldsymbol d}) = f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$ for every $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and every $t \in \R$?
  1. Only for ${\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol 0}$
  2. For every ${\boldsymbol d}$ with ${\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol d} = 0$
  3. For every ${\boldsymbol d}$ with ${\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol d} > 0$
  4. Only for ${\boldsymbol d}$ parallel to ${\boldsymbol c}$
Explanation. $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x} + t\,{\boldsymbol d}) = f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) + t\,{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol d}$: the value is constant exactly along the directions perpendicular to the gradient, i.e., inside the level sets.
lp-f-01-10 · level facile · notes
For the linear function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 4x_1 + 2x_2$, what do the level lines $4x_1 + 2x_2 = k$ look like as $k \in \R$ varies?
  1. They are parallel to each other and perpendicular to the vector $(4, 2)$
  2. They are parallel to each other and parallel to the vector $(4, 2)$
  3. They are concentric circles centred at the origin
  4. They all pass through the origin, with different slopes
Explanation. The gradient $(4, 2)$ is the same for every $k$: all the level lines are perpendicular to it, hence parallel to each other; only the one with $k = 0$ passes through the origin.
lp-f-01-11 · level facile · notes
Let $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}$ be a linear function on $\R^3$ and let $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ be a point with $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 5$. What is $f(2\,\bar{\boldsymbol x})$?
  1. $25$
  2. $7$
  3. $5$
  4. $10$
Explanation. A linear function is homogeneous: $f(2\,\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol c}'(2\,\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 2\,f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 10$. Adding $2$ or squaring are typical mistakes.
lp-f-01-12 · level facile · notes
Let $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}$ be a linear function on $\R^n$, with $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 4$ and $f(\hat{\boldsymbol x}) = 10$ at two points $\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \hat{\boldsymbol x}$. What is the value of $f$ at the midpoint $\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\boldsymbol x} + \hat{\boldsymbol x})$?
  1. $14$
  2. $7$
  3. $5$
  4. It cannot be said without knowing ${\boldsymbol c}$
Explanation. By linearity $f(\frac{1}{2}\bar{\boldsymbol x} + \frac{1}{2}\hat{\boldsymbol x}) = \frac{1}{2}f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) + \frac{1}{2}f(\hat{\boldsymbol x}) = 2 + 5 = 7$, whatever ${\boldsymbol c}$ is.

2. Half-spaces and polyhedra (12)

lp-f-02-01 · level facile · notes
Given ${\boldsymbol a} \in \R^{1 \times n}$ and $b \in \R$, which set is a half-space?
  1. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n : {\boldsymbol a}\,{\boldsymbol x} \le b\}$
  2. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n : {\boldsymbol a}\,{\boldsymbol x} \le b,~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$
  3. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n : {\boldsymbol a}\,{\boldsymbol x} = b\}$
  4. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n : x_1^2 + \dots + x_n^2 \le b\}$
Explanation. A half-space is the set of points satisfying one linear inequality; with the equality we obtain its supporting hyperplane.
lp-f-02-02 · level facile · notes
What is the supporting hyperplane of the half-space $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : -3x_1 + 2x_2 \le 6\}$?
  1. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : -3x_1 + 2x_2 = 6\}$
  2. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : -3x_1 + 2x_2 \ge 6\}$
  3. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : -3x_1 + 2x_2 = 0\}$
  4. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : -3x_1 + 2x_2 < 6\}$
Explanation. The supporting hyperplane is obtained by replacing the inequality with the equality, with the same right-hand side.
lp-f-02-03 · level facile · notes
What is a polyhedron in $\R^n$?
  1. The intersection of any number, even infinite, of half-spaces
  2. Any convex and closed set
  3. The union of a finite number of half-spaces
  4. The intersection of a finite number of half-spaces
Explanation. A polyhedron is defined by a finite number of linear inequalities (and possibly equalities); infinitely many half-spaces give sets such as a disc, convex but not polyhedra.
lp-f-02-04 · level facile · notes
What is a polytope?
  1. A polyhedron with at least one vertex
  2. A polyhedron in $\R^2$
  3. A polyhedron defined by equalities only
  4. A bounded polyhedron
Explanation. A polytope is a bounded polyhedron; for instance, the orthant $\{{\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ has a vertex but it is not bounded.
lp-f-02-05 · level facile · notes
Given ${\boldsymbol a} \in \R^{1 \times n}$, ${\boldsymbol a} \ne {\boldsymbol 0}$, and $b \in \R$, is the hyperplane $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n : {\boldsymbol a}\,{\boldsymbol x} = b\}$ a polyhedron?
  1. Yes: it is the union of the half-spaces ${\boldsymbol a}\,{\boldsymbol x} \le b$ and ${\boldsymbol a}\,{\boldsymbol x} \ge b$
  2. No: a polyhedron contains inequalities only
  3. Yes: it coincides with the half-space ${\boldsymbol a}\,{\boldsymbol x} \le b$
  4. Yes: it is the intersection of the half-spaces ${\boldsymbol a}\,{\boldsymbol x} \le b$ and $-{\boldsymbol a}\,{\boldsymbol x} \le -b$
Explanation. An equality is equivalent to two opposite inequalities; the union of the two half-spaces would instead be the whole $\R^n$.
lp-f-02-06 · level facile · notes
Which of the following polyhedra in $\R^2$ is bounded?
  1. $\{x_1 + x_2 \ge 4,~ x_1 \ge 0,~ x_2 \ge 0\}$
  2. $\{x_1 + x_2 \le 4,~ x_1 \ge 0,~ x_2 \ge 0\}$
  3. $\{x_1 - x_2 \le 1,~ x_1 \ge 0,~ x_2 \ge 0\}$
  4. $\{x_1 \le 4,~ x_2 \le 4\}$
Explanation. The first one is the triangle with vertices $(0,0)$, $(4,0)$, $(0,4)$: ${\boldsymbol \beta} = (4,4)$ suffices. The others contain half-lines.
lp-f-02-07 · level facile · notes
A polyhedron $P \subseteq \R^2$ contains the points $(3,5)$ and $(5,3)$. Which of the following points certainly belongs to $P$?
  1. $(1, -1)$
  2. $(8, 8)$
  3. $(6, 2)$
  4. $(4, 4)$
Explanation. A polyhedron is convex: it contains $\lambda(3,5) + (1-\lambda)(5,3)$ for $\lambda \in [0,1]$; with $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$ we get $(4,4)$. $(6,2)$ lies on the line but outside the segment.
lp-f-02-08 · level facile · notes
Which of the following subsets of $\R^2$ is not a polyhedron?
  1. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : x_1 + x_2 = 1\}$
  2. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : x_1 \ge 0\}$
  3. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : x_1^2 + x_2^2 \le 1\}$
  4. $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : 0 \le x_1 \le 1,~ 0 \le x_2 \le 1\}$
Explanation. The disc cannot be obtained with a finite number of linear inequalities. The other three are a hyperplane (two inequalities), a half-space and a square (four inequalities).
lp-f-02-09 · level facile · notes
A polyhedron $P \subseteq \R^n$ is bounded if it is contained in a ball of finite radius. Is the polyhedron $P = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : x_1 \le 1,~ -x_1 \le 1\}$ bounded?
  1. No: it is not a polyhedron
  2. No: $x_2$ can grow without bound
  3. Yes: it is defined by a finite number of half-spaces
  4. Yes: we have $-1 \le x_1 \le 1$
Explanation. $P$ is the vertical strip $-1 \le x_1 \le 1$: no constraint bounds $x_2$, so the points $(0, t)$ with large $t$ are in $P$. A finite number of half-spaces does not guarantee boundedness.
lp-f-02-10 · level facile · notes
Consider the half-space $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : x_1 + 2x_2 \le 4\}$. Which vector is perpendicular to its supporting hyperplane and points out of the half-space?
  1. $(1, 2)$
  2. $(2, 1)$
  3. $(-1, -2)$
  4. $(2, -1)$
Explanation. The coefficient vector $(1, 2)$ is perpendicular to the line $x_1 + 2x_2 = 4$ and, moving along it, $x_1 + 2x_2$ grows beyond $4$. The vector $(-1,-2)$ points inside, $(2,-1)$ is parallel to the line and $(2,1)$ is not perpendicular to it.
lp-f-02-11 · level facile · notes
Which point belongs to the supporting hyperplane of the half-space $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : 2x_1 - x_2 \le 4\}$, i.e., to the line $2x_1 - x_2 = 4$?
  1. $(2, 0)$
  2. $(1, -1)$
  3. $(3, 1)$
  4. $(0, 4)$
Explanation. $2 \cdot 2 - 0 = 4$. At the other points $2x_1 - x_2$ takes the values $-4$, $3$ and $5$: the first two lie in the half-space but not on the hyperplane, the last one is outside.
lp-f-02-12 · level facile · notes
A polyhedron is the intersection of a finite number of half-spaces. Let $P_1$ and $P_2$ be two polyhedra in $\R^n$. Which set is always a polyhedron?
  1. $P_1 \cap P_2$
  2. The set of points at distance at most $1$ from $P_1$
  3. $\R^n \setminus P_1$
  4. $P_1 \cup P_2$
Explanation. $P_1 \cap P_2$ is defined by the union of the two finite systems of inequalities. Union and complement may be non-convex, and the neighbourhood of a polyhedron has curved boundaries in general.

3. Linear optimization problems (12)

lp-f-03-01 · level facile · notes
Consider the LP problem in minimization form $\min\{f({\boldsymbol x}) : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$, with $f$ linear and feasible region $F \subseteq \R^n$. When is a point $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(LP)$ an optimal solution?
  1. If $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(LP) \in F$ and $f(\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(LP)) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$
  2. If $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(LP)$ is the point of $F$ closest to the origin
  3. If $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(LP) \in F$ and $f(\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(LP)) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$ for at least one ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$
  4. If $f(\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(LP)) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n$
Explanation. An optimal solution must be feasible and not worse than all the feasible solutions.
lp-f-03-02 · level facile · notes
An LP problem in maximization form, with optimal value $z(LP)$, has three known feasible solutions, of value $3$, $7$ and $5$. What can be concluded about $z(LP)$?
  1. $z(LP) \ge 15$
  2. $z(LP) \le 7$
  3. $z(LP) \ge 7$
  4. $z(LP) = 7$
Explanation. In maximization form every feasible solution gives a lower bound $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le z(LP)$; the best one is $7$. Whether it is optimal is unknown.
lp-f-03-03 · level facile · notes
An LP problem has a linear objective function and a feasible region defined by a finite number of linear constraints. For which reasons may it have no optimal solution?
  1. Also because the supremum of the objective function over $F$ may be finite but not attained
  2. Also because the feasible region is unbounded
  3. Also because it has several optimal solutions
  4. Only because it is infeasible or unbounded
Explanation. A polyhedron is closed and the function is linear: if the problem is feasible and not unbounded, the optimum is attained. An unbounded region does not prevent an optimum.
lp-f-03-04 · level teorema · notes
Let $F \subseteq \R^n$ be the feasible region of an LP problem, ${\boldsymbol c} \in \R^n$ and $a \in \R$, and assume that $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$ exists. Which property always holds?
  1. $\min\{a + {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\} = a \cdot \min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$
  2. $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\} = \max\{-{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$
  3. For $a > 0$: $\min\{a\,{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\} = a\,\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$
  4. For $a < 0$: $\min\{a\,{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\} = a\,\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$
Explanation. Multiplying by $a > 0$ does not change the optimal solutions. For $a < 0$ the minimum becomes $a$ times the maximum, a constant is added, and $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}\} = -\max\{-{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}\}$.
lp-f-03-05 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution $\tilde x$ and the optimal value $z(LP)$ of the LP problem in one variable $\min\{2x + 1 : -2 \le x \le 3\}$?
  1. $\tilde x = 0$, $z(LP) = 1$
  2. $\tilde x = -2$, $z(LP) = -3$
  3. $\tilde x = -2$, $z(LP) = -4$
  4. $\tilde x = 3$, $z(LP) = 7$
Explanation. The function is strictly increasing ($f'(x) = 2 > 0$), so the minimum is at the left endpoint: $2(-2) + 1 = -3$.
lp-f-03-06 · level facile · notes
Knowing that $\min\{\frac{1}{2}x : -5 \le x \le 5\} = -\frac{5}{2}$, what is $\min\{\frac{1}{2}x - 2 : -5 \le x \le 5\}$?
  1. $-\frac{5}{2}$
  2. $-\frac{1}{2}$
  3. $-\frac{9}{2}$
  4. $5$
Explanation. Adding a constant does not change the optimal solutions: the optimal value is $-2 + (-\frac{5}{2}) = -\frac{9}{2}$.
lp-f-03-07 · level facile · notes
What can be said about the LP problem in one variable $\max\{2x + 1 : x \ge -2\}$ and its optimal value $z(LP)$?
  1. It has an optimal solution, since the function is linear
  2. It is infeasible
  3. It is unbounded: $z(LP) = +\infty$
  4. It has optimal solution $\tilde x = -2$ with $z(LP) = -3$
Explanation. For every $M \ge 0$ the feasible solution $x = M$ has value $2M + 1 > M$: the objective function grows without bound.
lp-f-03-08 · level facile · notes
In an LP problem in minimization form, with optimal value $z(LP)$, a feasible solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ with objective function value $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 12$ is known. What can be concluded?
  1. That $z(LP) = 12$
  2. Nothing about $z(LP)$
  3. The upper bound $z(LP) \le 12$
  4. The lower bound $z(LP) \ge 12$
Explanation. The minimum does not exceed the value of any feasible solution: $UB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 12 \ge z(LP)$. In maximization form a feasible solution gives a lower bound instead.
lp-f-03-09 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution $\tilde x$ and the optimal value $z(LP)$ of the LP problem in one variable $\max\{-3x + 2 : 1 \le x \le 4\}$?
  1. $\tilde x = 4$, $z(LP) = -10$
  2. $\tilde x = 4$, $z(LP) = 14$
  3. $\tilde x = 1$, $z(LP) = -1$
  4. $\tilde x = 0$, $z(LP) = 2$
Explanation. The coefficient $-3$ is negative: the function decreases with $x$ and the maximum is at the left end point $x = 1$, with value $-3 + 2 = -1$. The point $x = 0$ is not feasible.
lp-f-03-10 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an LP problem and assume that $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\} = 5$. What can be said about $\min\{-2\,{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$?
  1. It equals $-\frac{5}{2}$, i.e., $5 / (-2)$
  2. It equals $10$, i.e., $2 \cdot 5$
  3. It equals $-10$, i.e., $-2 \cdot 5$
  4. It cannot be deduced: it equals $-2\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$
Explanation. Multiplying by $a = -2 < 0$ swaps minimum and maximum: $\min\{-2\,{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}\} = -2\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}\}$, which the minimum does not determine. The rule $\min\{a f\} = a\min\{f\}$ holds only for $a > 0$.
lp-f-03-11 · level teorema · notes
Consider an LP problem $\max\{f({\boldsymbol x}) : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$, with $f$ linear and feasible region $F$. Which statement about optimal solutions and optimal value $z(LP)$ is correct?
  1. If it exists, the optimal solution is unique, while the optimal value may not be unique
  2. If it exists, the optimal value is unique, while there can be more than one optimal solution
  3. Different optimal solutions can have different optimal values of the objective function
  4. If they exist, the optimal solution and the optimal value are always both unique
Explanation. The optimal value is the maximum of $f$ over $F$, hence it is unique; all optimal solutions share that value, but they can be infinitely many (for instance a whole edge).
lp-f-03-12 · level facile · notes
Consider the LP problem $\max\{x_1 + x_2 : x_1 + x_2 \le 3,~ x_1, x_2 \ge 0\}$. Which point is a feasible but not optimal solution?
  1. $(-1, 2)$
  2. $(1, 1)$
  3. $(2, 1)$
  4. $(3, 1)$
Explanation. $(1, 1)$ satisfies the constraints and has value $2 < 3 = z(LP)$. The point $(2, 1)$ is optimal (value $3$), $(3, 1)$ violates $x_1 + x_2 \le 3$ and $(-1, 2)$ violates $x_1 \ge 0$.

4. Linear Programming problems (12)

lp-f-04-01 · level facile · notes
Given ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times n}$, ${\boldsymbol b} \in \R^m$ and ${\boldsymbol c} \in \R^n$, which problem is in canonical maximization form?
  1. $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$
  2. $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b}\}$
  3. $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$
  4. $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$
Explanation. Canonical maximization form: maximization, less-than-or-equal-to constraints and non-negative variables. With equality constraints we have the standard form.
lp-f-04-02 · level facile · notes
Given ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times n}$, ${\boldsymbol b} \in \R^m$ and ${\boldsymbol c} \in \R^n$, which problem is in standard maximization form?
  1. $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol 0}\}$
  2. $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$
  3. $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$
  4. $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b}\}$
Explanation. Standard maximization form: maximization, equality constraints and non-negative variables.
lp-f-04-03 · level facile · notes
The feasible region of an LP problem in canonical maximization form with $n$ variables and $m$ constraints is the intersection of how many half-spaces?
  1. $m + n$
  2. $m \cdot n$
  3. $m$
  4. $n$
Explanation. Each less-than-or-equal-to constraint is a half-space, and each non-negativity constraint $x_j \ge 0$ is another half-space: $m + n$.
lp-f-04-04 · level facile · notes
In the graphical method for an LP problem in canonical maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with two variables and a bounded non-empty feasible region $F$, where is an optimal solution?
  1. At the last point of $F$ touched by the level lines moved in the direction of the gradient ${\boldsymbol c}$
  2. At the last point of $F$ touched by the level lines moved in the direction of $-{\boldsymbol c}$
  3. At the point of $F$ closest to the origin, measured along the direction of the gradient ${\boldsymbol c}$
  4. At the point where all the constraint lines intersect, if such a point exists
Explanation. In maximization form the function increases along ${\boldsymbol c}$: the level line is moved in that direction as long as it still touches $F$.
lp-f-04-05 · level facile · notes
For the LP problem $\max\{5x_1 + 2x_2 : -x_1 + 3x_2 \le 12,~ x_1 + 2x_2 \le 12,~ 3x_1 - 2x_2 \le 8,~ x_1, x_2 \ge 0\}$, which lower bound $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$ on the optimal value does the point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = (0, 4)$ give?
  1. $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 8$
  2. $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 20$
  3. $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 12$
  4. None: $(0,4)$ is not feasible
Explanation. $(0,4)$ satisfies the constraints ($12 \le 12$, $8 \le 12$, $-8 \le 8$) and $LB = 5 \cdot 0 + 2 \cdot 4 = 8 \le z(LP)$.
lp-f-04-06 · level facile · notes
How is the feasible region of an LP problem in standard maximization form defined, with data ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times n}$, ${\boldsymbol b} \in \R^m$, ${\boldsymbol c} \in \R^n$?
  1. By the constraints ${\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b}$ only, with ${\boldsymbol x}$ free
  2. By the constraints ${\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b}$ and ${\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  3. By the constraints ${\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol b}$ and ${\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. By the constraints ${\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b}$ and ${\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. $F = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$: hyperplanes intersected with the non-negative orthant.
lp-f-04-07 · level facile · notes
In the graphical method for an LP problem in canonical maximization form with two variables and feasible region $F$, the last level line touching $F$, moving in the direction of the gradient, contains a whole bounded edge of $F$. What is concluded?
  1. The problem has infinitely many optimal solutions: all the points of the edge
  2. The problem has no optimal solution, since it is not unique
  3. Only the two end points of the edge are optimal solutions
  4. The problem is unbounded, since the optimal solutions are infinitely many
Explanation. All the points of the edge have the same value, the optimal one; as for every convex combination of optimal solutions, there are infinitely many.
lp-f-04-08 · level facile · notes
With the graphical method, what are an optimal solution and the optimal value of the LP problem $\max\{3x_1 + x_2 : x_1 + x_2 \le 4,~ x_1 \le 3,~ x_1, x_2 \ge 0\}$?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x} = (3, 0)$, $z(LP) = 9$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x} = (0, 4)$, $z(LP) = 4$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x} = (3, 1)$, $z(LP) = 10$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x} = (4, 0)$, $z(LP) = 12$
Explanation. The vertices of the feasible region are $(0,0)$, $(3,0)$, $(3,1)$ and $(0,4)$, with values $0$, $9$, $10$ and $4$. The point $(4,0)$ violates $x_1 \le 3$.
lp-f-04-09 · level facile · notes
An LP problem in canonical maximization form with $n$ variables and $m$ constraints is transformed into standard maximization form by adding one slack variable per constraint. How many variables and equality constraints does the new problem have?
  1. $n + m$ variables and $n + m$ constraints
  2. $n$ variables and $n + m$ constraints
  3. $n$ variables and $m$ constraints
  4. $n + m$ variables and $m$ constraints
Explanation. Each constraint ${\boldsymbol a}_i{\boldsymbol x} \le b_i$ becomes ${\boldsymbol a}_i{\boldsymbol x} + x_{n+i} = b_i$ with $x_{n+i} \ge 0$: the variables become $n + m$ and the constraints remain $m$.
lp-f-04-10 · level facile · notes
For the LP problem in standard form $\max\{2x_1 + x_2 : x_1 + x_2 + x_3 = 4,~ x_1 - x_2 + x_4 = 2,~ x_1, x_2, x_3, x_4 \ge 0\}$, which statement on a solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and on the lower bound $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$ is correct?
  1. $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = (2, 2, 0, 0)$ is feasible, with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 6$
  2. $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = (1, 1, 2, 2)$ is feasible, with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 7$
  3. $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = (4, 0, 0, -2)$ is feasible, with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 8$
  4. $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = (3, 1, 0, 0)$ is feasible, with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 7$
Explanation. $(3,1,0,0)$ satisfies $3+1+0 = 4$, $3-1+0 = 2$ and non-negativity, with value $2 \cdot 3 + 1 = 7$. $(1,1,2,2)$ is feasible but has value $3$; $(2,2,0,0)$ violates the second constraint and $(4,0,0,-2)$ non-negativity.
lp-f-04-11 · level facile · notes
Which point belongs to the feasible region of the LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{x_1 + 2x_2 : x_1 + x_2 + x_3 = 4,~ x_1, x_2, x_3 \ge 0\}$?
  1. $(2, 1, 2)$
  2. $(1, 2, 0)$
  3. $(1, 2, 1)$
  4. $(3, 2, -1)$
Explanation. $(1,2,1)$ satisfies the equality ($1 + 2 + 1 = 4$) and non-negativity. $(1,2,0)$ and $(2,1,2)$ give sums $3$ and $5$; $(3,2,-1)$ satisfies the equality but has $x_3 < 0$.
lp-f-04-12 · level facile · notes
For an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with optimal value $z(LP)$, for which points $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is the value ${\boldsymbol c}'\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ a lower bound $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le z(LP)$?
  1. Only for the optimal solutions $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ of the problem
  2. For every $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol A}\bar{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b}$, even with negative entries
  3. For every $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, even if ${\boldsymbol A}\bar{\boldsymbol x} \ne {\boldsymbol b}$
  4. For every $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol A}\bar{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. Every feasible solution, i.e., with ${\boldsymbol A}\bar{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, has value not larger than the maximum. An infeasible point may have a value larger than $z(LP)$.

5. Possible outcomes and equivalent formulations (12)

lp-f-05-01 · level facile · notes
Which statement about the possible outcomes of an LP problem is correct?
  1. An LP problem can be both infeasible and unbounded
  2. An LP problem with an unbounded feasible region is unbounded
  3. Exactly one of the following holds: infeasible, unbounded, admits an optimal solution
  4. A feasible LP problem always admits an optimal solution
Explanation. The three outcomes are mutually exclusive and there is no fourth one; an unbounded region may still have an optimal solution.
lp-f-05-02 · level facile · notes
When is an LP problem in maximization form $\max\{f({\boldsymbol x}) : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$, with $f$ linear and feasible region $F$, called unbounded?
  1. If for every $M \in \R$ there exists $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \in F$ with $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) > M$
  2. If the feasible region $F$ is unbounded
  3. If there exists $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \in F$ with $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = +\infty$
  4. If there exists $M \in \R$ with $f({\boldsymbol x}) > M$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$
Explanation. Unbounded means that the values of the feasible solutions exceed any threshold; no solution has an infinite value.
lp-f-05-03 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the optimal solutions of an LP problem is correct?
  1. An LP problem admitting an optimal solution admits either exactly one or infinitely many optimal solutions
  2. If an LP problem has infinitely many optimal solutions, then it is unbounded
  3. An LP problem can have exactly two optimal solutions, at two adjacent vertices
  4. If the feasible region is bounded, the optimal solution is unique
Explanation. If $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)}$ and $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}^{(2)}$ are optimal, so is every $\lambda \tilde{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)} + (1-\lambda)\tilde{\boldsymbol x}^{(2)}$ with $\lambda \in [0,1]$.
lp-f-05-04 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ and $\hat{\boldsymbol x}$ be two optimal solutions of an LP problem with feasible region $F$. To prove that $\lambda\tilde{\boldsymbol x} + (1-\lambda)\hat{\boldsymbol x}$, with $\lambda \in [0,1]$, is optimal, which facts are used?
  1. The feasible region is bounded and the objective function is linear
  2. Each constraint of $F$ is preserved by combinations with weights $\lambda, 1-\lambda \ge 0$, and the objective function is linear
  3. Weak Duality and the Fundamental Theorem of Linear Programming
  4. Only the linearity of the objective function: the feasibility of the combination is automatic
Explanation. Feasibility follows from ${\boldsymbol a}{\boldsymbol x}^\lambda = \lambda {\boldsymbol a}\tilde{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol a}\tilde{\boldsymbol x}^{(2)} \le b$ (non-negative weights are needed); the value $z(LP)$ from linearity. Boundedness is not needed.
lp-f-05-05 · level facile · notes
In an LP problem, how is a free variable $x_j \gtreqless 0$ (of any sign) replaced to obtain an equivalent problem with non-negative variables only?
  1. $x_j = x_j^+ + x_j^-$ with $x_j^+, x_j^- \ge 0$
  2. The constraint $x_j \ge 0$ is added
  3. $x_j = x_j^+ - x_j^-$ with $x_j^+, x_j^- \ge 0$
  4. $x_j = -x_j^+$ with $x_j^+ \ge 0$
Explanation. Every real number is the difference of two non-negative numbers; $x_j = -x_j^+$ is the substitution for non-positive variables.
lp-f-05-06 · level facile · notes
Which less-than-or-equal-to constraint is equivalent to $3x_1 - x_2 \ge 4$?
  1. $-3x_1 + x_2 \le -4$
  2. $-3x_1 + x_2 \le 4$
  3. $3x_1 - x_2 \le -4$
  4. $3x_1 - x_2 \le 4$
Explanation. Both sides, including the right-hand side, are multiplied by $-1$ and the direction is reversed.
lp-f-05-07 · level facile · notes
How is the constraint $2x_1 + x_2 \ge 5$ written as an equality with a surplus variable $x_3$?
  1. $2x_1 + x_2 - x_3 = 5$, $x_3 \ge 0$
  2. $2x_1 + x_2 + x_3 = 5$, $x_3 \ge 0$
  3. $2x_1 + x_2 - x_3 = 5$, $x_3 \gtreqless 0$
  4. $2x_1 + x_2 \le 5$ and $-2x_1 - x_2 \le -5$
Explanation. The non-negative surplus variable is subtracted; adding it corresponds to a less-than-or-equal-to constraint, and two opposite inequalities give an equality.
lp-f-05-08 · level facile · notes
An LP problem $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$, with optimal value $z(LP_{\min})$, is transformed into the problem $\max\{-{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$, with optimal value $z(LP_{\max})$. How are the two problems related?
  1. Same optimal solutions and $z(LP_{\min}) = -z(LP_{\max})$
  2. Same optimal solutions and $z(LP_{\min}) = z(LP_{\max})$
  3. Opposite optimal solutions and the same optimal value
  4. Opposite optimal solutions and opposite optimal values
Explanation. $\min\{f\} = -\max\{-f\}$ over the same $F$: the optimal points coincide, the values are opposite.
lp-f-05-09 · level facile · notes
The constraint $\frac{1}{2}x_1 + \frac{2}{3}x_2 \le \frac{5}{6}$ is multiplied by the Least Common Denominator. Which equivalent constraint with integer data is obtained?
  1. $3x_1 + 4x_2 \le 5$
  2. $x_1 + x_2 \le 1$
  3. $3x_1 + 4x_2 \le 30$
  4. $6x_1 + 6x_2 \le 5$
Explanation. $lcd = 6$: multiplying both sides by $6 > 0$ gives $3x_1 + 4x_2 \le 5$, with the same set of points.
lp-f-05-10 · level facile · notes
An LP problem in maximization form has a non-empty and unbounded feasible region $F$. What can be concluded about its outcome?
  1. It always has an optimal solution, since $F$ is non-empty
  2. It is unbounded, since $F$ is unbounded
  3. Nothing: it may be unbounded or have an optimal solution
  4. It has no optimal solution, but it is not unbounded
Explanation. Unboundedness concerns the values of the objective function, not the set $F$: for example $\max\{-x : x \ge 0\}$ has an unbounded $F$ and optimal solution $\tilde x = 0$, while $\max\{x : x \ge 0\}$ is unbounded.
lp-f-05-11 · level facile · notes
The two reference forms of LP problems are the canonical maximization form, $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, and the standard maximization form, $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. What do they have in common?
  1. All constraints less-than-or-equal-to and an objective function to maximize
  2. All constraints equalities and all variables non-negative
  3. All right-hand sides non-negative and all variables non-negative
  4. An objective function to maximize and all variables non-negative
Explanation. Both maximize ${\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$; they differ in the constraints ($\le$ or $=$). The sign of the right-hand sides is fixed by neither of them.
lp-f-05-12 · level facile · notes
The LP problem $\max\{2x_1 + 2x_2 + x_3 : 2x_1 + 3x_2 + x_3 = 4,~ x_1, x_2, x_3 \ge 0\}$ has optimal value $z(LP) = 4$. What are its optimal solutions?
  1. All the points of the segment between $(2, 0, 0)$ and $(0, 0, 4)$
  2. Only the two vertices $(2, 0, 0)$ and $(0, 0, 4)$
  3. All the points of the feasible region
  4. Only the vertex $(2, 0, 0)$
Explanation. From the constraint, $2x_1 + 2x_2 + x_3 = 4 - x_2$: the value is $4$ if and only if $x_2 = 0$, i.e., on the whole segment $2x_1 + x_3 = 4$, $x_2 = 0$. If an LP problem has two optimal solutions, it has infinitely many.

Part: The simplex algorithm (42)

6. Simplex algorithm (14)

lp-f-06-01 · level facile · notes
Consider an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times (n+m)}$ and $\rank({\boldsymbol A}) = m$. When does a set $\pazocal{B}$ of column indices of ${\boldsymbol A}$ define a basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$?
  1. If $|\pazocal{B}| = m$ and ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  2. If $|\pazocal{B}| = m$ and the matrix ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ of the columns with index in $\pazocal{B}$ is invertible
  3. If $|\pazocal{B}| = n$ and the chosen columns are linearly independent
  4. If $|\pazocal{B}| = m$, whatever columns are chosen
Explanation. A basis is an invertible $m \times m$ square submatrix; the condition $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ concerns the feasibility of the basic solution, not the definition of basis.
lp-f-06-02 · level facile · notes
Consider an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times (n+m)}$ and $\rank({\boldsymbol A}) = m$. Given a basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$, with basic indices $\pazocal{B}$ and non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$, what is the associated basic solution?
  1. ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ and ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$
  2. ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ and ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$
  3. ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ and ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol b}$
  4. ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ and ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol b}$
Explanation. The non-basic variables are $0$ and the basic ones solve ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol b}$: ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b} = \bar{\boldsymbol b}$, which coincides with ${\boldsymbol b}$ only if ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol I}$.
lp-f-06-03 · level facile · notes
Consider an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times (n+m)}$ and $\rank({\boldsymbol A}) = m$. Given a basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and reduced cost vector $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$, when is the associated basic solution a basic feasible solution?
  1. When ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol I}$
  2. When $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
  3. When ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. When $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. The equality constraints hold by construction; what remains is the non-negativity of the basic variables, $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$.
lp-f-06-04 · level facile · notes
Consider an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times (n+m)}$ and $\rank({\boldsymbol A}) = m$. Given a basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$, with ${\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}}$ the cost vector of the basic variables and ${\boldsymbol A}_p$ column $p$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, what is the reduced cost of the non-basic variable $x_p$?
  1. $\bar c_p = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_p - c_p$
  2. $\bar c_p = c_p - {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_p$
  3. $\bar c_p = c_p - {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_p$
  4. $\bar c_p = c_p - {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_p$
Explanation. $\bar c_p$ is the change of $z$ per unit of $x_p$: the gain $c_p$ minus the loss ${\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_p$ due to the change of the basic variables.
lp-f-06-05 · level facile · notes
An LP problem in canonical maximization form with $n$ variables, $m$ constraints and ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ is brought to standard form with the slack variables $x_{n+1}, \dots, x_{n+m}$. Which basis immediately gives a basic feasible solution?
  1. Any basis formed by $m$ randomly chosen indices
  2. The one of the first $m$ variables, $\pazocal{B} = \{1, \dots, m\}$
  3. None: Phase $0$ is needed even with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. The one of the slack variables, $\pazocal{B} = \{n+1, \dots, n+m\}$
Explanation. With the slack variables in the basis $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, $\bar z = 0$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol c} = {\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{N}}$.
lp-f-06-06 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. Which variable can be chosen as the pivot column?
  1. A non-basic variable with a positive reduced cost
  2. The basic variable with the largest value
  3. A basic variable with a negative value
  4. A non-basic variable with a negative reduced cost
Explanation. Increasing a non-basic variable with $\bar c_p > 0$ increases $z$ by $\bar c_p$ per unit.
lp-f-06-07 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. With $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ the pivot column $x_p$, with $\bar c_p > 0$, is chosen. Which value $t_m$ does $x_p$ take in the new basic feasible solution?
  1. $t_m = \min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : i \in \pazocal{B},~ \bar a_{ip} < 0\}$
  2. $t_m = \min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : i \in \pazocal{B},~ \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$
  3. $t_m = \min\{\bar b_i / |\bar a_{ip}| : i \in \pazocal{B},~ \bar a_{ip} \ne 0\}$
  4. $t_m = \max\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : i \in \pazocal{B},~ \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$
Explanation. From ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - t\,\bar{\boldsymbol A}_p \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$: only the rows with $\bar a_{ip} > 0$ restrict $t$, and the minimum is needed to keep all the variables non-negative.
lp-f-06-08 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. Which condition, for a basis with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, certifies that the LP problem is unbounded?
  1. There exists $p \in \pazocal{N}$ with $\bar c_p > 0$ and $\bar a_{ip} \ge 0$ for every $i \in \pazocal{B}$
  2. There exists $r \in \pazocal{B}$ with $\bar b_r < 0$ and $\bar a_{rj} \ge 0$ for every $j \in \pazocal{N}$
  3. There exists $p \in \pazocal{N}$ with $\bar c_p < 0$ and $\bar a_{ip} \le 0$ for every $i \in \pazocal{B}$
  4. There exists $p \in \pazocal{N}$ with $\bar c_p > 0$ and $\bar a_{ip} \le 0$ for every $i \in \pazocal{B}$
Explanation. With $\bar a_{ip} \le 0$ no basic variable decreases when $x_p$ grows, and $z = \bar z + t\,\bar c_p$ grows without bound. The last condition is the certificate of infeasibility of the dual simplex algorithm.
lp-f-06-09 · level ipotesi · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. Let $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, $\bar c_p > 0$ and $\bar a_{ip} \le 0$ for every $i \in \pazocal{B}$. To prove that the point $x_p = t$, $x_i = \bar b_i - t\,\bar a_{ip}$ ($i \in \pazocal{B}$), other variables at $0$, is feasible for every $t \ge 0$, which facts are needed?
  1. $\bar b_i \ge 0$ and $\bar a_{ip} \le 0$ give $x_i \ge 0$; the equalities hold by construction
  2. Only the invertibility of ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$, which makes the point a solution of the system
  3. Only $\bar c_p > 0$, which makes $z$ grow as $t$ grows
  4. $\bar b_i > 0$ (non-degeneracy) and $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$ give $x_i \ge 0$ for every $t$
Explanation. Non-negativity follows from $\bar b_i \ge 0$ and $-t\,\bar a_{ip} \ge 0$. The condition $\bar c_p > 0$ is used instead to show that the value $\bar z + t\,\bar c_p$ grows without bound.
lp-f-06-10 · level facile · notes
Consider an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times (n+m)}$ and $\rank({\boldsymbol A}) = m$. How many equations form the simplex dictionary associated with a basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$?
  1. $n + m$: one for each variable
  2. $m$: one for each basic variable
  3. $n + 1$: one for each non-basic variable and one for the objective function
  4. $m + 1$: one for each basic variable and one for the objective function
Explanation. The dictionary writes the $m$ basic variables and $z$ as functions of the $n$ non-basic variables.
lp-f-06-11 · level facile · notes
An LP problem in canonical maximization form with constraints ${\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b}$, ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times n}$, is brought to standard form by adding the slack variables $x_{n+1}, \dots, x_{n+m}$, with constraint matrix $({\boldsymbol A}~~{\boldsymbol I})$. Why does this matrix have rank $m$?
  1. Because it contains the identity matrix of size $m$, made of the columns of the slack variables
  2. Because ${\boldsymbol A}$ has rank $m$ by assumption
  3. Because ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, and then the system has a solution
  4. Because it has more columns ($n + m$) than rows ($m$)
Explanation. The $m$ columns of the slack variables form ${\boldsymbol I}$, which has rank $m$, with no assumption on ${\boldsymbol A}$ or ${\boldsymbol b}$. Having more columns than rows is not enough.
lp-f-06-12 · level facile · notes
The LP problem $\max\{5x_1 + 2x_2 : -x_1 + 3x_2 \le 12,~ x_1 + 2x_2 \le 12,~ 3x_1 - 2x_2 \le 8,~ x_1, x_2 \ge 0\}$ has optimal solution $(5, \frac{7}{2})$. In the standard form with the slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$ of the three constraints, what are $(x_3, x_4, x_5)$ at this solution?
  1. $(0, 0, 0)$
  2. $(-\frac{13}{2}, 0, 0)$
  3. $(\frac{11}{2}, 0, 0)$
  4. $(\frac{13}{2}, 0, 0)$
Explanation. $x_{2+i} = b_i - \sum_j a_{ij} x_j$: $x_3 = 12 - (-5 + \frac{21}{2}) = \frac{13}{2}$, $x_4 = 12 - 12 = 0$, $x_5 = 8 - 8 = 0$. The value $\frac{11}{2}$ is the left-hand side $-x_1 + 3x_2$, not the slack.
lp-f-06-13 · level facile · notes
Consider an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. Which outcomes can the simplex algorithm return, with Phase $0$ in the initialization step?
  1. An optimal solution or a certificate of unboundedness; infeasibility is not detected
  2. A feasible solution, which is optimal only if it is non-degenerate
  3. An optimal solution, or a certificate of infeasibility or of unboundedness
  4. Always an optimal solution, possibly after Phase $0$
Explanation. Phase $0$ detects infeasibility; the pivoting step detects unboundedness ($\bar c_p > 0$ and $\bar a_{ip} \le 0$ for every $i$); otherwise the algorithm stops with $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$, i.e., with an optimal solution.
lp-f-06-14 · level facile · notes
Consider an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. In the pivoting step of the simplex algorithm one chooses the pivot column $x_p$ (non-basic, $p \in \pazocal{N}$) and the pivot row $x_r$ (basic, $r \in \pazocal{B}$). How are the sets $\pazocal{B}$ (basic indices) and $\pazocal{N}$ (non-basic indices) updated?
  1. $\pazocal{B} \leftarrow (\pazocal{B} \setminus \{r\}) \cup \{p\}$ and $\pazocal{N} \leftarrow (\pazocal{N} \setminus \{p\}) \cup \{r\}$
  2. $\pazocal{B} \leftarrow \pazocal{B} \cup \{p\}$ and $\pazocal{N} \leftarrow \pazocal{N} \setminus \{p\}$
  3. $\pazocal{B}$ and $\pazocal{N}$ do not change: only the values $\bar b_i$ are updated
  4. $\pazocal{B} \leftarrow (\pazocal{B} \setminus \{p\}) \cup \{r\}$ and $\pazocal{N} \leftarrow (\pazocal{N} \setminus \{r\}) \cup \{p\}$
Explanation. The pivot column $x_p$ enters the basis and the pivot row $x_r$ leaves it: $|\pazocal{B}|$ stays equal to $m$ and the basis changes in one column only.

7. Optimality conditions and pivot rules (12)

lp-f-07-01 · level teorema · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. Which statement on the optimality conditions of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ is correct?
  1. If $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, the basic feasible solution is optimal
  2. If $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$, the basic solution is optimal even if some $\bar b_i < 0$
  3. If $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$, the basic feasible solution is optimal
  4. If the basic feasible solution is optimal, then $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. This is the sufficient condition for optimality; the converse holds only for non-degenerate solutions, and without $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ the solution is not even feasible.
lp-f-07-02 · level teorema · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. Which statement on the uniqueness of the optimal solution is correct?
  1. If $\bar{\boldsymbol b} > {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$, the basic feasible solution is the unique optimal one
  2. If $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$, the basic feasible solution is the unique optimal one
  3. If the basic feasible solution is the unique optimal one, then $\bar{\boldsymbol c} < {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. If $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol c} < {\boldsymbol 0}$, the basic feasible solution is the unique optimal solution
Explanation. With $\bar{\boldsymbol c} < {\boldsymbol 0}$ every other feasible solution has ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}} \ne {\boldsymbol 0}$ and a strictly smaller value. A zero reduced cost may give other optimal solutions; the converse does not hold.
lp-f-07-03 · level teorema · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. A basic feasible solution is degenerate if $\bar b_i = 0$ for some $i \in \pazocal{B}$. Which statement on the necessary optimality conditions is correct?
  1. If the basic feasible solution is optimal, then $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$, even if it is degenerate
  2. If the basic feasible solution is degenerate, then it is not optimal
  3. If the basic feasible solution is optimal and non-degenerate, then $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. If $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$, then the basic feasible solution is non-degenerate
Explanation. Non-degeneracy is essential: a degenerate solution may be optimal with some $\bar c_j > 0$.
lp-f-07-04 · level ipotesi · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. To prove that $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$ imply the optimality of the basic solution, which facts are used?
  1. The basic solution is feasible and every feasible ${\boldsymbol x}$ has ${\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}} \le \bar z$, since ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  2. Non-degeneracy $\bar{\boldsymbol b} > {\boldsymbol 0}$ and Weak Duality applied to the primal-dual pair
  3. Bland's rule and the finite number of bases, which stop the simplex algorithm when $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. Only $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$: increasing a non-basic variable $z$ does not grow, feasibility is not needed
Explanation. For every feasible solution $\bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}} \le 0$, hence ${\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}\bar{\boldsymbol b}$, the value of the basic solution, which is feasible since $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$.
lp-f-07-05 · level ipotesi · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. It is proved by contradiction that an optimal and non-degenerate basic feasible solution ($\bar{\boldsymbol b} > {\boldsymbol 0}$) has $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$, assuming $\bar c_p > 0$. What is non-degeneracy needed for?
  1. It guarantees that Bland's rule does not cycle, hence that the simplex algorithm terminates
  2. There is $\vartheta > 0$ with $\bar b_i - \vartheta\,\bar a_{ip} \ge 0$ for every $i$: with $x_p = \vartheta$ we get a better solution
  3. It guarantees $\bar a_{ip} \le 0$ for every $i \in \pazocal{B}$, hence the problem would be unbounded
  4. It guarantees that ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ is invertible, hence that the dictionary exists
Explanation. If some $\bar b_i = 0$ with $\bar a_{ip} > 0$ we would get $\vartheta = 0$ and no improvement: this is the degenerate case.
lp-f-07-06 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. When is a basic feasible solution ($\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$) called degenerate?
  1. When $\bar a_{ij} = 0$ for some $i \in \pazocal{B}$, $j \in \pazocal{N}$
  2. When $\bar c_j = 0$ for some $j \in \pazocal{N}$
  3. When $\bar b_i < 0$ for some $i \in \pazocal{B}$
  4. When $\bar b_i = 0$ for some $i \in \pazocal{B}$
Explanation. In a degenerate solution some basic variable is $0$ too, besides all the non-basic variables, which are always $0$.
lp-f-07-07 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. In the pivoting operation with column $x_p$ the step is $t_m = \min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : i \in \pazocal{B},~ \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$. What happens in a degenerate pivoting operation?
  1. $t_m = 0$: the solution stays the same but the value of $z$ decreases
  2. $t_m = +\infty$: the LP problem is unbounded and the algorithm stops
  3. $t_m > 0$, but the new basis matrix is not invertible
  4. $t_m = 0$: the basis changes, the solution and the value of $z$ stay the same
Explanation. The pivot row has $\bar b_r = 0$, so $x_p$ enters the basis with value $0$; the value of $z$ increases only in non-degenerate operations.
lp-f-07-08 · level ipotesi · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. The simplex algorithm starts from a feasible basis and performs pivoting operations. To prove that, if the algorithm does not terminate, then it cycles (it repeats a dictionary), which fact is used?
  1. The bases are at most $\binom{n+m}{m}$, hence an infinite sequence repeats a dictionary
  2. Every pivoting operation strictly increases $z$, hence no dictionary is repeated
  3. Strong Duality, which bounds the value of $z$ by that of the dual
  4. Bland's rule, which prevents repeating a dictionary already visited
Explanation. It is a finiteness argument; Bland's rule is used for the next theorem, and degenerate operations do not increase $z$.
lp-f-07-09 · level teorema · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. Bland's rule chooses as pivot column the variable with the smallest index and $\bar c_j > 0$ and, in case of ties in the ratio test, the row with the smallest index. Which statement on the termination of the simplex algorithm is correct?
  1. The algorithm terminates if the column with the largest reduced cost is always chosen
  2. The algorithm terminates with any pivot rule
  3. With Bland's rule every pivoting operation is non-degenerate
  4. The algorithm terminates if in every pivoting operation the pivot column and row are chosen with Bland's rule
Explanation. Bland's rule (smallest index) prevents cycling; it does not remove degenerate operations, which may still occur.
lp-f-07-10 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. The ratio test for the pivot column $x_p$ computes $t_m = \min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : i \in \pazocal{B},~ \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$. With Bland's rule, which pivot row is chosen if several rows attain $t_m$?
  1. At random: Bland's rule concerns only the column
  2. The one with the largest coefficient $\bar a_{rp}$ is chosen
  3. The one with the largest value $\bar b_r$ is chosen
  4. The one with the smallest index is chosen
Explanation. Bland's rule uses the smallest index both for the pivot column (among $\bar c_p > 0$) and for the pivot row (among the rows attaining $t_m$).
lp-f-07-11 · level facile · notes
Consider an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. In the simplex algorithm the pivot column is always the non-basic variable with the largest reduced cost $\bar c_j$ (largest reduced cost rule). What can happen when degenerate basic solutions are met?
  1. It visits the same basis twice only if the problem is unbounded
  2. The algorithm can cycle, repeating the same sequence of bases forever
  3. It always terminates, since the value of $z$ strictly increases at every pivot
  4. It stops with a certificate of unboundedness even if the problem has an optimum
Explanation. With degenerate pivots ($t_m = 0$) the value of $z$ does not increase and the sequence of bases may repeat; anti-cycling rules, such as Bland's rule, are there precisely to avoid it.
lp-f-07-12 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form (variables $x_1, \dots, x_4 \ge 0$) the dictionary with $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 2\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{3, 4\}$ is: $x_1 = 2 - x_3 + x_4$, $x_2 = 3 - 2x_3 - x_4$, $z = 10 - x_3 - 2x_4$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). What is concluded?
  1. It is not optimal: it pays to increase $x_4$, which has a positive coefficient in the row of $x_1$
  2. The solution $(2, 3, 0, 0)$ is optimal, but not the only one
  3. The solution $(2, 3, 0, 0)$ is the unique optimal solution, with $z(LP) = 10$
  4. The problem is unbounded, since the column of $x_4$ has a coefficient $\bar a_{14} < 0$
Explanation. $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = (2, 3) \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and the reduced costs $-1, -2$ are negative: by the sufficient conditions for optimality the solution is optimal, and strictly negative reduced costs make it unique. What matters are the reduced costs in the row of $z$, not the coefficients of the rows.

8. Phase 0 of the simplex algorithm (16)

lp-f-08-01 · level facile · notes
For an LP problem in canonical maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, brought to standard form with the slack variables, when is Phase $0$ of the simplex algorithm needed?
  1. When $b_i < 0$ for some $i$
  2. When $c_j < 0$ for some $j$
  3. When the problem has more constraints than variables
  4. Always, before every execution of the simplex algorithm
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ the basis of the slack variables is feasible; with some $b_i < 0$ it is not, and Phase $0$ is needed.
lp-f-08-02 · level facile · notes
For an LP problem in canonical maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with some $b_i < 0$, how is the simplex auxiliary problem, with auxiliary variable $x_0$, built?
  1. $x_0 \ge 0$ is added, subtracted from the left-hand side of every constraint, and $-x_0$ is maximized
  2. $x_0 \ge 0$ is added only to the constraints with $b_i < 0$ and $x_0$ is maximized
  3. The constraints with $b_i < 0$ are multiplied by $-1$ and the original objective function is kept
  4. $x_0 \ge 0$ is added to every constraint and $-x_0$ is minimized
Explanation. Constraints $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j - x_0 = b_i$ for every $i$ and objective function $\max -x_0$: the optimal value is $0$ if and only if the original problem is feasible.
lp-f-08-03 · level facile · notes
For an LP problem in canonical maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the simplex auxiliary problem is $\max\{-x_0 : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} - x_0{\boldsymbol 1} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$, with auxiliary variable $x_0$. Which statement on the auxiliary problem is correct?
  1. It admits an optimal solution only if ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  2. It is never infeasible nor unbounded, so it always admits an optimal solution
  3. It is infeasible when the original problem is infeasible
  4. It may be unbounded when the original problem is unbounded
Explanation. With $x_0 = \max\{0, -\min_i b_i\}$ and the other original variables at $0$ we have a feasible solution, and the value $-x_0 \le 0$ is bounded.
lp-f-08-04 · level teorema · notes
For an LP problem in canonical maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the simplex auxiliary problem is $\max\{-x_0 : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} - x_0{\boldsymbol 1} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$, with auxiliary variable $x_0$. Which statement on the feasibility of the original problem is correct?
  1. It admits a feasible solution if and only if the auxiliary problem is feasible
  2. It admits a feasible solution if and only if the optimal value of the auxiliary problem is $0$
  3. It admits a feasible solution if and only if the special pivoting operation gives $x_0 = 0$
  4. It admits a feasible solution if and only if ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. The auxiliary problem is always feasible; what matters is its optimal value. With ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ the problem is feasible, but the converse does not hold.
lp-f-08-05 · level ipotesi · notes
For an LP problem in canonical maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the simplex auxiliary problem is $\max\{-x_0 : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} - x_0{\boldsymbol 1} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$, with auxiliary variable $x_0$. Which facts are used to prove that the original problem is feasible if and only if the auxiliary problem has optimal value $0$?
  1. ($\Rightarrow$) the special pivoting operation; ($\Leftarrow$) the fact that ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ in the auxiliary problem
  2. ($\Rightarrow$) every value is $-x_0 \le 0$ and $0$ is attained with $x_0 = 0$; ($\Leftarrow$) $\tilde x_0 = 0$, and $x_0$ is removed
  3. ($\Rightarrow$) and ($\Leftarrow$): Strong Duality between the original problem and the auxiliary problem
  4. ($\Rightarrow$) Bland's rule; ($\Leftarrow$) the non-degeneracy of the optimal solution of the auxiliary problem
Explanation. The proof uses only the definition of the auxiliary problem and the sign of its objective function.
lp-f-08-06 · level facile · notes
For an LP problem in canonical maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the simplex auxiliary problem is $\max\{-x_0 : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} - x_0{\boldsymbol 1} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$, with auxiliary variable $x_0$. The initial dictionary of the auxiliary problem, with the basis of the slack variables, is not feasible. In the special pivoting operation of Phase $0$, which variable enters and which leaves the basis?
  1. The variable with a positive reduced cost and smallest index enters, $x_0$ leaves
  2. $x_0$ enters, the basic variable with the most negative right-hand side $b_i$ leaves
  3. $x_0$ enters, the variable chosen by the usual ratio test leaves
  4. $x_0$ enters, the basic variable with the smallest index and $b_i < 0$ leaves
Explanation. With $r \in \argmin\{b_i\}$ we get $x_0 = -b_r$ and $x_i = b_i - b_r \ge 0$: the solution becomes feasible.
lp-f-08-07 · level facile · notes
For an LP problem in canonical maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the simplex auxiliary problem is $\max\{-x_0 : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} - x_0{\boldsymbol 1} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$, with auxiliary variable $x_0$. Phase $0$ ends with optimal value $0$ and with $x_0$ basic, of value $0$. What is done before moving to the original problem?
  1. The simplex algorithm is started on the original problem leaving $x_0$ basic at value $0$
  2. A pivoting operation on the row of $x_0$, with a column $x_j$ of coefficient $\bar a_{0j} \ne 0$
  3. Phase $0$ is repeated with another special pivoting operation on $x_0$
  4. It is concluded that the original problem is infeasible, since $x_0$ is basic
Explanation. The solution is degenerate: the pivot on the row of $x_0$ does not change the values of the other variables and makes $x_0$ non-basic.
lp-f-08-08 · level facile · notes
For an LP problem in canonical maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the simplex auxiliary problem is $\max\{-x_0 : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} - x_0{\boldsymbol 1} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$, with auxiliary variable $x_0$. At the end of Phase $0$, $x_0$ is removed and a feasible dictionary of the original problem is obtained. How is its objective function equation $z = \sum_j c_j x_j$ obtained?
  1. The equation of the auxiliary objective function $-x_0$ is kept
  2. We write $z = \sum_{j \in \pazocal{N}} c_j x_j$ with the original costs
  3. The equations of the basic variables are substituted into $z$
  4. The equation of the auxiliary objective function changes sign
Explanation. The basic variables are written as functions of the non-basic ones: substituting them into $z = \sum_j c_j x_j$ gives $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$. Keeping the original costs of the non-basic variables only ignores the contribution of the basic ones.
lp-f-08-09 · level teorema · notes
Which of the following statements is part of the Fundamental Theorem of Linear Programming, for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$?
  1. If the feasible region is non-empty, then the problem admits an optimal solution
  2. If the problem admits an optimal solution, then every optimal solution is basic
  3. If the problem is unbounded, then it has no basic feasible solution
  4. If the problem admits an optimal solution, then it admits a basic feasible solution which is optimal
Explanation. The theorem states: feasible implies a basic feasible solution; optimal implies a basic optimal one; and the problem is infeasible, unbounded or has an optimal solution. An unbounded problem still has basic feasible solutions.
lp-f-08-10 · level ipotesi · notes
The Fundamental Theorem of Linear Programming states that an LP problem in standard form is either infeasible, or unbounded, or it admits an optimal basic feasible solution. Which results are used to prove it?
  1. Strong Duality and Complementary Slackness, applied to the problem and its dual
  2. Only the sufficient optimality conditions, applied to any feasible basis
  3. The equivalence between vertices and basic feasible solutions, the convexity of $F$ and the theorem on extreme points
  4. Termination of Phase $0$ and simplex (Bland), the feasibility theorem, sufficient optimality conditions and certificate of unboundedness
Explanation. The proof is algorithmic: Phase $0$ and the simplex algorithm terminate, and each possible outcome is one of the three cases of the statement.
lp-f-08-11 · level facile · notes
An LP problem in standard form has $n+m = 5$ variables and $m = 3$ equality constraints. At most how many basic feasible solutions does it have?
  1. $5 \cdot 3 = 15$
  2. $3! = 6$
  3. $2^5 = 32$
  4. $\binom{5}{3} = 10$
Explanation. Each basic feasible solution corresponds to at least one choice of $m$ columns out of $n+m$: at most $\binom{n+m}{m}$.
lp-f-08-12 · level facile · notes
At the end of Phase $0$ of an LP problem with objective function $z = x_1 + 2x_2$ the auxiliary variable $x_0$ is removed and the equations $x_1 = 3 - x_2 + x_3$, $x_4 = 5 - 2x_2 + x_3$, $x_5 = 3 - x_3$ remain, with $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 4, 5\}$ and $\pazocal{N} = \{2, 3\}$. What is the row of the objective function in the initial dictionary of the simplex algorithm?
  1. $z = 2x_2$
  2. $z = x_1 + 2x_2$
  3. $z = 3 + 3x_2 + x_3$
  4. $z = 3 + x_2 + x_3$
Explanation. The equation of the basic variable $x_1$ is substituted in $z$: $z = (3 - x_2 + x_3) + 2x_2 = 3 + x_2 + x_3$, written with the non-basic variables only. Keeping $x_1$ or ignoring it does not give a dictionary.
lp-f-08-13 · level facile · notes
For an LP problem in canonical maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ Phase $0$ solves the simplex auxiliary problem $\max\{-x_0 : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} - x_0{\boldsymbol 1} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$ and finds optimal value $-1$. What is concluded?
  1. The original problem has an optimal solution of value $-1$
  2. The original problem is unbounded
  3. Phase $0$ is repeated with another special pivoting operation
  4. The original problem is infeasible
