Explanation. The original problem is feasible if and only if the auxiliary problem has optimal value $0$ (a solution with $x_0 = 0$). With a negative optimal value every solution has $x_0 > 0$: no ${\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ satisfies ${\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b}$.
lp-f-08-14 · level facile · notes
For the LP problem $\max\{x_1 + x_2 : x_1 + x_2 \le 2,~ -x_1 - x_2 \le -3,~ x_1, x_2 \ge 0\}$ consider the auxiliary problem $\max\{-x_0 : x_1 + x_2 - x_0 \le 2,~ -x_1 - x_2 - x_0 \le -3,~ x_0, x_1, x_2 \ge 0\}$. What is its optimal value?
  1. $-1$
  2. $-3$
  3. $-\frac{1}{2}$
  4. $0$
Explanation. With $s = x_1 + x_2$ the constraints read $s \le 2 + x_0$ and $s \ge 3 - x_0$, compatible if and only if $x_0 \ge \frac{1}{2}$: the optimal value is $-\frac{1}{2} < 0$, hence the original problem is infeasible. The value $-3$ is the one after the special pivot only.
lp-f-08-15 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm with Phase $0$ for $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with some $b_i < 0$, in which order are the steps of Phase $0$ executed?
  1. Special pivot, test of the optimal value, auxiliary problem, simplex on the auxiliary problem, $x_0$ out of the basis, original objective function
  2. Auxiliary problem, simplex on the auxiliary problem, special pivot, $x_0$ out of the basis, test of the optimal value, original objective function
  3. Auxiliary problem, special pivot, simplex on the auxiliary problem, test of the optimal value, $x_0$ out of the basis if needed, original objective function
  4. Original objective function, auxiliary problem, special pivot, simplex on the auxiliary problem, test of the optimal value, $x_0$ out of the basis
Explanation. The special pivot makes the dictionary of the auxiliary problem feasible, and the latter is solved by the simplex algorithm; if the optimal value is negative the algorithm stops (infeasible), otherwise $x_0$ is made non-basic and the original objective function is restored.
lp-f-08-16 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm with Phase $0$ for $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with the simplex auxiliary problem $\max\{-x_0 : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} - x_0{\boldsymbol 1} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$, how many times is the special pivoting operation ($x_0$ enters, the basic variable with the most negative $b_i$ leaves) performed?
  1. Once at the beginning of Phase $0$ and once at the end, to make $x_0$ leave
  2. Once for every constraint with $b_i < 0$
  3. At every iteration of Phase $0$, as long as $x_0$ is basic
  4. At most once, at the beginning of Phase $0$, and only if some $b_i < 0$
Explanation. One special pivot makes the dictionary of the auxiliary problem feasible: afterwards, $x_i = b_i - b_r \ge 0$ for every $i$. The following iterations are ordinary simplex pivots, including the one that makes $x_0$ leave.

Part: Duality and the dual simplex (33)

9. Duality theory (16)

lp-f-09-01 · level facile · notes
Given ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times n}$, ${\boldsymbol b} \in \R^m$ and ${\boldsymbol c} \in \R^n$, what is the dual problem of $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with dual variables ${\boldsymbol \pi}$?
  1. $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$
  2. $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$
  3. $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$
  4. $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$
Explanation. One non-negative dual variable for each primal constraint, one greater-than-or-equal-to dual constraint for each primal variable, dual costs ${\boldsymbol b}$ and the transposed matrix.
lp-f-09-02 · level teorema · notes
Consider a primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with its dual, of variables ${\boldsymbol \pi}$. Which statement of Weak Duality is correct?
  1. For every feasible solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ of the primal and every feasible solution $\bar{\boldsymbol \pi}$ of the dual: $\sum_j c_j \bar x_j \le \sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i$
  2. For every pair of feasible solutions: $\sum_j c_j \bar x_j = \sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i$
  3. For every feasible solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ of the primal and every $\bar{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$: $\sum_j c_j \bar x_j \le \sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i$
  4. For every pair of feasible solutions: $\sum_j c_j \bar x_j \ge \sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i$
Explanation. Every dual feasible solution gives an upper bound on the value of every primal feasible solution; without dual feasibility ($A'\bar{\boldsymbol\pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c}$) the inequality may fail.
lp-f-09-03 · level ipotesi · notes
Consider the primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ and its dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. Let $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol \pi}$ be feasible solutions of the primal and of the dual. Which facts are used to prove that $\sum_j c_j\bar x_j \le \sum_i b_i\bar\pi_i$?
  1. Only $\sum_j a_{ij}\bar x_j \le b_i$ and $\sum_i a_{ij}\bar\pi_i \ge c_j$: non-negativity is not needed
  2. The optimality of $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol \pi}$
  3. Strong Duality and Complementary Slackness
  4. $c_j \le \sum_i a_{ij}\bar\pi_i$ with $\bar x_j \ge 0$, and $\sum_j a_{ij}\bar x_j \le b_i$ with $\bar\pi_i \ge 0$
Explanation. The inequalities are multiplied by non-negative quantities: without $\bar x_j \ge 0$ and $\bar\pi_i \ge 0$ the directions are not preserved. Optimality is not needed.
lp-f-09-04 · level teorema · notes
Consider the primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ and its dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. Which statement of Strong Duality is correct?
  1. The primal has a unique optimal solution if and only if the dual has a unique optimal solution
  2. For every pair of primal and dual feasible solutions the values coincide
  3. If the primal is feasible, then the dual is feasible and the optimal values coincide
  4. The primal has an optimal solution if and only if the dual has an optimal solution, and in this case the optimal values coincide
Explanation. Strong Duality concerns the existence of optimal solutions and the equality of the optimal values, not uniqueness nor merely feasible solutions.
lp-f-09-05 · level ipotesi · notes
Consider the primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ and its dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. The primal problem has $n$ variables and, in standard form, slack variables $x_{n+1}, \dots, x_{n+m}$. Which tools does the proof of Strong Duality use?
  1. An optimal dictionary of the primal, $\tilde\pi_i = -\bar c_{n+i} \ge 0$ (reduced costs of the slack variables) and Weak Duality
  2. Complementary Slackness and the dual simplex algorithm applied to the primal
  3. Only Weak Duality, applied to any pair of feasible solutions
  4. The Corollary on unbounded and infeasible problems and the Fundamental Theorem
Explanation. From the optimal dictionary ($\bar c_j \le 0$ for every $j$) a dual feasible solution with the same value $\bar z$ is built; Weak Duality proves its optimality.
lp-f-09-06 · level facile · notes
The dual of an LP problem in canonical maximization form is written, rewritten in canonical maximization form, and its dual is written again. What is obtained, after bringing it back to canonical maximization form?
  1. A problem in standard maximization form
  2. The starting primal problem
  3. The primal problem with the opposite objective-function vector
  4. The dual problem
Explanation. The dual of the dual is the primal: the dual is rewritten in canonical maximization form by multiplying by $-1$, and its dual is written.
lp-f-09-07 · level teorema · notes
Consider the primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ and its dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. Which statement on the outcomes of the two problems is correct?
  1. If the primal is unbounded, then the dual is unbounded too
  2. If the primal is unbounded, then the dual is infeasible
  3. If the primal is infeasible, then the dual is unbounded
  4. If the dual is infeasible, then the primal is unbounded
Explanation. A dual feasible solution would bound the primal values from above. The other implications fail because primal and dual can both be infeasible.
lp-f-09-08 · level ipotesi · notes
Consider the primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ and its dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. Which result is enough to prove that, if the primal is unbounded, the dual is infeasible?
  1. Weak Duality: a dual feasible ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ would bound the primal values
  2. Strong Duality, which requires equal optimal values in the two problems
  3. The Fundamental Theorem applied to the dual and Complementary Slackness
  4. Phase $0$ applied to the dual, which detects its infeasibility
Explanation. By contradiction, with $\bar{\boldsymbol \pi}$ dual feasible we would have $\sum_j c_j \bar x_j \le M = \sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i$ for every feasible $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$.
lp-f-09-09 · level teorema · notes
Consider the primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ and its dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. Which combination of outcomes is possible?
  1. Primal with an optimal solution and dual unbounded
  2. Both unbounded
  3. Primal infeasible and dual with an optimal solution
  4. Both infeasible
Explanation. The possible cases are four: both with an optimum (same value), primal unbounded and dual infeasible, the converse, both infeasible.
lp-f-09-10 · level ipotesi · notes
Consider the primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ and its dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. Each problem is infeasible, unbounded or has an optimal solution. Which results are used to prove that the pair has exactly four possible combinations of outcomes?
  1. The Fundamental Theorem, Strong Duality and the Corollary on unbounded problems
  2. Only Weak Duality, applied in both directions to the primal-dual pair
  3. Complementary Slackness and the Corollary on unbounded problems
  4. Only Strong Duality, applied to both problems of the pair
Explanation. Out of the nine combinations, Strong Duality excludes the four with only one optimum and the Corollary excludes “both unbounded”.
lp-f-09-11 · level teorema · notes
Consider the primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ and its dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. Let $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ be primal feasible and $\bar{\boldsymbol \pi}$ dual feasible. Which statement of Complementary Slackness is correct?
  1. They are optimal if and only if $\bar\pi_i > 0$ for every active constraint $i$
  2. They are optimal if and only if $\bar\pi_i(\sum_j a_{ij}\bar x_j - b_i) = 0$ for every $i$ and $\bar x_j(c_j - \sum_i a_{ij}\bar\pi_i) = 0$ for every $j$
  3. They are optimal if and only if $\bar x_j > 0$ implies $\bar\pi_j > 0$
  4. They are optimal if and only if, for every $i$, exactly one of $\sum_j a_{ij}\bar x_j = b_i$ and $\bar\pi_i = 0$ holds
Explanation. The “or” of the conditions is inclusive: both may hold. An active constraint may have a zero dual variable.
lp-f-09-12 · level ipotesi · notes
Consider the primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ and its dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. Which results are used in the proof of Complementary Slackness?
  1. Only the Fundamental Theorem of Linear Programming, applied to the primal
  2. The Corollary on unbounded and infeasible problems, applied to the pair
  3. An optimal dictionary of the primal and Bland's rule to avoid cycling
  4. The sums of non-negative terms of Weak Duality, Strong Duality ($\Rightarrow$) and Weak Duality ($\Leftarrow$)
Explanation. Optimality means equal values (Strong Duality), i.e., equality in the chain, i.e., every term zero; conversely equal values imply optimality by Weak Duality.
lp-f-09-13 · level facile · notes
Consider the primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ and its dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ is optimal for the primal and primal constraint $2$ is satisfied at $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ with strict inequality. What can be said about an optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol \pi}$ of the dual?
  1. $\tilde\pi_2 > 0$
  2. $\tilde\pi_2 = 0$
  3. Dual constraint $2$ is active
  4. Nothing: Complementary Slackness concerns only the primal variables
Explanation. A non-active primal constraint has a positive slack, so its dual variable must be zero.
lp-f-09-14 · level facile · notes
The dual (in minimization form) of a generic LP problem in maximization form is written. What corresponds to a primal equality constraint and to a free primal variable?
  1. A free dual variable and a dual equality constraint
  2. A non-negative dual variable and a greater-than-or-equal-to dual constraint
  3. Two non-negative dual variables and two dual constraints
  4. A non-positive dual variable and a less-than-or-equal-to dual constraint
Explanation. Primal equality $\leftrightarrow$ $\pi_i \gtreqless 0$; free primal variable $\leftrightarrow$ dual constraint $=$.
lp-f-09-15 · level facile · notes
The dual (in minimization form) of a generic LP problem in maximization form is written. Which sign does the dual variable $\pi_i$ of a primal greater-than-or-equal-to constraint have?
  1. $\pi_i = 0$
  2. $\pi_i \gtreqless 0$
  3. $\pi_i \le 0$
  4. $\pi_i \ge 0$
Explanation. In a maximization problem the natural constraints are less-than-or-equal-to ($\pi_i \ge 0$); a greater-than-or-equal-to constraint gives $\pi_i \le 0$.
lp-f-09-16 · level facile · notes
Consider the primal problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ and its dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. A primal feasible solution of value $10$ and a dual feasible solution of value $14$ are known. What can be concluded about the optimal value $z(LP)$ of the primal?
  1. $z(LP) \ge 14$
  2. $10 \le z(LP) \le 14$
  3. $z(LP) \le 10$
  4. $z(LP) = 12$
Explanation. The primal solution gives a lower bound; by Weak Duality the dual one gives an upper bound.

10. Dual Simplex algorithm (17)

lp-f-10-01 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. When is the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ called dual feasible?
  1. When ${\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
  2. When $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  3. When $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. When $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. Primal feasible: $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$; dual feasible: $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$. Both together give an optimal basis.
lp-f-10-02 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. What does the dual simplex algorithm keep and towards what does it work?
  1. It keeps $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and goes on until $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
  2. It keeps a non-basic dual solution and solves a restricted primal problem
  3. It keeps $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$ and goes on until $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. It first solves the auxiliary problem and then the original problem
Explanation. It is the opposite of the simplex algorithm, which keeps $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and looks for $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$; the non-basic solution belongs to the primal-dual method.
lp-f-10-03 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. In a dual pivoting operation with Bland's rule, what is chosen first?
  1. The pivot column: the non-basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar c_p > 0$
  2. The pivot row: the basic variable with the largest value
  3. The pivot row: the basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar b_r < 0$
  4. The pivot column: the non-basic variable with the most negative reduced cost
Explanation. In the dual simplex algorithm the order is reversed: first the row (a negative value to fix), then the column with the ratio test on the reduced costs.
lp-f-10-04 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. In the dual simplex algorithm, with $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$, the pivot row $x_r$ with $\bar b_r < 0$ has been chosen. How is the pivot column determined?
  1. With $s_m = \max\{\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj} : j \in \pazocal{N},~ \bar a_{rj} < 0\}$
  2. With $s_m = \min\{\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj} : j \in \pazocal{N},~ \bar a_{rj} > 0\}$
  3. With $t_m = \min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : i \in \pazocal{B},~ \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$
  4. With $s_m = \min\{\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj} : j \in \pazocal{N},~ \bar a_{rj} < 0\}$
Explanation. We need to keep $\bar c_j - s\,\bar a_{rj} \le 0$: only the columns with $\bar a_{rj} < 0$ restrict $s$, and the minimum is taken.
lp-f-10-05 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. How does the value $\bar z$ change during the pivoting operations of the dual simplex algorithm, which keeps $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$?
  1. It stays constant until the basis becomes primal feasible
  2. It never decreases, and it is always a lower bound on the optimal value
  3. It never increases, and it is always an upper bound on the optimal value
  4. It may increase or decrease, depending on the sign of $\bar a_{rp}$
Explanation. With $s_m \ge 0$ and $\bar b_r < 0$ the new value is smaller or equal; a dual feasible basis gives an upper bound.
lp-f-10-06 · level teorema · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. Given a dual feasible basis ($\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$), which statement on the certificate of infeasibility is correct?
  1. If there exists $r \in \pazocal{B}$ with $\bar b_r < 0$ and $\bar a_{rj} \le 0$ for every $j \in \pazocal{N}$, the LP problem is infeasible
  2. If there exists $r \in \pazocal{B}$ with $\bar b_r < 0$, the LP problem is infeasible
  3. If there exists $r \in \pazocal{B}$ with $\bar b_r < 0$ and $\bar a_{rj} \ge 0$ for every $j \in \pazocal{N}$, the LP problem is infeasible
  4. If there exists $p \in \pazocal{N}$ with $\bar c_p > 0$ and $\bar a_{ip} \le 0$ for every $i \in \pazocal{B}$, the LP problem is infeasible
Explanation. From the row $x_r = \bar b_r - \sum_j \bar a_{rj} x_j$ with $\bar a_{rj} \ge 0$ we get $x_r \le \bar b_r < 0$ for every solution. The last condition certifies unboundedness instead.
lp-f-10-07 · level ipotesi · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. Let $r \in \pazocal{B}$ with $\bar b_r < 0$ and $\bar a_{rj} \ge 0$ for every $j \in \pazocal{N}$. Which facts does the proof, on the primal, that the LP problem is infeasible use?
  1. Strong Duality, applied to the dual solution associated with the basis
  2. The row $x_r = \bar b_r - \sum_{j \in \pazocal{N}} \bar a_{rj}x_j$, with $\bar a_{rj} \ge 0$ and ${\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, gives $x_r \le \bar b_r < 0$
  3. Only dual feasibility $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$, which makes the dual feasible
  4. Bland's rule and the finite number of bases, which stop the algorithm
Explanation. The second proof uses instead the unbounded dual and Weak Duality.
lp-f-10-08 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. The dual is $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c}\}$. With ${\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}}$ the costs of the basic variables, when is ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ a dual feasible solution?
  1. Always
  2. If and only if the basis is dual feasible, i.e., $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
  3. Only if ${\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. If and only if the basis is primal feasible, i.e., $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. $\bar c_j \le 0$ is equivalent to ${\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol A}_j \ge c_j$: the reduced costs are the dual slacks.
lp-f-10-09 · level facile · notes
The LP problem $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} \le {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ is brought to standard form with the slack variables. In which case does the basis of the slack variables immediately have all reduced costs non-positive, i.e., is it dual feasible?
  1. When ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  2. When ${\boldsymbol A} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  3. When ${\boldsymbol c} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. When ${\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol I}$ and ${\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ we have $\bar{\boldsymbol c} = {\boldsymbol c}$.
lp-f-10-10 · level teorema · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. A basis is primal feasible if $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and dual feasible if $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$. Which statement is correct?
  1. Every basis associated with an optimal basic feasible solution is also dual feasible
  2. A primal and dual feasible basis is optimal and, if an optimum exists, there is a basis with both properties
  3. A dual feasible basis always gives a basic feasible solution, even with some $\bar b_r < 0$
  4. If the problem has an optimal solution, every dual feasible basis is also primal feasible
Explanation. A degenerate optimal solution may also be associated with a basis with some $\bar c_j > 0$; only the existence of an optimal basis is guaranteed.
lp-f-10-11 · level facile · notes
The dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ of an LP problem in canonical maximization form with $n$ variables and $m$ constraints is brought to standard maximization form by adding $n$ surplus variables, numbered before the dual variables ($\pi_1, \dots, \pi_n$ surplus, $\pi_{n+1}, \dots, \pi_{n+m}$ dual). What is this numbering for?
  1. The right-hand sides of the dual then become all non-negative
  2. $\pi_j$ ($j \le n$) corresponds to the primal variable $x_j$ and $\pi_{n+i}$ to the slack variable $x_{n+i}$
  3. The constraint matrix of the dual then contains the identity in its last $m$ columns
  4. The surplus variables then have a positive cost in the objective function
Explanation. With the indices shifted by $n$, every primal variable and the associated dual variable have the same index: this is the correspondence used by the dual simplex algorithm and by Complementary Slackness.
lp-f-10-12 · level facile · notes
The dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c},~ {\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ of an LP problem in canonical maximization form with $n$ variables and $m$ constraints is brought to standard maximization form by adding $n$ surplus variables, numbered before the dual variables ($\pi_1, \dots, \pi_n$ surplus, $\pi_{n+1}, \dots, \pi_{n+m}$ dual). How many variables and how many equality constraints does the dual have in this standard form?
  1. $2(n + m)$ variables and $n + m$ constraints
  2. $n + m$ variables and $n$ constraints
  3. $n + m$ variables and $m$ constraints
  4. $m$ variables and $n$ constraints
Explanation. The dual has $m$ variables and one greater-than-or-equal-to constraint for every primal variable, i.e., $n$ constraints; with one surplus variable per constraint there are $n + m$ variables and $n$ equalities.
lp-f-10-13 · level facile · notes
For an LP problem in standard maximization form an optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ is known (with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$). The right-hand side ${\boldsymbol b}$ changes into $\hat{\boldsymbol b}$. Why can one re-optimize with the dual simplex algorithm starting from this basis?
  1. The reduced costs change, but they remain non-positive if $\hat{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  2. The dual simplex algorithm does not need a dual feasible initial basis
  3. The basis remains primal feasible, since the matrix ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ does not change
  4. The reduced costs do not depend on ${\boldsymbol b}$: the basis remains dual feasible, even if ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol b}$ has negative entries
Explanation. $\bar{\boldsymbol c}' = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{N}} - {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ does not contain ${\boldsymbol b}$: the basis is still dual feasible, and the dual simplex algorithm restores primal feasibility, often with few pivots.
lp-f-10-14 · level facile · notes
In the dual simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form one starts from a dual feasible basis (reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$) and keeps it so. When does the algorithm stop with an optimal solution?
  1. When $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$: the basis is dual feasible
  2. When the value $\bar z$ stops decreasing between two iterations
  3. When no row has a coefficient $\bar a_{rj} < 0$
  4. When $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$: the basis is also primal feasible
Explanation. $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$ holds from the start; the optimum is reached when $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ as well. A row with $\bar b_r < 0$ and $\bar a_{rj} \ge 0$ for every $j$ certifies infeasibility instead.
lp-f-10-15 · level facile · notes
In the dual simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form, with dictionary $x_i = \bar b_i - \sum_{j} \bar a_{ij} x_j$, $z = \bar z + \sum_j \bar c_j x_j$, a dual pivot is performed with row $x_r$ ($\bar b_r < 0$) and column $x_p$, with $s_m = \bar c_p / \bar a_{rp} \ge 0$. How is $\bar z$ updated?
  1. $\bar z \leftarrow \bar z + s_m\,\bar b_r$
  2. $\bar z \leftarrow \bar z - s_m\,\bar b_r$
  3. $\bar z \leftarrow \bar z + s_m\,\bar a_{rp}$
  4. $\bar z \leftarrow \bar z + \bar c_p\,\bar b_r$
Explanation. After the pivot $x_p = \bar b_r / \bar a_{rp}$ and $\bar z$ changes by $\bar c_p \bar b_r / \bar a_{rp} = s_m \bar b_r \le 0$: in the dual simplex algorithm the value never increases.
lp-f-10-16 · level facile · notes
For an LP problem in canonical maximization form with ${\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$ two executions are compared: the dual simplex algorithm on the primal problem and the simplex algorithm on the dual problem, written in standard maximization form with the dual variables numbered in correspondence with the primal variables. How are the two related?
  1. They visit different bases, but they end with the same optimal value
  2. They coincide only if all the basic solutions visited are non-degenerate
  3. The simplex algorithm on the dual needs Phase $0$, so the two sequences differ
  4. They describe the same sequence of bases: a primal variable is basic if and only if the corresponding dual variable is non-basic
Explanation. The dual simplex algorithm applied to the primal problem is exactly the simplex algorithm applied to the dual: at every iteration the two partitions are complementary according to the index correspondence.
lp-f-10-17 · level facile · notes
The simplex algorithm on the dual (in standard maximization form, with $\pi_j$ associated with the primal variable $x_j$ for every index $j$) is run in parallel with the dual simplex algorithm on the primal. In an iteration the dual variable $\pi_3$ enters the basis. What happens to the primal variable $x_3$?
  1. It enters the primal basis
  2. It stays basic, with an unchanged value
  3. It leaves the primal basis
  4. It becomes the pivot row of the next iteration
Explanation. The two partitions are complementary: $\pi_j$ is basic if and only if $x_j$ is non-basic. When $\pi_3$ enters the dual basis, $x_3$ leaves the primal basis.

Part: Matrix methods, sensitivity and geometry (68)

11. Revised simplex algorithm (12)

lp-f-11-01 · level facile · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with current basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (columns with index in $\pazocal{B}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, the vector ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ (${\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}}$: costs of the basic variables) is obtained without computing ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$. Which system is solved?
  1. ${\boldsymbol \pi}\,{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{N}}$
  2. ${\boldsymbol \pi}\,{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}$
  3. ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}\,{\boldsymbol \pi}' = {\boldsymbol b}$
  4. ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}\,{\boldsymbol \pi}' = {\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}}$
Explanation. From ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ we get ${\boldsymbol \pi}\,{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}$: the row vector ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ is on the left of ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$. The system ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol \pi}' = {\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}}$ uses the non-transposed matrix.
lp-f-11-02 · level facile · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with current basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (columns with index in $\pazocal{B}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, let $x_p$ be the pivot column and $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ the dictionary matrix. What is the vector ${\boldsymbol d}$, solution of ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_p$, used for?
  1. It gives the reduced costs of the non-basic variables: it is used to choose the column
  2. It gives the value $\bar z$ of the new solution: it is used in the stopping criterion
  3. It is the column of $x_p$ in $\bar{\boldsymbol A}$: it is used in the ratio test
  4. It is the row of $\bar{\boldsymbol A}$ of the pivot row: it is used in the ratio test
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_p$ and ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - {\boldsymbol d}\,x_p$; the reduced costs and $\bar z$ come from ${\boldsymbol \pi}$.
lp-f-11-03 · level facile · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with current basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (columns with index in $\pazocal{B}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, the pivot column $x_p$ has reduced cost $\bar c_p > 0$ and ${\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_p$ is computed. What is concluded if ${\boldsymbol d} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$?
  1. The pivoting operation is degenerate
  2. The LP problem is unbounded
  3. The current basic solution is optimal
  4. The LP problem is infeasible
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol d}$ is the column $\bar{\boldsymbol A}_p$: with $\bar c_p > 0$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol A}_p \le {\boldsymbol 0}$ we have the certificate of unboundedness.
lp-f-11-04 · level facile · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with current basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (columns with index in $\pazocal{B}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, the values of the basic variables are $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and, for the pivot column $x_p$, ${\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_p$. How is the step $t_m$ computed?
  1. $t_m = \min\{\bar b_i / d_i : i \in \pazocal{B},~ d_i > 0\}$
  2. $t_m = \min\{\bar c_j / d_j : j \in \pazocal{N},~ d_j < 0\}$
  3. $t_m = \max\{\bar b_i / d_i : i \in \pazocal{B},~ d_i > 0\}$
  4. $t_m = {\boldsymbol \pi}\,{\boldsymbol b}$
Explanation. It is the ratio test of the simplex algorithm with the column $\bar{\boldsymbol A}_p = {\boldsymbol d}$.
lp-f-11-05 · level facile · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with current basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (columns with index in $\pazocal{B}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, what does the $i$-th entry of the row vector ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ correspond to?
  1. To the $i$-th constraint of the problem
  2. To the $i$-th basic variable
  3. To the $i$-th column of ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$
  4. To the $i$-th non-basic variable
Explanation. Even if in the examples the components are labelled with the indices of $\pazocal{B}$, each one is associated with a constraint: it is a dual variable.
lp-f-11-06 · level facile · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with current basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (columns with index in $\pazocal{B}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, what is updated at every iteration, instead of the whole dictionary?
  1. The whole matrix $\bar{\boldsymbol A}$ and the vector $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$
  2. The partition $\pazocal{B}$, $\pazocal{N}$ and the vector $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$
  3. Only the reduced cost vector $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$
  4. The inverse ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$, computed explicitly
Explanation. The revised simplex algorithm computes neither the inverse nor the whole dictionary: it solves the two systems and updates $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$ with $t_m$ and ${\boldsymbol d}$.
lp-f-11-07 · level media · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with current basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (columns with index in $\pazocal{B}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, we have ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 4 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}} = (6, 8)$ and ${\boldsymbol b} = (4, 8)'$. What are ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ and the value $\bar z = {\boldsymbol \pi}\,{\boldsymbol b}$ of the basic solution?
  1. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (3, 2)$, $\bar z = 28$
  2. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (12, 32)$, $\bar z = 304$
  3. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (\frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{2})$, $\bar z = \frac{16}{3}$
  4. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (3, 2)$, $\bar z = 34$
Explanation. From ${\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}$: $2\pi_1 = 6$, $4\pi_2 = 8$. Then $\bar z = {\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol b} = 12 + 16 = 28$ (not ${\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}} = 34$).
lp-f-11-08 · level facile · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ obtained from a canonical form with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, the initial basis is that of the slack variables (${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol I}$, basic costs ${\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol 0}$). What are ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$?
  1. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol b}'$ and $\bar c_j = c_j - b_j$ for every $j \in \pazocal{N}$
  2. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\bar c_j = c_j$ for every $j \in \pazocal{N}$
  3. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\bar c_j = -c_j$ for every $j \in \pazocal{N}$
  4. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol 1}'$ and $\bar c_j = c_j - 1$ for every $j \in \pazocal{N}$
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ the first system gives ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol 0}$, hence $\bar c_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol A}_j = c_j$: the initial reduced costs are the original costs.
lp-f-11-09 · level facile · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form, the pivot column is $x_p$, ${\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_p$ and the ratio test gives the step $t_m$. How are the values $\bar b_i$ of the basic variables updated?
  1. $x_p$ enters with value $t_m$ and $\bar b_i \leftarrow \bar b_i / d_i$ for the others
  2. $x_p$ enters with value $t_m$ and $\bar b_i \leftarrow \bar b_i - t_m\,d_i$ for the others
  3. $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ is recomputed with the explicit inverse
  4. $x_p$ enters with value $t_m$ and $\bar b_i \leftarrow \bar b_i + t_m\,d_i$ for the others
Explanation. With $x_p = t$ the basic variables are $\bar{\boldsymbol b} - t\,{\boldsymbol d}$; for $t = t_m$ the variable of the pivot row becomes zero and leaves the basis.
lp-f-11-10 · level facile · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form, ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ solves ${\boldsymbol \pi}\,{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}$ and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ is column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$. How is the reduced cost of the non-basic variable $x_j$ computed?
  1. $\bar c_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol \pi}\,{\boldsymbol A}_j$
  2. $\bar c_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_j$
  3. $\bar c_j = c_j - \pi_j$
  4. $\bar c_j = {\boldsymbol \pi}\,{\boldsymbol A}_j - c_j$
Explanation. $\bar c_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol \pi}\,{\boldsymbol A}_j$: one scalar product per non-basic column is enough. ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_j$ is a vector, not a scalar.
lp-f-11-11 · level facile · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form, the current basis is ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$ and the pivot column has ${\boldsymbol A}_p = (3, 4)'$. What is the vector ${\boldsymbol d}$ solving ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_p$?
  1. ${\boldsymbol d} = (1, 2)'$
  2. ${\boldsymbol d} = (3, \frac{1}{2})'$
  3. ${\boldsymbol d} = (3, 4)'$
  4. ${\boldsymbol d} = (7, 8)'$
Explanation. From the second equation $2d_2 = 4$, hence $d_2 = 2$; from the first $d_1 + d_2 = 3$, hence $d_1 = 1$. $(3, \frac{1}{2})$ solves the transposed system, $(7, 8)$ is ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_p$.
lp-f-11-12 · level facile · notes
The simplex algorithm with the whole dictionary updates at every iteration the whole matrix $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$. How many columns of $\bar{\boldsymbol A}$ does the revised simplex algorithm compute at every iteration instead?
  1. One for every non-basic variable with a positive reduced cost
  2. All of them, like the simplex algorithm with the dictionary
  3. Only one: the pivot column ${\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_p$
  4. None: ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ and the reduced costs are enough
Explanation. The reduced costs come from ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ ($\bar c_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol A}_j$); the ratio test needs only the column of the entering variable, ${\boldsymbol d}$.

12. Revised dual simplex algorithm (12)

lp-f-12-01 · level facile · notes
In the revised dual simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with a dual feasible basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, which system gives the values $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$ of the basic variables, used to choose the pivot row?
  1. ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}\,\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol b}$
  2. ${\boldsymbol \pi}\,{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}$
  3. ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}\,{\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_p$
  4. ${\boldsymbol u}\,{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol e}'_r$
Explanation. The first system of the revised dual simplex algorithm gives $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$; the second one, ${\boldsymbol u}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol e}'_r$, gives the pivot row.
lp-f-12-02 · level facile · notes
In the revised dual simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with a dual feasible basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, the pivot row $x_r$ has been chosen; ${\boldsymbol e}_r$ is the unit vector of the position of $x_r$ in $\pazocal{B}$. How are the coefficients $\bar a_{rj}$ ($j \in \pazocal{N}$) of the row of $x_r$ in the dictionary obtained?
  1. We compute the whole $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and read its row
  2. We solve ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}\,{\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_r$ and set $\bar a_{rj} = d_j$
  3. We solve ${\boldsymbol u}\,{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol e}'_r$ and set $\bar a_{rj} = {\boldsymbol u}\,{\boldsymbol A}_j$
  4. We set $\bar a_{rj} = a_{rj}$, i.e., we use row $r$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol u} = {\boldsymbol e}'_r{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ selects the row of $\bar{\boldsymbol A}$ associated with $x_r$ without computing the whole matrix.
lp-f-12-03 · level facile · notes
In the revised dual simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with a dual feasible basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, what does the algorithm update at every iteration, instead of recomputing it with a linear system?
  1. The reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$
  2. The vector ${\boldsymbol u}$
  3. The matrix ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$
  4. The values of the basic variables $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$
Explanation. The two revised algorithms are symmetric: the revised simplex algorithm updates $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$, the revised dual one updates $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$.
lp-f-12-04 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, the dictionary of the basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$) is ${\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = \bar{\boldsymbol b} - \bar{\boldsymbol A}\,{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, $z = \bar z + \bar{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{N}}$, with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$, $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = (\bar a_{ij}) = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$. In the dual simplex algorithm a pivoting operation is performed with row $x_r$ (it leaves the basis) and column $x_p$ (it enters). What is the reduced cost of $x_r$ in the new dictionary?
  1. $\bar c_r = \bar c_p - \bar a_{rp}$
  2. $\bar c_r = -\bar c_p / \bar a_{rp}$
  3. $\bar c_r = 0$
  4. $\bar c_r = \bar c_p / \bar a_{rp}$
Explanation. It is the coefficient $-s$ of $x_r$ with $s = s_m = \bar c_p / \bar a_{rp} \ge 0$: the new reduced cost is non-positive, as required.
lp-f-12-05 · level facile · notes
The pseudo-code of the revised dual simplex algorithm starts from the basis of the slack variables, without an initial phase. Which assumption does it make on the input problem?
  1. Canonical maximization form with ${\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$: the basis of the slack variables is dual feasible
  2. Any dual feasible solution, even a non-basic one, is given
  3. Canonical maximization form with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$: the basis of the slack variables is primal feasible
  4. Standard minimization form with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$: it starts from the dual solution ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol I}$ and $\bar c_j = c_j \le 0$ the initial basis is dual feasible; a non-basic dual solution is the input of the primal-dual method.
lp-f-12-06 · level facile · notes
In the revised dual simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with a dual feasible basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, when is it concluded that the problem is infeasible?
  1. When $\bar c_j = 0$ for some non-basic variable $x_j$
  2. When the pivot row $x_r$ has $\bar a_{rj} \le 0$ for every $j \in \pazocal{N}$
  3. When $\bar b_r < 0$ for some $r \in \pazocal{B}$, i.e., at every iteration
  4. When the pivot row $x_r$ has $\bar a_{rj} \ge 0$ for every $j \in \pazocal{N}$
Explanation. The ratio test on the columns with $\bar a_{rj} < 0$ has no candidate: this is the certificate of infeasibility.
lp-f-12-07 · level media · notes
In the revised dual simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with a dual feasible basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$) and ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, the pivot row $x_r$ ($\bar b_r < 0$) has $\bar a_{r1} = -1$, $\bar a_{r2} = -3$, with $\pazocal{N} = \{1, 2\}$, $\bar c_1 = -2$, $\bar c_2 = -3$. The pivot column minimizes $\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj}$ over the $j$ with $\bar a_{rj} < 0$. Which column enters and which reduced costs are obtained?
  1. $x_1$ enters; $\bar c_2 = 3$, $\bar c_r = -2$
  2. $x_2$ enters; $\bar c_1 = -3$, $\bar c_r = -1$
  3. $x_2$ enters; $\bar c_1 = -1$, $\bar c_r = -1$
  4. $x_2$ enters; $\bar c_1 = -1$, $\bar c_r = 1$
Explanation. Ratios $\frac{-2}{-1} = 2$ and $\frac{-3}{-3} = 1$: $x_2$ enters with $s_m = 1$. Then $\bar c_1 = -2 - 1 \cdot (-1) = -1$ and $\bar c_r = -s_m = -1$.
lp-f-12-08 · level facile · notes
In the revised dual simplex algorithm, after a dual pivoting operation with pivot row $x_r$ and pivot column $x_p$ (coefficients $\bar a_{rj}$ of the row of $x_r$ and reduced costs $\bar c_j \le 0$), how are the reduced costs of the other non-basic variables $x_j$, $j \ne p$, updated?
  1. $\bar c_j \leftarrow \bar c_j + \frac{\bar c_p}{\bar a_{rp}}\,\bar a_{rj}$
  2. $\bar c_j \leftarrow \frac{\bar c_j}{\bar a_{rj}}$
  3. $\bar c_j \leftarrow \bar c_j - \frac{\bar b_r}{\bar a_{rp}}\,\bar a_{rj}$
  4. $\bar c_j \leftarrow \bar c_j - \frac{\bar c_p}{\bar a_{rp}}\,\bar a_{rj}$
Explanation. It is the update of the objective function row in a pivoting operation: the pivot row multiplied by $\bar c_p / \bar a_{rp}$, the minimum of the ratio test already computed, is subtracted.
lp-f-12-09 · level facile · notes
In the revised dual simplex algorithm the reduced costs are updated with $\bar c_j \leftarrow \bar c_j - \frac{\bar c_p}{\bar a_{rp}}\,\bar a_{rj}$, where $x_r$ is the pivot row and $x_p$ the pivot column. Why do the new reduced costs remain non-positive?
  1. Because $\bar b_r < 0$ and hence the value $\bar z$ does not increase
  2. Because $\frac{\bar c_p}{\bar a_{rp}} \le \frac{\bar c_j}{\bar a_{rj}}$ for every $j$ with $\bar a_{rj} < 0$
  3. Because $\bar a_{rp} > 0$ and $\bar c_p < 0$ by the choice of the column
  4. Because ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ remains invertible after the pivoting operation
Explanation. The pivot column attains the minimum of the ratio test over the columns with $\bar a_{rj} < 0$: for these the inequality gives the right sign, and for $\bar a_{rj} \ge 0$ a non-negative quantity is subtracted from $\bar c_j \le 0$ (with $\frac{\bar c_p}{\bar a_{rp}} \ge 0$).
lp-f-12-10 · level facile · notes
The revised simplex algorithm and the revised dual simplex algorithm solve at every iteration two systems with the matrix ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ and update a third vector instead of recomputing it. Which one?
  1. ${\boldsymbol d}$ in the revised simplex, ${\boldsymbol u}$ in the revised dual simplex
  2. $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$ in the revised simplex, $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$ in the revised dual simplex
  3. ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ in both algorithms
  4. $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$ in the revised simplex, $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$ in the revised dual simplex
Explanation. The revised simplex algorithm obtains $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$ from ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ and updates $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$ with $t_m$ and ${\boldsymbol d}$; the revised dual simplex obtains $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$ from the first system and updates $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$ with the row $\bar a_{rj}$.
lp-f-12-11 · level facile · notes
The problem $\max\{-2x_1 - 4x_2 : -x_1 - x_2 + x_3 = -2,~ x_1 - x_2 + x_4 = 0,~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ (slack variables $x_3, x_4$) is solved by the revised dual simplex algorithm, starting from the dual feasible basis $\pazocal{B} = \{3, 4\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{1, 2\}$, with ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol I}$ and reduced costs $\bar c_1 = -2$, $\bar c_2 = -4$. Pivot row: the basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar b_r < 0$; pivot column: $\min\{\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj} : \bar a_{rj} < 0\}$. What is the partition after the first iteration?
  1. $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 4\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{2, 3\}$
  2. $\pazocal{B} = \{3, 4\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{1, 2\}$
  3. $\pazocal{B} = \{2, 4\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{1, 3\}$
  4. $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 3\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{2, 4\}$
Explanation. $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol b} = (-2, 0)$: the pivot row is $x_3$. With ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol I}$ its row is $\bar a_{31} = -1$, $\bar a_{32} = -1$; the ratios are $\frac{-2}{-1} = 2$ and $\frac{-4}{-1} = 4$, hence $x_1$ enters and $x_3$ leaves.
lp-f-12-12 · level facile · notes
The problem $\max\{-2x_1 - 4x_2 : -x_1 - x_2 + x_3 = -2,~ x_1 - x_2 + x_4 = 0,~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ is solved by the revised dual simplex algorithm. At the second iteration $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 4\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{2, 3\}$, with reduced costs $\bar c_2 = -2$, $\bar c_3 = -2$. Pivot row: the basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar b_r < 0$ ($\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$); pivot column: $\min\{\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj} : \bar a_{rj} < 0\}$, with $\bar a_{rj} = {\boldsymbol u}{\boldsymbol A}_j$ and ${\boldsymbol u}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol e}'_r$. What are the pivot row and column?
  1. Pivot row $x_1$, pivot column $x_2$
  2. None: the basis is already optimal
  3. Pivot row $x_4$, pivot column $x_3$
  4. Pivot row $x_4$, pivot column $x_2$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 0 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ gives $\bar b_1 = 2$, $\bar b_4 = -2$: pivot row $x_4$. With ${\boldsymbol u} = (1, 1)$ we get $\bar a_{42} = -2$ and $\bar a_{43} = 1$: the only candidate is $x_2$. After the pivot the optimum $(1, 1)$ with $z = -6$ is reached.

13. The primal-dual method (15)

lp-f-13-01 · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, and the dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$ is column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$), let ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ be a dual feasible solution. What is the admissible set $\pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi})$?
  1. The set of the basic indices of an optimal basis
  2. The set of the indices $i$ with $\pi_i > 0$
  3. The set of the indices $j$ with ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} = c_j$
  4. The set of the indices $j$ with ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} < c_j$
Explanation. By Complementary Slackness only the variables $x_j$ with $j \in \pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi})$ may be positive.
lp-f-13-02 · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, and the dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$ is column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$), $\pazocal{J} = \pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi}) = \{j : {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} = c_j\}$ is the admissible set of a dual feasible solution ${\boldsymbol \pi}$. What is the restricted primal problem $RP(\pazocal{J})$, with artificial variables ${\boldsymbol a} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$?
  1. $\min {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}$ subject to ${\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b}$, $x_j = 0$ for $j \in \pazocal{J}$
  2. $\min \sum_i a_i$ subject to ${\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol a} = {\boldsymbol b}$ with all the variables $x_j$
  3. $\min \sum_i a_i$ subject to $\sum_{j \in \pazocal{J}} {\boldsymbol A}_j x_j + {\boldsymbol a} = {\boldsymbol b}$, $x_j \ge 0$, ${\boldsymbol a} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. $\max {\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol y}$ subject to ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} \le 0$ for $j \in \pazocal{J}$, $y_i \le 1$
Explanation. Primal solutions using only the admissible variables are sought, with $m$ artificial variables as in Phase $0$; the third option is its dual.
lp-f-13-03 · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, and the dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$ is column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$), $\pazocal{J} = \pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi}) = \{j : {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} = c_j\}$ is the admissible set of a dual feasible solution ${\boldsymbol \pi}$, and $\xi(\pazocal{J})$ is the optimal value of the restricted primal problem $\min\{\sum_i a_i : \sum_{j \in \pazocal{J}} {\boldsymbol A}_j x_j + {\boldsymbol a} = {\boldsymbol b},~ x_j \ge 0,~ {\boldsymbol a} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. What is concluded if $\xi(\pazocal{J}) = 0$?
  1. The primal problem is infeasible, since the artificial variables are zero
  2. The solution of the restricted primal, with $x_j = 0$ for $j \notin \pazocal{J}$, and ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ are optimal
  3. The dual solution ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ must be improved with a dual step $\theta > 0$
  4. The dual problem is unbounded, hence the primal is infeasible
Explanation. With $\xi = 0$ the artificial variables are zero: we have a primal feasible solution satisfying Complementary Slackness together with ${\boldsymbol \pi}$.
lp-f-13-04 · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, and the dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$ is column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$), $\pazocal{J} = \pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi}) = \{j : {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} = c_j\}$ is the admissible set of a dual feasible solution ${\boldsymbol \pi}$. The restricted primal problem is $RP(\pazocal{J}) = \min\{\sum_i a_i : \sum_{j \in \pazocal{J}} {\boldsymbol A}_j x_j + {\boldsymbol a} = {\boldsymbol b},~ x_j \ge 0,~ {\boldsymbol a} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. What are the constraints of its dual $DRP(\pazocal{J})$, with variables ${\boldsymbol y}$?
  1. ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} \le c_j$ for every $j$ and ${\boldsymbol y}$ free
  2. ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} \ge 0$ for every $j \in \pazocal{J}$ and $y_i \ge 0$ for every $i$
  3. ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} \le 0$ for every $j \in \pazocal{J}$ and $y_i \le 1$ for every $i$
  4. ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} = 0$ for every $j \notin \pazocal{J}$
Explanation. The columns ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ ($j \in \pazocal{J}$) give ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} \le 0$, those of the artificial variables give $y_i \le 1$.
lp-f-13-05 · level facile · notes
The dual simplex algorithm keeps a dual feasible basis. What distinguishes the primal-dual method, for a problem $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, from the dual simplex algorithm?
  1. It works towards dual feasibility starting from a primal basis
  2. It keeps a primal feasible basis and a dual infeasible basis
  3. It keeps a dual feasible solution which need not be basic
  4. It does not use duality: it works on the primal problem only
Explanation. The dual simplex algorithm keeps a dual feasible basis; the primal-dual method can start from any dual feasible solution.
lp-f-13-06 · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, and the dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$ is column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$), the admissible set of a dual feasible solution ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ is $\pazocal{J} = \{j : {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} = c_j\}$. If ${\boldsymbol c} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, which initial dual feasible solution is immediate?
  1. None: Phase $0$ on the dual is always needed
  2. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol b}$, with $\pazocal{J} = \{1, \dots, n\}$
  3. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}$, with $\pazocal{J} = \emptyset$
  4. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol 0}$, with $\pazocal{J} = \{j : c_j = 0\}$
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ the dual constraints become $0 \le c_j$; the active ones are those with $c_j = 0$.
lp-f-13-07 · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, and the dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$ is column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$), $\pazocal{J} = \pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi}) = \{j : {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} = c_j\}$ is the admissible set of a dual feasible solution ${\boldsymbol \pi}$; the restricted primal problem has optimal value $\xi(\pazocal{J}) > 0$, ${\boldsymbol y}$ is the optimal solution of its dual and the dual step is ${\boldsymbol \pi} + \theta{\boldsymbol y}$, $\theta > 0$. What is concluded if ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} \le 0$ for every $j \notin \pazocal{J}$?
  1. The primal is unbounded
  2. $\theta = 0$ and the step is repeated
  3. The current pair is optimal
  4. The dual is unbounded and hence the primal is infeasible
Explanation. $\theta$ can grow without bound keeping dual feasibility, while ${\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ grows by $\theta\,\xi(\pazocal{J}) > 0$.
lp-f-13-08 · level ipotesi · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, and the dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$ is column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$), $\pazocal{J} = \pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi}) = \{j : {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} = c_j\}$ is the admissible set of a dual feasible solution ${\boldsymbol \pi}$; $\xi(\pazocal{J}) > 0$ is the optimal value of the restricted primal $RP(\pazocal{J})$ and ${\boldsymbol y}$ the optimal solution of its dual $DRP(\pazocal{J})$. Why does the step ${\boldsymbol \pi} \leftarrow {\boldsymbol \pi} + \theta{\boldsymbol y}$, with $\theta > 0$, strictly increase ${\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi}$?
  1. Because $y_i \le 1$ for every $i$ and $\theta$ is chosen as the minimum ratio
  2. By Weak Duality between the primal problem and its dual, applied to ${\boldsymbol \pi}$
  3. Because ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and ${\boldsymbol y} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, hence ${\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol y} \ge 0$
  4. By Strong Duality between $RP(\pazocal{J})$ and $DRP(\pazocal{J})$: ${\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol y} = \xi(\pazocal{J}) > 0$
Explanation. The increase is $\theta\,{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol y}$, and ${\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol y}$ is the optimal value of $DRP$, equal to that of $RP$, positive when the method does not stop. ${\boldsymbol y}$ is not necessarily non-negative.
lp-f-13-09 · level teorema · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, and the dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$ is column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$), let ${\boldsymbol x}$ be primal feasible and ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ dual feasible, with admissible set $\pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi}) = \{j : {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} = c_j\}$. Which statement is correct?
  1. They are optimal if and only if $x_j = 0$ for every $j \notin \pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi})$
  2. They are optimal if and only if $x_j > 0$ for every $j \in \pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi})$
  3. They are optimal if and only if $x_j = 0$ for every $j \in \pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi})$
  4. They are optimal if and only if $\pazocal{J}({\boldsymbol \pi}) = \{1, \dots, n\}$
Explanation. It is the form of Complementary Slackness for the primal in standard minimization form: only the admissible variables may be positive, but they need not all be.
lp-f-13-10 · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ is dual feasible with dual slacks $s_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge 0$, $\pazocal{J} = \{j : s_j = 0\}$, and the step is ${\boldsymbol \pi} + \theta{\boldsymbol y}$ with maximum $\theta$. Which index enters $\pazocal{J}$?
  1. The index $j \notin \pazocal{J}$ minimizing $s_j / {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y}$ among those with ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} > 0$
  2. The index $j \notin \pazocal{J}$ maximizing $s_j / {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y}$ among those with ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} > 0$
  3. An index $j \in \pazocal{J}$ with ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} < 0$, whose dual slack becomes positive
  4. The index $j \notin \pazocal{J}$ with the smallest dual slack $s_j$, whatever ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y}$ is
Explanation. The new slack is $s_j - \theta\,{\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y}$: the maximum step $\theta$ is the minimum ratio, and the index attaining it has zero slack, so it enters the admissible set. The indices of $\pazocal{J}$ with ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} < 0$ may instead leave it.
lp-f-13-11 · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$, in which order are the steps of an iteration executed, starting from a dual feasible solution ${\boldsymbol \pi}$?
  1. Dual slacks $s_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ and admissible set $\pazocal{J}$, restricted primal $RP(\pazocal{J})$ and its dual, step ${\boldsymbol \pi} \leftarrow {\boldsymbol \pi} + \theta{\boldsymbol y}$
  2. Step ${\boldsymbol \pi} \leftarrow {\boldsymbol \pi} + \theta{\boldsymbol y}$, dual slacks $s_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ and admissible set $\pazocal{J}$, restricted primal $RP(\pazocal{J})$
  3. Dual slacks $s_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi}$, step ${\boldsymbol \pi} \leftarrow {\boldsymbol \pi} + \theta{\boldsymbol y}$, admissible set $\pazocal{J}$ and restricted primal $RP(\pazocal{J})$
  4. Restricted primal $RP(\pazocal{J})$, step ${\boldsymbol \pi} \leftarrow {\boldsymbol \pi} + \theta{\boldsymbol y}$, dual slacks $s_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ and admissible set $\pazocal{J}$
Explanation. $\pazocal{J}$ depends on ${\boldsymbol \pi}$; $RP(\pazocal{J})$ uses only the admissible variables and gives ${\boldsymbol y}$ (optimal solution of the restricted dual), which the dual step needs.
lp-f-13-12 · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$, the restricted primal problem is $RP(\pazocal{J}) = \min\{\sum_i a_i : \sum_{j \in \pazocal{J}} {\boldsymbol A}_j x_j + {\boldsymbol a} = {\boldsymbol b},~ x_j \ge 0,~ {\boldsymbol a} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with artificial variables ${\boldsymbol a}$. What is the assumption ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ for?
  1. To make the solution with the artificial variables only, ${\boldsymbol a} = {\boldsymbol b}$, feasible for $RP(\pazocal{J})$
  2. To guarantee that the primal problem is feasible
  3. To guarantee that ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ is dual feasible
  4. To obtain $\xi(\pazocal{J}) = 0$ already at the first iteration
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, ${\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ and ${\boldsymbol a} = {\boldsymbol b}$ form a basic feasible solution of $RP(\pazocal{J})$, from which the simplex algorithm starts. The feasibility of ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ depends instead on ${\boldsymbol c} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$.
lp-f-13-13 · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol c} > {\boldsymbol 0}$ one starts from ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol 0}$. The admissible set is $\pazocal{J} = \{j : {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} = c_j\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$: column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$) and the restricted primal is $\min\{\sum_i a_i : \sum_{j \in \pazocal{J}} {\boldsymbol A}_j x_j + {\boldsymbol a} = {\boldsymbol b},~ x_j \ge 0,~ {\boldsymbol a} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$. What is the first restricted primal like?
  1. $\pazocal{J} = \{1, \dots, n\}$: it contains all the variables $x_j$
  2. $\pazocal{J} = \emptyset$: it has only the artificial variables and optimal value $\xi(\pazocal{J}) = \sum_i b_i$
  3. $\pazocal{J} = \emptyset$: it has optimal value $\xi(\pazocal{J}) = 0$
  4. $\pazocal{J} = \{j : b_j > 0\}$: it contains the variables of the constraints with positive right-hand side
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ we have $s_j = c_j > 0$ for every $j$, hence $\pazocal{J} = \emptyset$; the constraints become ${\boldsymbol a} = {\boldsymbol b}$ and $\xi(\pazocal{J}) = \sum_i b_i$, positive if ${\boldsymbol b} \ne {\boldsymbol 0}$.
lp-f-13-14 · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol b} = (4, 2)'$, the current dual solution is ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (1, 0)'$, the optimal solution of the dual of the restricted primal is ${\boldsymbol y} = (1, -1)'$ and the step is $\theta = 2$. What are the new dual solution ${\boldsymbol \pi} + \theta{\boldsymbol y}$ and the change of ${\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi}$?
  1. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (-1, 2)'$; ${\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ decreases by $4$
  2. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (3, -2)'$; ${\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ increases by $4$
  3. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (2, -1)'$; ${\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ increases by $2$
  4. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (3, -2)'$; ${\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ increases by $8$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol \pi} + 2{\boldsymbol y} = (1 + 2, 0 - 2) = (3, -2)$. The change is $\theta\,{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol y} = 2 \cdot (4 - 2) = 4 > 0$: the dual value strictly increases, as it must.
lp-f-13-15 · level facile · notes
The (primal) simplex algorithm, the dual simplex algorithm and the primal-dual method for an LP problem are compared. What does the (primal) simplex algorithm keep and what does it work towards?
  1. It keeps a primal feasible basis ($\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$) and works towards dual feasibility ($\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$)
