  2. It keeps a dual feasible solution, not basic, and works towards primal feasibility
  3. It keeps a primal and dual feasible basis and works towards a larger optimal value
  4. It keeps a dual feasible basis ($\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$) and works towards primal feasibility ($\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$)
Explanation. The simplex algorithm preserves $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and stops when $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$; the dual simplex algorithm does the opposite and the primal-dual method keeps a dual feasible solution which need not be basic.

14. Sensitivity analysis (15)

lp-f-14-01 · level facile · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form, given a basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$, which two conditions define an optimal basis?
  1. ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ invertible and $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  2. $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
  3. $\bar{\boldsymbol b} > {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol c} < {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and ${\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. The two conditions are sufficient for optimality; a change of the data preserves the optimality of the basis as long as both hold.
lp-f-14-02 · level facile · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$), optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $z(LP)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$, which of the two optimality conditions of the basis can a change of the right-hand side vector ${\boldsymbol b}$ violate?
  1. Only $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  2. None: the basis always remains optimal
  3. Only $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. Both conditions
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol b}$ appears only in $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$; ${\boldsymbol c}$ appears only in the reduced costs.
lp-f-14-03 · level facile · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$), optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $z(LP)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$, $c_k$ is replaced by $c_k + \delta$, with $k \in \pazocal{N}$. For which $\delta$ does the basis remain optimal?
  1. $\delta \le -\bar c_k$
  2. $\delta \le \bar c_k$
  3. $\delta \ge -\bar c_k$
  4. For every $\delta$, since $x_k$ is $0$
Explanation. Only the reduced cost of $x_k$ changes; it becomes $\bar c_k + \delta$ and must remain non-positive.
lp-f-14-04 · level facile · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$), optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $z(LP)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$, $c_k$ is replaced by $c_k + \delta$, with $k \in \pazocal{B}$, and $\delta$ is such that the basis remains optimal. What changes?
  1. Same optimal solution, optimal value $z(LP) + \delta\,\tilde x_k$
  2. Different optimal solution, same optimal value $z(LP)$
  3. Same optimal solution, optimal value $z(LP) + \delta$
  4. Both the optimal solution and the optimal value unchanged
Explanation. $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$ does not depend on ${\boldsymbol c}$, so the solution does not change; its value changes by $\delta\,\tilde x_k$.
lp-f-14-05 · level facile · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$), optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $z(LP)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$, what are the shadow prices of the optimal basis (${\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}}$: costs of the basic variables)?
  1. The reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$ of the non-basic variables, with the opposite sign
  2. The entries of ${\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$: increase of $z(LP)$ per unit increase of $b_k$
  3. The values $\bar{\boldsymbol b}$ of the basic variables, i.e., the optimal quantities
  4. The entries of ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}}$: increase of $z(LP)$ per unit increase of $c_k$
Explanation. As long as the basis remains optimal, $z(LP) \to z(LP) + \delta\,\pi_k$: $\pi_k$ is the marginal value of constraint $k$.
lp-f-14-06 · level facile · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$), optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $z(LP)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$, constraint $k$ is not active at $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$: its slack variable is basic with a positive value. What is the shadow price $\pi_k$ of the constraint?
  1. $\pi_k = 0$
  2. $\pi_k$ equals the value of the slack variable
  3. $\pi_k < 0$
  4. $\pi_k > 0$
Explanation. A small change of $b_k$ does not change the optimal value; this agrees with Complementary Slackness.
lp-f-14-07 · level facile · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$), optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $z(LP)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$, a new variable $x_\star \ge 0$ with cost $c_\star$ and column ${\boldsymbol A}_\star$ is added. With ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$, when does $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ (with $x_\star = 0$) remain optimal?
  1. When $c_\star \le 0$
  2. When ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_\star \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
  3. When $\bar c_\star = c_\star - {\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol A}_\star \le 0$
  4. Never: the dual simplex algorithm must always be restarted
Explanation. The basis remains primal feasible; if $\bar c_\star > 0$ the (primal) simplex algorithm is restarted with $x_\star$ as first pivot column.
lp-f-14-08 · level facile · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$), optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $z(LP)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$, a new less-than-or-equal-to constraint violated by $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ is added, with a new slack variable. How is the problem re-optimized?
  1. With the dual simplex algorithm from the basis extended with the new slack variable, which is the first pivot row
  2. It is not needed: the extended basis remains optimal
  3. With the simplex algorithm from the extended basis, with the new slack variable as first pivot column
  4. With Phase $0$ and then the simplex algorithm, from scratch
Explanation. The extended basis remains dual feasible (the reduced costs do not change) but the new slack variable is negative.
lp-f-14-09 · level teorema · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$), optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $z(LP)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$, the dual is $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c}\}$. Which statement on the shadow prices ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ is correct?
  1. ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ is an optimal solution of the dual problem and $z(LP) = \sum_i \pi_i b_i$
  2. $z(LP) = \sum_i \pi_i c_i$
  3. ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ is a feasible solution of the primal with value $z(LP)$
  4. ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ is optimal for the dual only if the primal solution is degenerate
Explanation. The optimal basis is dual feasible, so ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ is dual feasible, and $\bar z = {\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol b}$: by duality ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ is optimal.
lp-f-14-10 · level ipotesi · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (basic indices $\pazocal{B}$, non-basic indices $\pazocal{N}$), optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $z(LP)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c}$, to show that the shadow prices ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ are an optimal solution of the dual $\min\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c}\}$, which facts are enough?
  1. Only primal feasibility $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and the formula $\bar z = {\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol b}$
  2. Non-degeneracy $\bar{\boldsymbol b} > {\boldsymbol 0}$ of the optimal solution and Bland's rule
  3. Complementary Slackness only, without the dual feasibility of ${\boldsymbol \pi}$
  4. $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$ gives ${\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol A}_j \ge c_j$ for every column $j$, $\bar z = {\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol b}$ and Weak Duality
Explanation. A dual feasible solution whose value equals that of a primal feasible solution is optimal by Weak Duality.
lp-f-14-11 · level media · notes
An LP problem in canonical maximization form with two constraints has optimal value $z(LP) = 28$ and shadow prices ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (3, 2)$ of the optimal basis. If $b_1$ increases by $2$ and the basis remains optimal, what is the new optimal value?
  1. $30$
  2. $56$
  3. $32$
  4. $34$
Explanation. $z(LP) + \delta\,\pi_1 = 28 + 2 \cdot 3 = 34$; using $\pi_2$ would give $32$.
lp-f-14-12 · level facile · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$), the shadow prices are ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1} = (0, 2, 1)$. A variable $x_\star \ge 0$ is added with cost $c_\star = 4$ and column ${\boldsymbol A}_\star = (1, 1, 1)'$. What is concluded?
  1. $\bar c_\star = 1 > 0$: the basis remains optimal, with $x_\star = 0$
  2. $\bar c_\star = 7 > 0$: the dual simplex algorithm restarts with $x_\star$ as pivot row
  3. $\bar c_\star = -1 \le 0$: the basis remains optimal, with $x_\star = 0$
  4. $\bar c_\star = 1 > 0$: the basis is no longer optimal and the simplex algorithm restarts with $x_\star$ as pivot column
Explanation. $\bar c_\star = c_\star - {\boldsymbol \pi}{\boldsymbol A}_\star = 4 - 3 = 1 > 0$: the basis stays primal feasible but is no longer dual feasible, hence the simplex algorithm resumes with $x_\star$ entering.
lp-f-14-13 · level facile · notes
The LP problem $\max\{5x_1 + 2x_2 : -x_1 + 3x_2 \le 12,~ x_1 + 2x_2 \le 12,~ 3x_1 - 2x_2 \le 8,~ x_1, x_2 \ge 0\}$ has optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x} = (5, \frac{7}{2})$. The constraint $x_1 + x_2 \le 6$ is added, with slack variable $x_6$, and the optimal basis is extended with $x_6$. What is concluded?
  1. $x_6 = \frac{5}{2}$: the extended basis remains optimal
  2. $x_6 = 6$: the extended basis remains optimal
  3. $x_6 = -\frac{5}{2}$: re-optimize with the simplex algorithm, with $x_6$ as the first pivot column
  4. $x_6 = -\frac{5}{2}$: re-optimize with the dual simplex algorithm, with $x_6$ as the first pivot row
Explanation. $x_6 = 6 - (5 + \frac{7}{2}) = -\frac{5}{2} < 0$: the extended basis stays dual feasible (the reduced costs do not change) but it is not primal feasible, hence the dual simplex algorithm is used.
lp-f-14-14 · level facile · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$), a single datum is changed. Which changes can make the basis lose primal feasibility ($\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$), so that one re-optimizes with the dual simplex algorithm?
  1. The change of a cost $c_k$ and the addition of a new variable
  2. Any change of the data of the problem
  3. Only the addition of a new variable
  4. The change of a right-hand side $b_k$ and the addition of a new constraint
Explanation. $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ changes with ${\boldsymbol b}$, and a violated new constraint gives a negative slack variable. Costs and new variables affect instead the reduced costs, i.e., dual feasibility.
lp-f-14-15 · level facile · notes
In the sensitivity analysis of an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with optimal basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$ (with $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and reduced costs $\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$), a single datum is changed. Which changes can make the basis lose dual feasibility ($\bar{\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$), so that one re-optimizes with the simplex algorithm starting from the previous basis?
  1. Only the change of a right-hand side $b_k$
  2. The change of a right-hand side $b_k$ and the addition of a new constraint
  3. The change of a cost $c_k$ and the addition of a new variable
  4. None: the reduced costs do not depend on the data
Explanation. The reduced costs $\bar c_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_j$ depend on the costs, and a new variable brings a new reduced cost; in both cases the basis stays primal feasible.

15. The geometry of Linear Programming (14)

lp-f-15-01 · level facile · notes
When does a non-empty polyhedron $F \subseteq \R^n$ have dimension $k$?
  1. When it has exactly $k$ distinct vertices
  2. When it is defined by exactly $k$ linear constraints
  3. When it contains $k+1$ affinely independent points, but not $k+2$
  4. When $n - k$ variables are $0$ at each of its points
Explanation. The dimension is that of the affine hull: the number of independent directions along which one can move inside $F$.
lp-f-15-02 · level facile · notes
Which statement about the dimension of polyhedra is correct?
  1. $F \subseteq \R^n$ is full-dimensional if $\dim(F) = n$; $\dim(\emptyset) = -1$
  2. Every non-empty polyhedron in $\R^n$ has dimension $n$; $\dim(\emptyset) = 0$
  3. A polyhedron formed by a single point has dimension $1$
  4. The dimension of a polyhedron is the number of its variables
Explanation. A point has dimension $0$; the dimension is not the number of variables.
lp-f-15-03 · level facile · notes
What can be said about the non-empty feasible region $F = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with $\rank({\boldsymbol A}) \ge 1$?
  1. It is always full-dimensional: $\dim(F) = n$
  2. It is not full-dimensional: $\dim(F) \le n - \rank({\boldsymbol A})$
  3. It always has dimension $0$, since it is formed by vertices
  4. It always has dimension $\rank({\boldsymbol A})$, the number of independent constraints
Explanation. All the points of $F$ satisfy the equalities, and each independent equality reduces the dimension by one.
lp-f-15-04 · level facile · notes
An inequality ${\boldsymbol a}'{\boldsymbol x} \le \beta$ is valid for the polyhedron $F$ if it is satisfied by all the points of $F$. What is a face of $F$?
  1. Any convex subset of $F$
  2. The set $\{{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol a}'{\boldsymbol x} = \beta\}$ for any inequality ${\boldsymbol a}'{\boldsymbol x} \le \beta$
  3. The set of the points of $F$ satisfying all the constraints with equality
  4. The set $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in F : {\boldsymbol a}'{\boldsymbol x} = \beta\}$ for an inequality ${\boldsymbol a}'{\boldsymbol x} \le \beta$ valid for $F$
Explanation. The inequality must be valid (satisfied by all of $F$); $F$ and $\emptyset$ are faces of $F$.
lp-f-15-05 · level facile · notes
A face of the polyhedron $F$ is a set $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in F : {\boldsymbol a}'{\boldsymbol x} = \beta\}$, with ${\boldsymbol a}'{\boldsymbol x} \le \beta$ valid for $F$. What are the dimensions of a facet and of a vertex of $F$?
  1. Facet: $1$; vertex: $0$
  2. Facet: always $n - 1$; vertex: $0$
  3. Facet: $\dim(F)$; vertex: $1$
  4. Facet: $\dim(F) - 1$; vertex: $0$
Explanation. Facets are the largest proper faces, vertices the smallest non-empty faces (a point).
lp-f-15-06 · level teorema · notes
A vertex of a polyhedron $F$ is a face of dimension $0$; an extreme point of $F$ is a point which does not lie strictly between two distinct points of $F$. Which statement, for ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$, is correct?
  1. $\{{\boldsymbol x}\}$ is a vertex of $F$ if and only if ${\boldsymbol x}$ is an extreme point of $F$
  2. ${\boldsymbol x}$ is an extreme point if and only if it satisfies at least one constraint with equality
  3. Every extreme point is the midpoint of two distinct points of $F$
  4. Every point of the boundary of $F$ is an extreme point
Explanation. An extreme point does not lie strictly between two distinct points of $F$; the points inside an edge are on the boundary but not extreme.
lp-f-15-07 · level teorema · notes
Consider an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times (n+m)}$ and $\rank({\boldsymbol A}) = m$. Let $F$ be its feasible region. Which statement is correct?
  1. $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \in F$ is a basic feasible solution if and only if it is a vertex of $F$
  2. The basic feasible solutions are the interior points of $F$, not the vertices
  3. Every vertex of $F$ corresponds to one and only one basis ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}$
  4. Every basic solution, feasible or not, is a vertex of $F$
Explanation. The correspondence between bases and vertices is onto but not necessarily one-to-one: a degenerate vertex may have several bases.
lp-f-15-08 · level ipotesi · notes
Consider an LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{m \times (n+m)}$ and $\rank({\boldsymbol A}) = m$. In the proof that every basic feasible solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is a vertex of the feasible region $F$, which facts are used?
  1. The columns ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ with $\bar x_j > 0$ are independent, hence $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is an extreme point, and extreme points and vertices coincide
  2. Strong Duality and Complementary Slackness applied to the basic solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$
  3. The non-degeneracy of $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and Bland's rule, which avoid repeated bases
  4. The boundedness of $F$, which makes each of its points a convex combination of vertices
Explanation. If $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = \lambda{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol x}^{(2)}$, the difference ${\boldsymbol d}$ is supported on independent columns and ${\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol 0}$, hence ${\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol 0}$.
lp-f-15-09 · level teorema · notes
Which statement justifies, for LP problems in standard form, the graphical method and the search for an optimal solution among the vertices of the feasible region?
  1. If the feasible region is bounded, the unique optimum is a vertex
  2. Every LP problem with a non-empty feasible region has an optimal vertex
  3. If an LP problem admits an optimal solution, at least one vertex of the feasible region is optimal
  4. Every optimal solution of an LP problem is a vertex
Explanation. The optimal solutions may form a face of positive dimension, but among them there is at least one vertex; an unbounded problem has no optimum.
lp-f-15-10 · level ipotesi · notes
For an LP problem in standard form, from which results does it follow that, if an optimal solution exists, a vertex of the feasible region is optimal?
  1. From Strong Duality and Complementary Slackness applied to the pair
  2. From the boundedness of the feasible region and the linearity of the objective function
  3. From Bland's rule and the definition of face of the polyhedron
  4. From the Fundamental Theorem and the equivalence between basic feasible solutions and vertices
Explanation. It is a geometric reading of the Fundamental Theorem: a basic optimum is a vertex.
lp-f-15-11 · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard form with feasible region $F$, what does a pivoting operation do geometrically?
  1. It always moves to an adjacent vertex along an edge, even when it is degenerate
  2. It moves to an interior point of $F$ along the direction of the gradient ${\boldsymbol c}$
  3. If non-degenerate it moves to an adjacent vertex along an edge; if degenerate it stays at the same vertex
  4. It moves to any vertex with a larger value, even one not adjacent to the current one
Explanation. Two vertices are adjacent if the segment joining them is an edge; the simplex algorithm is a walk on the edges of the polyhedron.
lp-f-15-12 · level media · notes
What is the dimension of the polyhedron $F = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^3 : x_1 + x_2 + x_3 = 1,~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$?
  1. $3$
  2. $2$
  3. $1$
  4. $0$
Explanation. $F$ is the triangle with vertices $(1,0,0)$, $(0,1,0)$, $(0,0,1)$: the differences $(-1,1,0)$ and $(-1,0,1)$ are independent and the equality removes one dimension, $3 - 1 = 2$.
lp-f-15-13 · level facile · notes
For the feasible region $F$ of an LP problem in standard maximization form, which correspondence between a geometric notion and an algebraic notion is correct?
  1. Vertex: basic solution, even infeasible
  2. Degenerate vertex: one single associated basis
  3. Edge between two adjacent vertices: non-degenerate pivoting operation
  4. Facet of $F$: basic variable
Explanation. A non-degenerate pivot moves from a vertex to an adjacent one along an edge. The vertices are the basic feasible solutions; a degenerate vertex is associated with several bases; a facet corresponds to a constraint which cannot be removed.
lp-f-15-14 · level facile · notes
The simplex algorithm visits one vertex of the feasible region at a time. Why is counting the vertices not enough to prove that it is an efficient algorithm?
  1. Because the simplex algorithm can also visit points which are not vertices
  2. Because the number of vertices can grow exponentially with the number of variables and constraints
  3. Because the vertices are infinitely many when the feasible region is unbounded
  4. Because every vertex requires an exponential number of pivoting operations
Explanation. For a problem in standard form the vertices are at most $\binom{n+m}{m}$, a number which can be exponential; the practical behaviour of the simplex algorithm is much better than its worst case.

Part: Modelling (14)

16. Linear Programming modeling techniques (14)

lp-f-16-01 · level facile · notes
Starting from the verbal description of a problem, in which order are the elements of a Linear Programming model written?
  1. Numerical data of the instance, constraints, objective function
  2. Constraints, decision variables, objective function
  3. Objective function, constraints, decision variables
  4. Decision variables, objective function, constraints
Explanation. First the decisions (with unit of measure and domain), then the quantity to optimize, then the requirements; the model is written with symbolic data.
lp-f-16-02 · level facile · notes
Which of the following is a frequent mistake when writing an LP model?
  1. Using rational instead of integer data
  2. Writing a minimization instead of a maximization problem
  3. Using equality constraints
  4. Forgetting the non-negativity constraints of the variables
Explanation. The other two are constraints that are not dimensionally consistent and the use of undefined variables.
lp-f-16-03 · level facile · notes
In a blending problem with $r$ raw materials, $x_\ell \ge 0$ is the quantity of raw material $\ell$ in the blend. How is the requirement «raw material $j$ is at least a fraction $\alpha \in (0,1)$ of the blend» written linearly?
  1. $x_j \ge \alpha \sum_{\ell=1}^r x_\ell$
  2. $x_j / \sum_{\ell=1}^r x_\ell \ge \alpha$
  3. $x_j \ge \alpha$
  4. $\alpha\,x_j \ge \sum_{\ell=1}^r x_\ell$
Explanation. Both sides are multiplied by the denominator, which is positive; the ratio is not linear and $x_j \ge \alpha$ confuses quantity and fraction.
lp-f-16-04 · level facile · notes
In the transportation model, $x_{ij} \ge 0$ is the quantity shipped from warehouse $i$ (availability $a_i > 0$) to client $j$ (demand $d_j > 0$), with constraints $\sum_j x_{ij} \le a_i$ and $\sum_i x_{ij} \ge d_j$. When does the model admit feasible solutions?
  1. Always
  2. If and only if $\sum_i a_i \ge \sum_j d_j$
  3. If and only if $a_i \ge d_j$ for every $i$ and $j$
  4. If and only if $\sum_i a_i = \sum_j d_j$
Explanation. With availability constraints $\le a_i$ and demand constraints $\ge d_j$ it is enough that the total supply covers the total demand.
lp-f-16-05 · level facile · notes
In the multi-period planning model, $x_t$ is the production of period $t$, $y_t$ the inventory at the end of period $t$ (with $y_0 = 0$) and $d_t$ the demand of period $t$. What is the inventory balance constraint of period $t$?
  1. $x_t - y_t = d_t$
  2. $y_t + x_t - y_{t-1} = d_t$
  3. $y_{t-1} + x_t - y_t = d_t$
  4. $y_{t-1} + x_t + y_t = d_t$
Explanation. Initial inventory plus production equals demand plus final inventory.
lp-f-16-06 · level facile · notes
In a model with variables $x_j$, how is the objective function $\min \max_i \{\sum_j a_{ij} x_j\}$ linearized with an auxiliary variable $w$?
  1. $\min w$ with $w \ge \sum_j a_{ij} x_j$ for every $i$, $w$ free
  2. $\max w$ with $w \ge \sum_j a_{ij} x_j$ for every $i$
  3. $\min \sum_i \sum_j a_{ij} x_j$
  4. $\min w$ with $w \le \sum_j a_{ij} x_j$ for every $i$
Explanation. $w$ is not smaller than each function, hence than the maximum, and minimizing it makes it equal to the maximum at the optimum.
lp-f-16-07 · level facile · notes
In a model with variables $x_j$, when can a term $|\sum_j a_{ij} x_j|$ of the objective function be replaced by a variable $w_i$ and the constraints $w_i \ge \sum_j a_{ij} x_j$, $w_i \ge -\sum_j a_{ij} x_j$?
  1. Only if $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j \ge 0$
  2. When it appears with a positive coefficient in an objective function to be minimized
  3. When it appears with a positive coefficient in an objective function to be maximized
  4. Always, whatever the sign of the coefficient
Explanation. Minimization makes $w_i$ equal to the absolute value; when maximizing, $w_i$ could grow without bound.
lp-f-16-08 · level facile · notes
Which requirement cannot be modelled with linear constraints on continuous variables?
  1. A fixed cost paid only if an activity is performed
  2. A min-max objective function
  3. An absolute value with a positive coefficient in a minimization problem
  4. A ratio constraint with a positive denominator
Explanation. Fixed costs, yes/no decisions, indivisible quantities and either-or constraints need integer variables; the other three cases can be linearized.
lp-f-16-09 · level facile · notes
A maximization problem requires some variables to be integer. Ignoring the integrality requirements gives an LP model. What does its optimal value provide?
  1. An upper bound on the optimal value of the correct model
  2. A lower bound on the optimal value of the correct model
  3. No information
  4. The optimal value of the correct model
Explanation. The LP model is a relaxation: it has more feasible solutions, so in a maximization problem its optimum is not smaller.
lp-f-16-10 · level facile · notes
In the production planning model, $x_j \ge 0$ are the units produced of product $j$, $c_j$ the unit profit, $b_i$ the availability of resource $i$ and $a_{ij}$ the quantity of resource $i$ consumed by one unit of product $j$. What is the constraint of resource $i$?
  1. $\sum_i a_{ij} x_j \le b_i$
  2. $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j \ge b_i$
  3. $\sum_j c_j x_j \le b_i$
  4. $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j \le b_i$
Explanation. The total consumption of resource $i$ is $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j$ (sum over the products) and it must not exceed the availability $b_i$; the model is in canonical maximization form.
lp-f-16-11 · level facile · notes
In the production planning model with $r$ products and $s$ resources, $x_j \ge 0$ are the units of product $j$, $c_j$ the unit profit, $a_{ij}$ the consumption of resource $i$ per unit of product $j$ and $b_i$ the availability of resource $i$: $\max\{\sum_j c_j x_j : \sum_j a_{ij} x_j \le b_i~ \forall i,~ x_j \ge 0\}$. In which form is this LP problem?
  1. It is not an LP problem, since the units produced must be integer
  2. Standard maximization form
  3. Canonical maximization form
  4. Canonical minimization form
Explanation. Maximization with less-than-or-equal-to constraints and non-negative variables: canonical maximization form. The quantities are continuous variables in the LP model.
lp-f-16-12 · level facile · notes
$100$ kg of animal feed have to be obtained by mixing three raw materials, with $x_j \ge 0$ kg of material $j$. One kg of materials $1, 2, 3$ contains respectively $0.12$, $0.08$ and $0.03$ kg of proteins, and the feed must contain at least $8$ kg of proteins. What is the protein constraint?
  1. $0.12\,x_1 + 0.08\,x_2 + 0.03\,x_3 \ge 8$
  2. $0.12\,x_1 + 0.08\,x_2 + 0.03\,x_3 \ge 0.08$
  3. $0.12\,x_1 + 0.08\,x_2 + 0.03\,x_3 \le 8$
  4. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \ge 8$
Explanation. The total proteins are $\sum_j p_j x_j$ and must be at least $8$ kg. The value $0.08$ is the fraction $8/100$, which would have to be multiplied by the total quantity.
lp-f-16-13 · level facile · notes
In a transportation problem with $2$ warehouses and $3$ clients, $x_{ij} \ge 0$ is the quantity shipped from warehouse $i$ to client $j$. Client $2$ has demand $d_2 = 30$, which it must receive at least in full. What is its demand constraint?
  1. $x_{21} + x_{22} + x_{23} \ge 30$
  2. $x_{12} + x_{22} + x_{32} \ge 30$
  3. $x_{12} + x_{22} \ge 30$
  4. $x_{12} + x_{22} \le 30$
Explanation. Client $2$ receives from all warehouses $i = 1, 2$: $\sum_i x_{i2} \ge d_2$. The sum $x_{21} + x_{22} + x_{23}$ is what warehouse $2$ ships, and there is no third warehouse.
lp-f-16-14 · level facile · notes
In a multi-period model with $3$ periods, $x_t$ is the production and $y_t$ the inventory at the end of period $t$, with $y_0 = 0$ and balance $y_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + y_t$. With demands $d = (20, 30, 10)$ and productions $x = (30, 20, 10)$, what are the inventories $(y_1, y_2, y_3)$?
  1. $(10, -10, 0)$
  2. $(30, 50, 60)$
  3. $(-10, 0, 0)$
  4. $(10, 0, 0)$
Explanation. $y_1 = 0 + 30 - 20 = 10$, $y_2 = 10 + 20 - 30 = 0$, $y_3 = 0 + 10 - 10 = 0$. Forgetting the previous inventory gives $y_2 = -10$; $(30, 50, 60)$ is the cumulative production.

Generated questions (36)

Each generator builds a new instance, with different numbers, at every start: a few examples here.

1. Linear functions (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-insieme-livello · level facile · notes
Consider the linear function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 4\,x_1 - 5\,x_2$. Which of the following points belongs to the level set $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : f({\boldsymbol x}) = 26\}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 8 \\ -7 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. We have $4 \cdot 4 - 5 \cdot (-2) = 26$. The level sets are lines perpendicular to the gradient $\nabla f = (4, -5)$: moving along the gradient changes the value, the opposite point gives the value $-26$ and swapping the coordinates does not preserve the value.
Example 2 · lp-g-insieme-livello · level facile · notes
Consider the linear function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = -x_1 + 4\,x_2$. Which of the following points belongs to the level set $\{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : f({\boldsymbol x}) = -11\}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. We have $-1 \cdot (-1) + 4 \cdot (-3) = -11$. The level sets are lines perpendicular to the gradient $\nabla f = (-1, 4)$: moving along the gradient changes the value, the opposite point gives the value $11$ and swapping the coordinates does not preserve the value.
Example 1 · lp-g-variazione-direzione · level facile · notes
Consider the linear function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1 + 4\,x_2$ on $\R^2$, the point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = (-2, -1)$ and the direction ${\boldsymbol d} = (-3, 3)$. What is the change $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x} + 2\,{\boldsymbol d}) - f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$?
  1. $-30$
  2. $9$
  3. $12$
  4. $18$
Explanation. By linearity $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x} + t\,{\boldsymbol d}) - f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = t\,{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol d} = 2 \cdot (1 \cdot (-3) + 4 \cdot 3) = 18$, whatever $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is; the value $12$ is $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x} + 2\,{\boldsymbol d})$, not the change.
Example 2 · lp-g-variazione-direzione · level facile · notes
Consider the linear function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = -x_1 + 4\,x_2$ on $\R^2$, the point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = (0, 3)$ and the direction ${\boldsymbol d} = (-1, -3)$. What is the change $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x} + 4\,{\boldsymbol d}) - f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$?
  1. $-44$
  2. $-11$
  3. $-32$
  4. $52$
Explanation. By linearity $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x} + t\,{\boldsymbol d}) - f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = t\,{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol d} = 4 \cdot (-1 \cdot (-1) + 4 \cdot (-3)) = -44$, whatever $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is; the value $-32$ is $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x} + 4\,{\boldsymbol d})$, not the change.

2. Half-spaces and polyhedra (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-poliedro-punto · level facile · notes
Consider the polyhedron $P = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : 2\,x_2 \le 2,~ x_1 - 2\,x_2 \le 1,~ 3\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 4\}$. Which of the following points belongs to $P$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The point $(1, 0)$ satisfies the three inequalities, so it belongs to the intersection of the three half-spaces. The others: $(-2, 3)$ violates constraint 1 ($6 > 2$); $(2, -1)$ violates constraint 2 ($4 > 1$); $(3, 1)$ violates constraint 3 ($11 > 4$).
Example 2 · lp-g-poliedro-punto · level facile · notes
Consider the polyhedron $P = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : -x_1 \le 0,~ x_1 - 3\,x_2 \le -5,~ -x_1 - 3\,x_2 \le -11\}$. Which of the following points belongs to $P$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 5 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The point $(2, 3)$ satisfies the three inequalities, so it belongs to the intersection of the three half-spaces. The others: $(-1, 5)$ violates constraint 1 ($1 > 0$); $(5, 2)$ violates constraint 2 ($-1 > -5$); $(1, 3)$ violates constraint 3 ($-10 > -11$).
Example 1 · lp-g-vincoli-attivi · level facile · notes
Consider the polyhedron $P = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : (1)~ -2\,x_1 + 3\,x_2 \le 6,~ (2)~ 2\,x_1 \le 4,~ (3)~ 2\,x_1 - 2\,x_2 \le -4,~ (4)~ 3\,x_1 + 3\,x_2 \le 10\}$ and its point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = (0, 2)$. A constraint is active at $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ if it is satisfied with equality, i.e., if $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ lies on its supporting hyperplane. Which constraints are active at $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$?
  1. constraints 2 and 4
  2. constraints 1 and 3
  3. constraints 1, 2, 3 and 4
  4. only constraint 1
Explanation. Substituting $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$: $(1)~ 6 = 6,~ (2)~ 0 < 4,~ (3)~ -4 = -4,~ (4)~ 6 < 10$. The constraints with equality are active; the others are satisfied with strict inequality.
Example 2 · lp-g-vincoli-attivi · level facile · notes
Consider the polyhedron $P = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : (1)~ -2\,x_1 - 2\,x_2 \le -3,~ (2)~ x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 2,~ (3)~ -x_1 \le 2,~ (4)~ x_1 - 3\,x_2 \le 2\}$ and its point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = (2, 0)$. A constraint is active at $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ if it is satisfied with equality, i.e., if $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ lies on its supporting hyperplane. Which constraints are active at $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$?
  1. constraints 1 and 3
  2. constraints 1, 2 and 4
  3. constraints 2 and 4
  4. constraints 1, 2, 3 and 4
Explanation. Substituting $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$: $(1)~ -4 < -3,~ (2)~ 2 = 2,~ (3)~ -2 < 2,~ (4)~ 2 = 2$. The constraints with equality are active; the others are satisfied with strict inequality.

3. Linear optimization problems (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-ottimo-una-variabile · level facile · notes
Consider the LP problem in one variable $\min\{x + 1 : x \ge -4\}$, with optimal solution $\tilde x$ and optimal value $z(LP)$ if they exist. Which statement is correct?
  1. $\tilde x = -4$, $z(LP) = -3$
  2. The problem is unbounded
  3. $\tilde x = -3$, $z(LP) = -2$
  4. $\tilde x = -4$, $z(LP) = -5$
Explanation. The coefficient of $x$ is $1$: in a minimization problem it pays to decrease $x$ up to the endpoint $\tilde x = -4$, with $z(LP) = 1 \cdot (-4) + 1 = -3$.
Example 2 · lp-g-ottimo-una-variabile · level facile · notes
Consider the LP problem in one variable $\max\{-x + 4 : -2 \le x \le 6\}$, with optimal solution $\tilde x$ and optimal value $z(LP)$ if they exist. Which statement is correct?
  1. $\tilde x = -2$, $z(LP) = 6$
  2. $\tilde x = -2$, $z(LP) = -2$
  3. $\tilde x = 6$, $z(LP) = -2$
  4. The problem is unbounded
Explanation. The coefficient of $x$ is $-1$: in a maximization problem it pays to decrease $x$ up to the endpoint $\tilde x = -2$, with $z(LP) = -1 \cdot (-2) + 4 = 6$.
Example 1 · lp-g-proprieta-scala · level facile · notes
Let $F \subseteq \R^n$ be the feasible region of an LP problem and assume that $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\} = 0$ and $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\} = 6$. What is $\min\{2\,{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} + 5 : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$?
  1. $5$
  2. $10$
  3. $17$
  4. $-5$
Explanation. The constant is added to the optimal value and the factor multiplies it, but with a positive factor the minimization stays as it is: $2 \cdot 0 + 5 = 5$.
Example 2 · lp-g-proprieta-scala · level facile · notes
Let $F \subseteq \R^n$ be the feasible region of an LP problem and assume that $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\} = -2$ and $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\} = 6$. What is $\max\{2\,{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} + 2 : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$?
  1. $14$
  2. $16$
  3. $-2$
  4. $8$
Explanation. The constant is added to the optimal value and the factor multiplies it, but with a positive factor the maximization stays as it is: $2 \cdot 6 + 2 = 14$.

4. Linear Programming problems (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-lower-bound · level facile · notes
Consider the LP problem in canonical maximization form $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 5\,x_1 + 6\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & -2\,x_1 + 3\,x_2 \le 10 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 4 \\ & 4\,x_1 - x_2 \le 6 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with optimal value $z(LP)$. Every feasible solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ gives the lower bound $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol c}'\bar{\boldsymbol x} \le z(LP)$. Which of the following statements is correct?
  1. $\bar x_1 = 0,~ \bar x_2 = 7$: feasible solution with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 42$
  2. $\bar x_1 = 0,~ \bar x_2 = 1$: feasible solution with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 5$
  3. $\bar x_1 = 1,~ \bar x_2 = 0$: feasible solution with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 5$
  4. $\bar x_1 = -2,~ \bar x_2 = 2$: feasible solution with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 2$
Explanation. The point $(1, 0)$ satisfies the three constraints and non-negativity, and $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 5 \cdot 1 + 6 \cdot 0 = 5 \le z(LP)$. The point $(0, 7)$ violates constraint 1, $(-2, 2)$ violates non-negativity and for $(0, 1)$ the correct value is $6$.
Example 2 · lp-g-lower-bound · level facile · notes
Consider the LP problem in canonical maximization form $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 6\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & -2\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 14 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 6 \\ & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 4 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with optimal value $z(LP)$. Every feasible solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ gives the lower bound $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol c}'\bar{\boldsymbol x} \le z(LP)$. Which of the following statements is correct?
  1. $\bar x_1 = 3,~ \bar x_2 = 2$: feasible solution with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 28$
  2. $\bar x_1 = 1,~ \bar x_2 = 0$: feasible solution with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 6$
  3. $\bar x_1 = -1,~ \bar x_2 = 2$: feasible solution with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 4$
  4. $\bar x_1 = 0,~ \bar x_2 = 3$: feasible solution with $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 18$
Explanation. The point $(1, 0)$ satisfies the three constraints and non-negativity, and $LB(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 6 \cdot 1 + 5 \cdot 0 = 6 \le z(LP)$. The point $(3, 2)$ violates constraint 2, $(-1, 2)$ violates non-negativity and for $(0, 3)$ the correct value is $15$.
Example 1 · lp-g-metodo-grafico · level media · notes
Solve with the graphical method the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 4\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 4\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \le 27 \\ & x_1 + 5\,x_2 \le 18 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$ (the optimal solution is unique). What are the optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ and the optimal value $z(LP)$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 0,~ \tilde x_2 = \frac{18}{5},~ z(\, LP \,) = \frac{72}{5}$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 3,~ \tilde x_2 = 3,~ z(\, LP \,) = 24$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = \frac{27}{4},~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = 27$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 18,~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = 72$
Explanation. The vertices of the feasible region other than the origin are $(\frac{27}{4}, 0)$: $27$, $(3, 3)$: $24$, $(0, \frac{18}{5})$: $\frac{72}{5}$: the maximum value is attained at $(\frac{27}{4}, 0)$. The point $(18, 0)$ is the intercept of the less restrictive constraint on the axis and it is not feasible.
Example 2 · lp-g-metodo-grafico · level media · notes
Solve with the graphical method the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 5\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 9 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \le 29 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$ (the optimal solution is unique). What are the optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ and the optimal value $z(LP)$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 2,~ \tilde x_2 = 5,~ z(\, LP \,) = 30$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = \frac{9}{2},~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = \frac{45}{2}$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 0,~ \tilde x_2 = \frac{29}{5},~ z(\, LP \,) = \frac{116}{5}$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = \frac{29}{2},~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = \frac{145}{2}$
Explanation. The vertices of the feasible region other than the origin are $(\frac{9}{2}, 0)$: $\frac{45}{2}$, $(2, 5)$: $30$, $(0, \frac{29}{5})$: $\frac{116}{5}$: the maximum value is attained at $(2, 5)$. The point $(\frac{29}{2}, 0)$ is the intercept of the less restrictive constraint on the axis and it is not feasible.

5. Possible outcomes and equivalent formulations (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-forme-equivalenti · level facile · notes
Consider the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & -4\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 - 2\,x_3 - 2\,x_4 \\ \text{subject to} & -3\,x_1 - 3\,x_2 - x_3 + x_4 \le -2 \\ & x_2 + 3\,x_3 - 3\,x_4 = 5 \\ & -x_1 + 5\,x_2 - x_3 \ge -2 \\ & -2\,x_1 + x_2 + 3\,x_3 + 2\,x_4 = 3 \\ & x_1 \le 0,~ x_2 \gtreqless 0,~ x_3 \gtreqless 0,~ x_4 \ge 0 \end{array}$. The equivalent LP problem in standard maximization form is obtained with the usual transformations (a minimization problem becomes a maximization one by changing the sign of the objective function, $x_j = -x_j^+$ for the non-positive variables, $x_j = x_j^+ - x_j^-$ for the free variables, one slack or surplus variable for each inequality). How many variables and constraints does it have?
  1. $8$ non-negative variables and $4$ equality constraints
  2. $8$ non-negative variables and $6$ equality constraints
  3. $6$ non-negative variables and $4$ equality constraints
  4. $10$ non-negative variables and $4$ equality constraints
Explanation. The variables become $1 + 1 + 2 \cdot 2 = 6$ (each free variable requires two), plus one slack or surplus variable for each of the $2$ inequalities: $8$ in total. The constraints remain $4$, all equalities; the equality constraints receive no additional variable.
Example 2 · lp-g-forme-equivalenti · level facile · notes
Consider the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 6\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 + 3\,x_3 \\ \text{subject to} & 4\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 - 2\,x_3 \le 9 \\ & 5\,x_1 + 3\,x_2 + 3\,x_3 \ge -1 \\ & 2\,x_1 + x_2 - 3\,x_3 = -2 \\ & -x_1 - x_2 - 3\,x_3 \ge 4 \\ & x_1 \gtreqless 0,~ x_2 \ge 0,~ x_3 \ge 0 \end{array}$. The equivalent LP problem in standard maximization form is obtained with the usual transformations (a minimization problem becomes a maximization one by changing the sign of the objective function, $x_j = -x_j^+$ for the non-positive variables, $x_j = x_j^+ - x_j^-$ for the free variables, one slack or surplus variable for each inequality). How many variables and constraints does it have?
  1. $7$ non-negative variables and $4$ equality constraints
  2. $6$ non-negative variables and $4$ equality constraints
  3. $7$ non-negative variables and $5$ equality constraints
  4. $8$ non-negative variables and $4$ equality constraints
Explanation. The variables become $2 + 0 + 2 \cdot 1 = 4$ (each free variable requires two), plus one slack or surplus variable for each of the $3$ inequalities: $7$ in total. The constraints remain $4$, all equalities; the equality constraints receive no additional variable.
Example 1 · lp-g-esito-due-variabili · level media · notes
Consider the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & -4\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & x_1 + 3\,x_2 \le 10 \\ & 3\,x_1 + x_2 \ge 5 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$. Which statement about its outcome is correct?
  1. The LP problem has an optimal solution, with $z(LP) = 0$
  2. The LP problem has an optimal solution, with $z(LP) = 20$
  3. The LP problem is infeasible
  4. The LP problem has an optimal solution, with $z(LP) = 10$
Explanation. The feasible region is non-empty and no feasible direction makes the objective function grow without bound; the maximum over the feasible vertices is $z(LP) = 10$. The other values come from infeasible basic points or non-optimal vertices.
Example 2 · lp-g-esito-due-variabili · level media · notes
Consider the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 3\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 3\,x_1 - x_2 \le -5 \\ & 3\,x_1 + 3\,x_2 \ge 6 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$. Which statement about its outcome is correct?
  1. The LP problem is unbounded
  2. The LP problem is infeasible
  3. The LP problem has an optimal solution, with $z(LP) = 6$
  4. The LP problem has an optimal solution, with $z(LP) = 4$
Explanation. The feasible region is non-empty and contains a half-line along a direction ${\boldsymbol d} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ which respects the constraints and has ${\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol d} > 0$: the objective function grows without bound.

6. Simplex algorithm (3)

Example 1 · lp-g-pivot-nuovo-z · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form (all variables $x_j \ge 0$), consider the dictionary with $\pazocal{B} = \{2, 4, 5\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{1, 3\}$: $\begin{array}{rcl} x_2 & = & 1 - 2\,x_1 + 2\,x_3 \\ x_4 & = & 9 + x_1 + x_3 \\ x_5 & = & 1 + 2\,x_1 - x_3 \\ z & = & 9 + 3\,x_1 + 2\,x_3 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). A pivoting operation is performed with pivot column $x_1$ and the pivot row chosen by the ratio test. What is the value of $\bar z$ in the new dictionary?
  1. $\frac{19}{2}$
  2. $\frac{15}{2}$
  3. $12$
  4. $\frac{21}{2}$
Explanation. Ratio test: $t_m = \min\{\frac{1}{2}\} = \frac{1}{2}$ (only the rows with $\bar a_{i1} > 0$). The new value is $\bar z + t_m \, \bar c_{1} = 9 + \frac{1}{2} \cdot 3 = \frac{21}{2}$.
Example 2 · lp-g-pivot-nuovo-z · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form (all variables $x_j \ge 0$), consider the dictionary with $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 3, 5\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{2, 4\}$: $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & 8 - 3\,x_2 - 2\,x_4 \\ x_3 & = & 9 - 3\,x_2 - 2\,x_4 \\ x_5 & = & 8 - 3\,x_2 + 2\,x_4 \\ z & = & 5 + 5\,x_2 + 5\,x_4 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). A pivoting operation is performed with pivot column $x_4$ and the pivot row chosen by the ratio test. What is the value of $\bar z$ in the new dictionary?
  1. $9$
  2. $\frac{55}{2}$
  3. $25$
  4. $-15$
Explanation. Ratio test: $t_m = \min\{\frac{8}{2},~ \frac{9}{2}\} = 4$ (only the rows with $\bar a_{i4} > 0$). The new value is $\bar z + t_m \, \bar c_{4} = 5 + 4 \cdot 5 = 25$.
Example 1 · lp-g-esito-dizionario · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form (all variables $x_j \ge 0$), consider the dictionary with $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 2, 5\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{3, 4\}$: $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & 6 - x_3 - 3\,x_4 \\ x_2 & = & 0 - 3\,x_3 + 2\,x_4 \\ x_5 & = & 3 + 3\,x_3 + 3\,x_4 \\ z & = & 10 + 3\,x_3 - 3\,x_4 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). Pivoting follows Bland's rule (pivot column: the non-basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar c_j > 0$; pivot row: ratio test $\min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index); $t_m$ is the step given by the ratio test. Which of the following statements is correct?
  1. With Bland's rule a non-degenerate pivoting operation is needed, with $t_m = 1$.
  2. The basic feasible solution of the dictionary is optimal.
  3. With Bland's rule a degenerate pivoting operation is needed ($t_m = 0$).
  4. The LP problem is unbounded: the variable $x_3$ gives the certificate of unboundedness.
Explanation. The pivot column is $x_3$ and a row with $\bar a_{i3} > 0$ has $\bar b_i = 0$: the ratio test gives $t_m = 0$, the solution is degenerate and the value of $z$ does not change.
Example 2 · lp-g-esito-dizionario · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form (all variables $x_j \ge 0$), consider the dictionary with $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 3, 4\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{2, 5\}$: $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & 8 - x_2 + x_5 \\ x_3 & = & 7 - x_2 + 3\,x_5 \\ x_4 & = & 8 + 3\,x_2 + 3\,x_5 \\ z & = & 11 - 2\,x_2 + 2\,x_5 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). Pivoting follows Bland's rule (pivot column: the non-basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar c_j > 0$; pivot row: ratio test $\min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index); $t_m$ is the step given by the ratio test. Which of the following statements is correct?
  1. With Bland's rule a degenerate pivoting operation is needed ($t_m = 0$).
  2. The basic feasible solution of the dictionary is optimal.
  3. With Bland's rule a non-degenerate pivoting operation is needed, with $t_m = 2$.
  4. The LP problem is unbounded: the variable $x_5$ gives the certificate of unboundedness.
Explanation. The variable $x_5$ has a positive reduced cost and $\bar a_{i5} \le 0$ for every $i \in \pazocal{B}$: increasing it no basic variable becomes negative and $z$ grows without bound.
Example 1 · lp-g-simplesso-completo · level complessa · notes
Solve the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 6\,x_1 + 6\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & x_1 - x_2 \le 7 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 14 \\ & 2\,x_1 \le 4 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$ with the simplex algorithm, starting from the basis of the slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$ and using Bland's rule (pivot column: the non-basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar c_j > 0$; pivot row: ratio test $\min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index). What are the optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ and the optimal value $z(LP)$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 2,~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = 12$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 2,~ \tilde x_2 = \frac{5}{2},~ z(\, LP \,) = 27$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 0,~ \tilde x_2 = \frac{7}{2},~ z(\, LP \,) = 21$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 7,~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = 42$
Explanation. Pivoting operations: $x_1$ enters, $x_5$ leaves; $x_2$ enters, $x_4$ leaves. The final dictionary has all reduced costs negative, hence $(2, \frac{5}{2})$ with $z(LP) = 27$ is the unique optimal solution. The other options are a vertex visited earlier, non-optimal vertices or infeasible basic solutions.
Example 2 · lp-g-simplesso-completo · level complessa · notes
Solve the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 2\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 \le 15 \\ & 4\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 9 \\ & x_1 + 3\,x_2 \le 14 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$ with the simplex algorithm, starting from the basis of the slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$ and using Bland's rule (pivot column: the non-basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar c_j > 0$; pivot row: ratio test $\min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index). What are the optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ and the optimal value $z(LP)$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 0,~ \tilde x_2 = \frac{9}{2},~ z(\, LP \,) = 9$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 3,~ \tilde x_2 = \frac{11}{3},~ z(\, LP \,) = \frac{40}{3}$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 14,~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = 28$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = \frac{9}{4},~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = \frac{9}{2}$
Explanation. Pivoting operations: $x_1$ enters, $x_4$ leaves; $x_2$ enters, $x_1$ leaves. The final dictionary has all reduced costs negative, hence $(0, \frac{9}{2})$ with $z(LP) = 9$ is the unique optimal solution. The other options are a vertex visited earlier, non-optimal vertices or infeasible basic solutions.

7. Optimality conditions and pivot rules (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-pivot-bland · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form (all variables $x_j \ge 0$), consider the dictionary with $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 2, 6\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{3, 4, 5\}$: $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & 7 - x_3 - 3\,x_4 \\ x_2 & = & 9 - x_3 - 2\,x_4 + 3\,x_5 \\ x_6 & = & 3 - x_3 - 3\,x_5 \\ z & = & 3 + 4\,x_3 + 3\,x_4 + 3\,x_5 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). With Bland's rule (pivot column: the non-basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar c_j > 0$; pivot row: ratio test $\min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index), what are the pivot column and the pivot row?
  1. pivot column $x_3$, pivot row $x_6$
  2. pivot column $x_4$, pivot row $x_1$
  3. pivot column $x_3$, pivot row $x_1$
  4. pivot column $x_3$, pivot row $x_2$
Explanation. The pivot column is the non-basic variable with the smallest index and a positive reduced cost: $x_3$ ($\bar c_{3} = 4$), not the one with the largest reduced cost. Ratio test on the rows with $\bar a_{i3} > 0$: $t_m = \min\{\frac{7}{1},~ \frac{9}{1},~ \frac{3}{1}\} = 3$, attained by $x_6$ (smallest index in case of ties).
Example 2 · lp-g-pivot-bland · level facile · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form (all variables $x_j \ge 0$), consider the dictionary with $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 4, 6\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{2, 3, 5\}$: $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & 1 + 3\,x_2 + 3\,x_3 + x_5 \\ x_4 & = & 6 - x_2 + 2\,x_3 + x_5 \\ x_6 & = & 9 - 2\,x_2 + x_3 - 2\,x_5 \\ z & = & 4 + 3\,x_2 + 4\,x_3 + 5\,x_5 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). With Bland's rule (pivot column: the non-basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar c_j > 0$; pivot row: ratio test $\min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index), what are the pivot column and the pivot row?
  1. pivot column $x_2$, pivot row $x_4$
  2. pivot column $x_2$, pivot row $x_6$
  3. pivot column $x_5$, pivot row $x_6$
  4. pivot column $x_2$, pivot row $x_1$
Explanation. The pivot column is the non-basic variable with the smallest index and a positive reduced cost: $x_2$ ($\bar c_{2} = 3$), not the one with the largest reduced cost. Ratio test on the rows with $\bar a_{i2} > 0$: $t_m = \min\{\frac{6}{1},~ \frac{9}{2}\} = \frac{9}{2}$, attained by $x_6$ (smallest index in case of ties).
Example 1 · lp-g-ottimi-multipli · level media · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form (all variables $x_j \ge 0$), consider the dictionary with $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 2, 4\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{3, 5\}$: $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & 8 - x_3 - x_5 \\ x_2 & = & 8 + 3\,x_3 - 3\,x_5 \\ x_4 & = & 2 + 2\,x_3 + 3\,x_5 \\ z & = & 1 - x_3 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). The dictionary is optimal and the reduced cost of $x_5$ is zero. A pivoting operation is performed with column $x_5$ (pivot row from the ratio test, ties broken by the smallest index). What is obtained?
  1. $x_2$ leaves, the new solution has $x_5 = \frac{8}{3}$ and $z = 1$
  2. $x_1$ leaves, the new solution has $x_5 = 8$ and $z = 1$
  3. $x_2$ leaves, the new solution has $x_5 = 8$ and $z = 1$
  4. $x_2$ leaves, the new solution has $x_5 = \frac{8}{3}$ and $z = \frac{11}{3}$
Explanation. Ratio test: $t_m = \min\{\frac{8}{1},~ \frac{8}{3}\} = \frac{8}{3}$, attained by $x_2$. The value becomes $\bar z + t_m\,\bar c_{5} = \bar z = 1$ since $\bar c_{5} = 0$: another optimal basic feasible solution is obtained, hence the optimum is not unique.
Example 2 · lp-g-ottimi-multipli · level media · notes
In the simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form (all variables $x_j \ge 0$), consider the dictionary with $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 3, 5\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{2, 4\}$: $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & 8 - 2\,x_2 - x_4 \\ x_3 & = & 9 - 2\,x_2 - x_4 \\ x_5 & = & 8 - 2\,x_2 + 3\,x_4 \\ z & = & 5 - 4\,x_2 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). The dictionary is optimal and the reduced cost of $x_4$ is zero. A pivoting operation is performed with column $x_4$ (pivot row from the ratio test, ties broken by the smallest index). What is obtained?
  1. $x_3$ leaves, the new solution has $x_4 = 9$ and $z = 14$
  2. $x_3$ leaves, the new solution has $x_4 = 9$ and $z = 5$
  3. $x_1$ leaves, the new solution has $x_4 = 8$ and $z = 13$
  4. $x_1$ leaves, the new solution has $x_4 = 8$ and $z = 5$
Explanation. Ratio test: $t_m = \min\{\frac{8}{1},~ \frac{9}{1}\} = 8$, attained by $x_1$. The value becomes $\bar z + t_m\,\bar c_{4} = \bar z = 5$ since $\bar c_{4} = 0$: another optimal basic feasible solution is obtained, hence the optimum is not unique.

8. Phase 0 of the simplex algorithm (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-fase0-pivot-speciale · level facile · notes
Consider the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 5\,x_1 + x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & -x_1 - 3\,x_2 \le -6 \\ & -3\,x_1 - x_2 \le -8 \\ & x_1 - 2\,x_2 \le 6 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$ and its simplex auxiliary problem $\max\{-x_0 : \sum_j a_{ij} x_j - x_0 \le b_i~ \forall i,~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$, in standard form with the slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$ in the basis (infeasible initial dictionary). What does the special pivoting operation of Phase $0$ produce?
  1. $x_4$ leaves the basis and $x_0 = 8$
  2. $x_4$ leaves the basis and $x_0 = -8$
  3. no variable can leave the basis: the ratio test has no candidate
  4. $x_3$ leaves the basis and $x_0 = 6$
Explanation. In the special pivoting operation $x_0$ enters the basis and the basic variable with the most negative right-hand side leaves: $r \in \argmin\{b_i\}$, i.e., $x_4$ with $b_2 = -8$. After the pivot $x_0 = -b_2 = 8$ and $x_i = b_i - b_2 \ge 0$ for the other basic variables.
Example 2 · lp-g-fase0-pivot-speciale · level facile · notes
Consider the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 5\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le -8 \\ & 3\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 3 \\ & 3\,x_1 + 3\,x_2 \le -4 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$ and its simplex auxiliary problem $\max\{-x_0 : \sum_j a_{ij} x_j - x_0 \le b_i~ \forall i,~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$, in standard form with the slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$ in the basis (infeasible initial dictionary). What does the special pivoting operation of Phase $0$ produce?
  1. no variable can leave the basis: the ratio test has no candidate
  2. $x_5$ leaves the basis and $x_0 = 4$
  3. $x_3$ leaves the basis and $x_0 = -8$
  4. $x_3$ leaves the basis and $x_0 = 8$
Explanation. In the special pivoting operation $x_0$ enters the basis and the basic variable with the most negative right-hand side leaves: $r \in \argmin\{b_i\}$, i.e., $x_3$ with $b_1 = -8$. After the pivot $x_0 = -b_1 = 8$ and $x_i = b_i - b_1 \ge 0$ for the other basic variables.
Example 1 · lp-g-fase0-esito · level complessa · notes
Apply to the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & -x_1 + x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & x_1 \le 4 \\ & -x_1 - 3\,x_2 \le -2 \\ & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 8 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$ Phase $0$, i.e., the simplex auxiliary problem $\max\{-x_0 : \sum_j a_{ij} x_j - x_0 \le b_i~ \forall i,~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$ solved by the simplex algorithm after the special pivoting operation, and, if needed, the simplex algorithm; both use Bland's rule (pivot column: the non-basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar c_j > 0$; pivot row: ratio test $\min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index). Which statement is correct?
  1. The LP problem admits an optimal solution with $z(\, LP \,) = 8$.
  2. The LP problem admits an optimal solution with $z(\, LP \,) = -2$.
  3. The LP problem is unbounded.
  4. The LP problem is infeasible: the optimal value of the auxiliary problem is $-2$.
Explanation. Phase $0$ ends with optimal value $0$ (the problem is feasible) and gives the initial basis; after substituting the original objective function, the simplex algorithm ends with $z(LP) = 8$ at the vertex $(0, 8)$.
Example 2 · lp-g-fase0-esito · level complessa · notes
Apply to the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 4\,x_1 - 2\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 3\,x_1 + 3\,x_2 \le 5 \\ & x_1 - 2\,x_2 \le 4 \\ & -3\,x_2 \le -4 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$ Phase $0$, i.e., the simplex auxiliary problem $\max\{-x_0 : \sum_j a_{ij} x_j - x_0 \le b_i~ \forall i,~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0},~ x_0 \ge 0\}$ solved by the simplex algorithm after the special pivoting operation, and, if needed, the simplex algorithm; both use Bland's rule (pivot column: the non-basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar c_j > 0$; pivot row: ratio test $\min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index). Which statement is correct?
  1. The LP problem admits an optimal solution with $z(\, LP \,) = \frac{20}{3}$.
  2. The LP problem is unbounded.
  3. The LP problem admits an optimal solution with $z(\, LP \,) = -\frac{4}{3}$.
  4. The LP problem is infeasible: the optimal value of the auxiliary problem is $-4$.
Explanation. Phase $0$ ends with optimal value $0$ (the problem is feasible) and gives the initial basis; after substituting the original objective function, the simplex algorithm ends with $z(LP) = -\frac{4}{3}$ at the vertex $(\frac{1}{3}, \frac{4}{3})$.

9. Duality theory (4)

Example 1 · lp-g-scrivere-duale · level facile · notes
What is the dual problem of the LP problem in canonical maximization form $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 5\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 2 \\ & 4\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 10 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with the dual variable $\pi_i$ associated with constraint $i$?
  1. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 2\,\pi_1 + 10\,\pi_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,\pi_1 + 4\,\pi_2 \le 5 \\ & 2\,\pi_1 + 4\,\pi_2 \le 5 \\ & \pi_1, \pi_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  2. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 2\,\pi_1 + 10\,\pi_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,\pi_1 + 2\,\pi_2 \ge 5 \\ & 4\,\pi_1 + 4\,\pi_2 \ge 5 \\ & \pi_1, \pi_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  3. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 2\,\pi_1 + 10\,\pi_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,\pi_1 + 4\,\pi_2 \ge 5 \\ & 2\,\pi_1 + 4\,\pi_2 \ge 5 \\ & \pi_1, \pi_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  4. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 5\,\pi_1 + 5\,\pi_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,\pi_1 + 4\,\pi_2 \ge 2 \\ & 2\,\pi_1 + 4\,\pi_2 \ge 10 \\ & \pi_1, \pi_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
Explanation. The dual is $\min {\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ subject to ${\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c}$, ${\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$: the right-hand sides become the costs, and dual constraint $j$ uses column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$ (not the row) with direction $\ge$.
Example 2 · lp-g-scrivere-duale · level facile · notes
What is the dual problem of the LP problem in canonical maximization form $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 7\,x_1 + 6\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 5 \\ & -2\,x_2 \le 11 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with the dual variable $\pi_i$ associated with constraint $i$?
  1. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 5\,\pi_1 + 11\,\pi_2 \\ \text{subject to} & \pi_1 \ge 7 \\ & 4\,\pi_1 - 2\,\pi_2 \ge 6 \\ & \pi_1, \pi_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  2. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 5\,\pi_1 + 11\,\pi_2 \\ \text{subject to} & \pi_1 + 4\,\pi_2 \ge 7 \\ & -2\,\pi_2 \ge 6 \\ & \pi_1, \pi_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  3. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 5\,\pi_1 + 11\,\pi_2 \\ \text{subject to} & \pi_1 \le 7 \\ & 4\,\pi_1 - 2\,\pi_2 \le 6 \\ & \pi_1, \pi_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  4. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 7\,\pi_1 + 6\,\pi_2 \\ \text{subject to} & \pi_1 \ge 5 \\ & 4\,\pi_1 - 2\,\pi_2 \ge 11 \\ & \pi_1, \pi_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
Explanation. The dual is $\min {\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ subject to ${\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol c}$, ${\boldsymbol \pi} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$: the right-hand sides become the costs, and dual constraint $j$ uses column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$ (not the row) with direction $\ge$.
Example 1 · lp-g-duale-da-dizionario · level facile · notes
The LP problem in canonical maximization form $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 6\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 3\,x_1 + x_2 \le 20 \\ & x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 5 \\ & x_1 + 3\,x_2 \le 16 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$, has the optimal dictionary $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & 5 - 2\,x_2 - x_4 \\ x_3 & = & 5 + 5\,x_2 + 3\,x_4 \\ x_5 & = & 11 - x_2 + x_4 \\ z & = & 30 - 8\,x_2 - 6\,x_4 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). What is the optimal solution of the dual problem ($\tilde\pi_i$ is the dual variable associated with constraint $i$)?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (5, 0, 11)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (0, -6, 0)$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (0, 8, 0)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (0, 6, 0)$
Explanation. As in the proof of Strong Duality, $\tilde\pi_i = -\bar c_{2+i}$: the reduced cost of the slack variable $x_{2+i}$ with the opposite sign, and $0$ if the slack variable is basic. Check: $\sum_i b_i \tilde\pi_i = 20 \cdot 0 + 5 \cdot 6 + 16 \cdot 0 = 30 = z(LP)$.
Example 2 · lp-g-duale-da-dizionario · level facile · notes
The LP problem in canonical maximization form $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 5\,x_1 + x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 6 \\ & 5\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \le 15 \\ & 4\,x_1 + x_2 \le 14 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$, has the optimal dictionary $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & \frac{6}{5} - \frac{4}{5}\,x_2 - \frac{1}{5}\,x_3 \\ x_4 & = & 9 - x_2 + x_3 \\ x_5 & = & \frac{46}{5} + \frac{11}{5}\,x_2 + \frac{4}{5}\,x_3 \\ z & = & 6 - 3\,x_2 - x_3 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). What is the optimal solution of the dual problem ($\tilde\pi_i$ is the dual variable associated with constraint $i$)?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (-1, 0, 0)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (0, 9, \frac{46}{5})$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (0, 3, 1)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (1, 0, 0)$
Explanation. As in the proof of Strong Duality, $\tilde\pi_i = -\bar c_{2+i}$: the reduced cost of the slack variable $x_{2+i}$ with the opposite sign, and $0$ if the slack variable is basic. Check: $\sum_i b_i \tilde\pi_i = 6 \cdot 1 + 15 \cdot 0 + 14 \cdot 0 = 6 = z(LP)$.
Example 1 · lp-g-scarti-complementari · level media · notes
The solution $\tilde x_1 = 3,~ \tilde x_2 = 3$ is optimal for the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 3\,x_1 + 12\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 3\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 21 \\ & 4\,x_2 \le 12 \\ & 3\,x_1 + 3\,x_2 \le 22 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$. Using Complementary Slackness, what is the optimal solution of the dual problem ($\tilde\pi_i$ is the dual variable associated with constraint $i$)?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (2, 1, 0)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (-3, 3, 0)$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (9, 0, -8)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (1, 2, 0)$
Explanation. Constraint 3 is not active at $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$, hence $\tilde\pi_3 = 0$. Since $\tilde x_1, \tilde x_2 > 0$, both dual constraints are active: $3\,\pi_1 = 3$ and $4\,\pi_1 + 4\,\pi_2 = 12$ (columns of ${\boldsymbol A}$), which give $\tilde\pi_1 = 1$, $\tilde\pi_2 = 2$.
Example 2 · lp-g-scarti-complementari · level media · notes
The solution $\tilde x_1 = 2,~ \tilde x_2 = 4$ is optimal for the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 8\,x_1 + 16\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & x_1 \le 2 \\ & x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 18 \\ & 3\,x_1 + 3\,x_2 \le 21 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$. Using Complementary Slackness, what is the optimal solution of the dual problem ($\tilde\pi_i$ is the dual variable associated with constraint $i$)?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (-4, -4, 0)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (8, 2, 0)$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (-8, 0, \frac{16}{3})$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (4, 4, 0)$
Explanation. Constraint 3 is not active at $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$, hence $\tilde\pi_3 = 0$. Since $\tilde x_1, \tilde x_2 > 0$, both dual constraints are active: $\pi_1 + \pi_2 = 8$ and $4\,\pi_2 = 16$ (columns of ${\boldsymbol A}$), which give $\tilde\pi_1 = 4$, $\tilde\pi_2 = 4$.
Example 1 · lp-g-coppia-primale-duale · level complessa · notes
Solve the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 6\,x_1 + 6\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & x_1 - x_2 \le 7 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 14 \\ & 2\,x_1 \le 4 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$ with the simplex algorithm, starting from the basis of the slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$ and using Bland's rule (pivot column: the non-basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar c_j > 0$; pivot row: ratio test $\min\{\bar b_i / \bar a_{ip} : \bar a_{ip} > 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index). What is the optimal solution of the dual problem ($\tilde\pi_i$ is the dual variable associated with constraint $i$)?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (0, 0, 3)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (0, \frac{3}{2}, \frac{3}{2})$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (\frac{15}{2}, 0, 0)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (0, -\frac{3}{2}, -\frac{3}{2})$
Explanation. Pivoting operations: $x_1$ enters, $x_5$ leaves; $x_2$ enters, $x_4$ leaves. In the optimal dictionary $\tilde\pi_i = -\bar c_{2+i}$ (reduced cost of the slack variable $x_{2+i}$ with the opposite sign, $0$ if basic). Check with Strong Duality: $7 \cdot 0 + 14 \cdot \frac{3}{2} + 4 \cdot \frac{3}{2} = 27 = z(LP)$.

What is the optimal solution of the dual problem ($\tilde\pi_i$ is the dual variable associated with constraint $i$)?

  3. $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2, \tilde\pi_3) = (0, -1, 0)$

In the optimal dictionary $\tilde\pi_i = -\bar c_{2+i}$ (reduced cost of the slack variable $x_{2+i}$ with the opposite sign, $0$ if basic).
