10. Dual Simplex algorithm (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-pivot-duale · level media · notes
In the dual simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form (all variables $x_j \ge 0$), consider the dual feasible dictionary ($\bar c_j \le 0$ for every $j$) with $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 2, 5\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{3, 4, 6\}$: $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & -4 - x_3 - 3\,x_4 + 3\,x_6 \\ x_2 & = & -3 + 2\,x_3 + 2\,x_4 + 3\,x_6 \\ x_5 & = & -7 + x_3 + 3\,x_6 \\ z & = & -5 - 3\,x_3 - 5\,x_4 - x_6 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). A dual pivoting operation is performed with Bland's rule of the dual simplex algorithm (pivot row: the basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar b_r < 0$; pivot column: $\min\{\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj} : \bar a_{rj} < 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index). What are the pivot row, the pivot column and the new value of $\bar z$?
  1. pivot row $x_1$, pivot column $x_6$, new $\bar z = -\frac{11}{3}$
  2. pivot row $x_1$, pivot column $x_4$, new $\bar z = -\frac{35}{3}$
  3. pivot row $x_5$, pivot column $x_6$, new $\bar z = -\frac{22}{3}$
  4. pivot row $x_1$, pivot column $x_6$, new $\bar z = -\frac{19}{3}$
Explanation. The pivot row is the basic variable with the smallest index and a negative value: $x_1$ ($\bar b_{1} = -4$). On the columns with $\bar a_{1j} < 0$: $s_m = \min\{\frac{-1}{-3}\} = \frac{1}{3}$, attained by $x_6$. The new value is $\bar z + s_m \bar b_{1} = -5 + \frac{1}{3} \cdot (-4) = -\frac{19}{3}$, which does not increase.
Example 2 · lp-g-pivot-duale · level media · notes
In the dual simplex algorithm for an LP problem in standard maximization form (all variables $x_j \ge 0$), consider the dual feasible dictionary ($\bar c_j \le 0$ for every $j$) with $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 5, 6\}$, $\pazocal{N} = \{2, 3, 4\}$: $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & -7 + 2\,x_2 - 3\,x_3 + 3\,x_4 \\ x_5 & = & -3 - 2\,x_3 - 2\,x_4 \\ x_6 & = & 2 + 3\,x_2 + 2\,x_3 \\ z & = & -1 - 3\,x_2 - 4\,x_3 - 3\,x_4 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). A dual pivoting operation is performed with Bland's rule of the dual simplex algorithm (pivot row: the basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar b_r < 0$; pivot column: $\min\{\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj} : \bar a_{rj} < 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index). What are the pivot row, the pivot column and the new value of $\bar z$?
  1. pivot row $x_1$, pivot column $x_4$, new $\bar z = -8$
  2. pivot row $x_1$, pivot column $x_4$, new $\bar z = 6$
  3. pivot row $x_1$, pivot column $x_3$, new $\bar z = -\frac{31}{3}$
  4. pivot row $x_1$, pivot column $x_2$, new $\bar z = -\frac{23}{2}$
Explanation. The pivot row is the basic variable with the smallest index and a negative value: $x_1$ ($\bar b_{1} = -7$). On the columns with $\bar a_{1j} < 0$: $s_m = \min\{\frac{-3}{-2},~ \frac{-3}{-3}\} = 1$, attained by $x_4$. The new value is $\bar z + s_m \bar b_{1} = -1 + 1 \cdot (-7) = -8$, which does not increase.
Example 1 · lp-g-simplesso-duale-completo · level complessa · notes
Solve the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & -8\,x_1 - x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 4\,x_1 - 2\,x_2 \le 5 \\ & -x_1 - 3\,x_2 \le 7 \\ & -3\,x_1 + x_2 \le -4 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$ with the dual simplex algorithm, starting from the basis of the slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$ (dual feasible since ${\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$) and using Bland's rule of the dual simplex algorithm (pivot row: the basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar b_r < 0$; pivot column: $\min\{\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj} : \bar a_{rj} < 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index). What are the optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ and the optimal value $z(LP)$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = \frac{5}{4},~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = -10$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 0,~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = 0$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = \frac{3}{2},~ \tilde x_2 = \frac{1}{2},~ z(\, LP \,) = -\frac{25}{2}$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = \frac{4}{3},~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = -\frac{32}{3}$
Explanation. Dual pivoting operations (row first, then column): $x_5$ leaves, $x_1$ enters; $x_3$ leaves, $x_2$ enters. The final dictionary has $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and negative reduced costs: $(\frac{3}{2}, \frac{1}{2})$ with $z(LP) = -\frac{25}{2}$. The intermediate solutions are infeasible and have a larger value.
Example 2 · lp-g-simplesso-duale-completo · level complessa · notes
Solve the LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & -6\,x_1 - 7\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & -x_1 \le -3 \\ & -x_1 + x_2 \le 5 \\ & -x_1 - 4\,x_2 \le -11 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$ with the dual simplex algorithm, starting from the basis of the slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$ (dual feasible since ${\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$) and using Bland's rule of the dual simplex algorithm (pivot row: the basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar b_r < 0$; pivot column: $\min\{\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj} : \bar a_{rj} < 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index). What are the optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ and the optimal value $z(LP)$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 0,~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = 0$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 0,~ \tilde x_2 = \frac{11}{4},~ z(\, LP \,) = -\frac{77}{4}$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 3,~ \tilde x_2 = 0,~ z(\, LP \,) = -18$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 3,~ \tilde x_2 = 2,~ z(\, LP \,) = -32$
Explanation. Dual pivoting operations (row first, then column): $x_3$ leaves, $x_1$ enters; $x_5$ leaves, $x_2$ enters. The final dictionary has $\bar{\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and negative reduced costs: $(3, 2)$ with $z(LP) = -32$. The intermediate solutions are infeasible and have a larger value.

11. Revised simplex algorithm (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-rivisto-pi · level facile · notes
In an LP problem in standard maximization form with ${\boldsymbol A} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 2 & 1 & 0 \\ 4 & 4 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol c}' = (1, 7, 0, 0)$ and ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, consider the basis $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 4\}$ (columns ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, costs ${\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}}$ of the basic variables in the order of $\pazocal{B}$). The revised simplex algorithm computes the row vector ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ by solving a linear system. What are ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ and the reduced cost $\bar c_2$ of the non-basic variable $x_2$?
  1. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (\frac{1}{2}, -2)$, $\bar c_2 = 14$
  2. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (\frac{1}{2}, 0)$, $\bar c_2 = -6$
  3. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (\frac{1}{2}, 0)$, $\bar c_2 = 6$
  4. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (\frac{1}{2}, -2)$, $\bar c_2 = -14$
Explanation. The first system is ${\boldsymbol \pi} \, {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}$ with ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 4 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and ${\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}} = (1, 0)$ (row vector on the left, not ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol \pi}' = {\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}}$): ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (\frac{1}{2}, 0)$. Then $\bar c_2 = c_2 - {\boldsymbol \pi} {\boldsymbol A}_2 = 6$.
Example 2 · lp-g-rivisto-pi · level facile · notes
In an LP problem in standard maximization form with ${\boldsymbol A} = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 3 & 1 & 0 \\ 2 & -2 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol c}' = (2, 3, 0, 0)$ and ${\boldsymbol b} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, consider the basis $\pazocal{B} = \{2, 3\}$ (columns ${\boldsymbol A}_j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$, costs ${\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}}$ of the basic variables in the order of $\pazocal{B}$). The revised simplex algorithm computes the row vector ${\boldsymbol \pi} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ by solving a linear system. What are ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ and the reduced cost $\bar c_4$ of the non-basic variable $x_4$?
  1. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (0, -\frac{3}{2})$, $\bar c_4 = \frac{3}{2}$
  2. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (0, -\frac{3}{2})$, $\bar c_4 = -\frac{3}{2}$
  3. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (0, 3)$, $\bar c_4 = 3$
  4. ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (0, 3)$, $\bar c_4 = -3$
Explanation. The first system is ${\boldsymbol \pi} \, {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}}$ with ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ -2 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and ${\boldsymbol c}'_{\pazocal{B}} = (3, 0)$ (row vector on the left, not ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol \pi}' = {\boldsymbol c}_{\pazocal{B}}$): ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (0, -\frac{3}{2})$. Then $\bar c_4 = c_4 - {\boldsymbol \pi} {\boldsymbol A}_4 = \frac{3}{2}$.
Example 1 · lp-g-rivisto-passo · level media · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for the LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with ${\boldsymbol A} = \begin{pmatrix} 5 & 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 2 & 3 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol b} = (9, 8)'$, ${\boldsymbol c}' = (1, 1, 0, 0)$, the current basis is $\pazocal{B} = \{2, 3\}$ and the pivot column is $x_1$ ($\bar c_1 > 0$). One computes $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and ${\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_1$ (entries in the order of $\pazocal{B}$) and the step $t_m$ of the ratio test. Which variable leaves the basis and what is $t_m$?
  1. $x_3$ leaves, $t_m = \frac{9}{5}$
  2. $x_2$ leaves, $t_m = \frac{4}{3}$
  3. $x_2$ leaves, $t_m = 4$
  4. $x_3$ leaves, $t_m = 45$
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 3 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ we get $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = (\frac{8}{3}, 9)'$ and ${\boldsymbol d} = (\frac{2}{3}, 5)'$ (system ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_1$, not transposed). Then $t_m = \min\{\frac{\frac{8}{3}}{\frac{2}{3}},~ \frac{9}{5}\} = \frac{9}{5}$, attained by $x_3$.
Example 2 · lp-g-rivisto-passo · level media · notes
In the revised simplex algorithm for the LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with ${\boldsymbol A} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 1 & 0 \\ 2 & 2 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol b} = (11, 4)'$, ${\boldsymbol c}' = (6, 8, 0, 0)$, the current basis is $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 3\}$ and the pivot column is $x_2$ ($\bar c_2 > 0$). One computes $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and ${\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_2$ (entries in the order of $\pazocal{B}$) and the step $t_m$ of the ratio test. Which variable leaves the basis and what is $t_m$?
  1. $x_3$ leaves, $t_m = 9$
  2. $x_3$ leaves, $t_m = 2$
  3. $x_1$ leaves, $t_m = 2$
  4. $x_1$ leaves, $t_m = 1$
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 2 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ we get $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = (2, 9)'$ and ${\boldsymbol d} = (1, 1)'$ (system ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol d} = {\boldsymbol A}_2$, not transposed). Then $t_m = \min\{\frac{2}{1},~ \frac{9}{1}\} = 2$, attained by $x_1$.

12. Revised dual simplex algorithm (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-rivisto-duale-u · level facile · notes
In an LP problem in standard maximization form with ${\boldsymbol A} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 2 & 1 & 0 \\ 4 & 4 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol c}' = (1, 7, 0, 0)$, the current basis is $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 4\}$ and the pivot row of the revised dual simplex algorithm is $x_1$. Solving the second system ${\boldsymbol u} \, {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol e}'_{1}$ (${\boldsymbol e}_{1}$: unit vector of the position of $x_1$ in $\pazocal{B}$), what are ${\boldsymbol u}$ and the coefficient $\bar a_{13}$ of the row of $x_1$ in $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$?
  1. ${\boldsymbol u} = (\frac{1}{2}, -2)$, $\bar a_{13} = \frac{1}{2}$
  2. ${\boldsymbol u} = (2, 0)$, $\bar a_{13} = 2$
  3. ${\boldsymbol u} = (-\frac{1}{2}, 0)$, $\bar a_{13} = -\frac{1}{2}$
  4. ${\boldsymbol u} = (\frac{1}{2}, 0)$, $\bar a_{13} = \frac{1}{2}$
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 4 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, the vector ${\boldsymbol u} = {\boldsymbol e}'_{1} {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1} = (\frac{1}{2}, 0)$ is the row of ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ associated with $x_1$ (not the column). Then $\bar a_{13} = {\boldsymbol u} \, {\boldsymbol A}_{3} = \frac{1}{2}$.
Example 2 · lp-g-rivisto-duale-u · level facile · notes
In an LP problem in standard maximization form with ${\boldsymbol A} = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 3 & 1 & 0 \\ 2 & -2 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol c}' = (2, 3, 0, 0)$, the current basis is $\pazocal{B} = \{2, 3\}$ and the pivot row of the revised dual simplex algorithm is $x_3$. Solving the second system ${\boldsymbol u} \, {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol e}'_{3}$ (${\boldsymbol e}_{3}$: unit vector of the position of $x_3$ in $\pazocal{B}$), what are ${\boldsymbol u}$ and the coefficient $\bar a_{31}$ of the row of $x_3$ in $\bar{\boldsymbol A} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{N}}$?
  1. ${\boldsymbol u} = (1, \frac{3}{2})$, $\bar a_{31} = 2$
  2. ${\boldsymbol u} = (-\frac{1}{2}, \frac{3}{2})$, $\bar a_{31} = \frac{7}{2}$
  3. ${\boldsymbol u} = (-1, -\frac{3}{2})$, $\bar a_{31} = -2$
  4. ${\boldsymbol u} = (-2, 0)$, $\bar a_{31} = 2$
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ -2 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$, the vector ${\boldsymbol u} = {\boldsymbol e}'_{3} {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1} = (1, \frac{3}{2})$ is the row of ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}$ associated with $x_3$ (not the column). Then $\bar a_{31} = {\boldsymbol u} \, {\boldsymbol A}_{1} = 2$.
Example 1 · lp-g-rivisto-duale-passo · level media · notes
In the revised dual simplex algorithm for the LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with ${\boldsymbol A} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 2 & 1 & 0 \\ -1 & 4 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol b} = (10, 4)'$, ${\boldsymbol c}' = (-3, -4, 0, 0)$, the current basis $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 3\}$ is dual feasible, with reduced costs $\bar c_2 = -16$, $\bar c_4 = -3$. One uses Bland's rule of the dual simplex algorithm (pivot row: the basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar b_r < 0$; pivot column: $\min\{\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj} : \bar a_{rj} < 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index), where $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and $\bar a_{rj} = {\boldsymbol u}\,{\boldsymbol A}_j$ with ${\boldsymbol u}\,{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol e}'_r$. What are the pivot row and the pivot column?
  1. pivot row $x_1$, pivot column $x_2$
  2. pivot row $x_3$, pivot column $x_2$
  3. pivot row $x_1$, pivot column $x_4$
  4. pivot row $x_3$, pivot column $x_4$
Explanation. We have $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = (-4, 18)'$: the pivot row is $x_1$, the smallest index among the negative basic variables. Then ${\boldsymbol u} = (0, -1)$ and $\bar a_{12} = -4$, $\bar a_{14} = -1$: $\min\{\frac{-16}{-4},~ \frac{-3}{-1}\}$ is attained by $x_4$.
Example 2 · lp-g-rivisto-duale-passo · level media · notes
In the revised dual simplex algorithm for the LP problem in standard maximization form $\max\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ with ${\boldsymbol A} = \begin{pmatrix} 4 & -2 & 1 & 0 \\ 4 & 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol b} = (5, 1)'$, ${\boldsymbol c}' = (-4, -3, 0, 0)$, the current basis $\pazocal{B} = \{2, 4\}$ is dual feasible, with reduced costs $\bar c_1 = -10$, $\bar c_3 = -\frac{3}{2}$. One uses Bland's rule of the dual simplex algorithm (pivot row: the basic variable with the smallest index and $\bar b_r < 0$; pivot column: $\min\{\bar c_j / \bar a_{rj} : \bar a_{rj} < 0\}$, ties broken by the smallest index), where $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol b}$ and $\bar a_{rj} = {\boldsymbol u}\,{\boldsymbol A}_j$ with ${\boldsymbol u}\,{\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol e}'_r$. What are the pivot row and the pivot column?
  1. pivot row $x_2$, pivot column $x_3$
  2. pivot row $x_4$, pivot column $x_3$
  3. pivot row $x_2$, pivot column $x_1$
  4. pivot row $x_4$, pivot column $x_1$
Explanation. We have $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = (-\frac{5}{2}, 1)'$: the pivot row is $x_2$, the smallest index among the negative basic variables. Then ${\boldsymbol u} = (-\frac{1}{2}, 0)$ and $\bar a_{21} = -2$, $\bar a_{23} = -\frac{1}{2}$: $\min\{\frac{-10}{-2},~ \frac{-\frac{3}{2}}{-\frac{1}{2}}\}$ is attained by $x_3$.

13. The primal-dual method (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-primale-duale-passo · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$: column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$), the dual feasible solution ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ has dual slacks $s_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge 0$ and admissible set $\pazocal{J} = \{j : s_j = 0\}$. The restricted primal problem has optimal value $\xi(\pazocal{J}) > 0$, ${\boldsymbol y}$ is the optimal solution of its dual and the dual step ${\boldsymbol \pi} + \theta\,{\boldsymbol y}$ uses the largest $\theta$ keeping all $s_j \ge 0$. For the indices $j \notin \pazocal{J}$: $s_{1} = 7,~ {\boldsymbol A}_{1}'{\boldsymbol y} = 4$; $s_{2} = 7,~ {\boldsymbol A}_{2}'{\boldsymbol y} = 2$; $s_{4} = 8,~ {\boldsymbol A}_{4}'{\boldsymbol y} = 3$; $s_{7} = 5,~ {\boldsymbol A}_{7}'{\boldsymbol y} = -3$. What is $\theta$ and which index enters $\pazocal{J}$?
  1. $\theta = \frac{7}{4}$, the index $1$ enters the admissible set
  2. $\theta = \frac{5}{3}$, the index $7$ enters the admissible set
  3. $\theta = \frac{2}{7}$, the index $2$ enters the admissible set
  4. $\theta = \frac{7}{2}$, the index $2$ enters the admissible set
Explanation. $\theta = \min\{ s_j / {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} : j \notin \pazocal{J},~ {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} > 0\} = \min\{\frac{7}{4},~ \frac{7}{2},~ \frac{8}{3}\} = \frac{7}{4}$: the indices with ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} \le 0$ do not restrict the step. The minimum is attained by $j = 1$, whose dual slack becomes zero.
Example 2 · lp-g-primale-duale-passo · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$: column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$), the dual feasible solution ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ has dual slacks $s_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} \ge 0$ and admissible set $\pazocal{J} = \{j : s_j = 0\}$. The restricted primal problem has optimal value $\xi(\pazocal{J}) > 0$, ${\boldsymbol y}$ is the optimal solution of its dual and the dual step ${\boldsymbol \pi} + \theta\,{\boldsymbol y}$ uses the largest $\theta$ keeping all $s_j \ge 0$. For the indices $j \notin \pazocal{J}$: $s_{1} = 8,~ {\boldsymbol A}_{1}'{\boldsymbol y} = 2$; $s_{4} = 6,~ {\boldsymbol A}_{4}'{\boldsymbol y} = 3$; $s_{5} = 9,~ {\boldsymbol A}_{5}'{\boldsymbol y} = 3$; $s_{7} = 7,~ {\boldsymbol A}_{7}'{\boldsymbol y} = -2$. What is $\theta$ and which index enters $\pazocal{J}$?
  1. $\theta = 2$, the index $4$ enters the admissible set
  2. $\theta = 4$, the index $1$ enters the admissible set
  3. $\theta = \frac{1}{4}$, the index $1$ enters the admissible set
  4. $\theta = -\frac{7}{2}$, the index $7$ enters the admissible set
Explanation. $\theta = \min\{ s_j / {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} : j \notin \pazocal{J},~ {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} > 0\} = \min\{\frac{8}{2},~ \frac{6}{3},~ \frac{9}{3}\} = 2$: the indices with ${\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol y} \le 0$ do not restrict the step. The minimum is attained by $j = 4$, whose dual slack becomes zero.
Example 1 · lp-g-primale-duale-ammesso · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol A} = \begin{pmatrix} -2 & -2 & 3 & -2 \\ 0 & 1 & -1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}$ and ${\boldsymbol c}' = (1, -1, 2, 0)$, consider the dual solution ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (1, 1)'$ of the dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$. The admissible set is $\pazocal{J} = \{j : {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} = c_j\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$: column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$). Which set is obtained?
  1. $\pazocal{J} = \{4\}$
  2. $\pazocal{J} = \{2, 3\}$
  3. $\pazocal{J} = \{1, 4\}$
  4. $\pazocal{J} = \emptyset$
Explanation. The dual slacks $s_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ are $s_1 = 3,~ s_2 = 0,~ s_3 = 0,~ s_4 = 3$: they are all $\ge 0$, hence ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ is dual feasible, and $\pazocal{J}$ contains the indices with $s_j = 0$.
Example 2 · lp-g-primale-duale-ammesso · level facile · notes
In the primal-dual method for $\min\{{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b},~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, with ${\boldsymbol A} = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 3 & 2 & -2 \\ -2 & 2 & 1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$ and ${\boldsymbol c}' = (-7, 13, 9, 10)$, consider the dual solution ${\boldsymbol \pi} = (1, 3)'$ of the dual $\max\{{\boldsymbol b}'{\boldsymbol \pi} : {\boldsymbol A}'{\boldsymbol \pi} \le {\boldsymbol c}\}$. The admissible set is $\pazocal{J} = \{j : {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi} = c_j\}$ (${\boldsymbol A}_j$: column $j$ of ${\boldsymbol A}$). Which set is obtained?
  1. $\pazocal{J} = \{1, 4\}$
  2. $\pazocal{J} = \{2, 3, 4\}$
  3. $\pazocal{J} = \{1\}$
  4. $\pazocal{J} = \emptyset$
Explanation. The dual slacks $s_j = c_j - {\boldsymbol A}_j'{\boldsymbol \pi}$ are $s_1 = 0,~ s_2 = 4,~ s_3 = 4,~ s_4 = 3$: they are all $\ge 0$, hence ${\boldsymbol \pi}$ is dual feasible, and $\pazocal{J}$ contains the indices with $s_j = 0$.

14. Sensitivity analysis (3)

Example 1 · lp-g-sens-costo-fuori-base · level facile · notes
The LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 8\,x_1 + x_2 + 8\,x_3 \\ \text{subject to} & 3\,x_1 + 3\,x_2 + 4\,x_3 \le 18 \\ & 4\,x_2 + 3\,x_3 \le 14 \\ & x_1, x_2, x_3 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with slack variables $x_4, x_5$, has the optimal dictionary $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & 6 - x_2 - \frac{4}{3}\,x_3 - \frac{1}{3}\,x_4 \\ x_5 & = & 14 - 4\,x_2 - 3\,x_3 \\ z & = & 48 - 7\,x_2 - \frac{8}{3}\,x_3 - \frac{8}{3}\,x_4 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). Only the objective-function coefficient $c_2$ is changed. For which values of $c_2$ does the basis of this dictionary remain optimal?
  1. $c_2 \le 7$
  2. $c_2 \ge 8$
  3. $c_2 \le 8$
  4. $c_2 \le -6$
Explanation. The variable $x_2$ is non-basic: with $c_2 + \delta$ only its reduced cost changes, and it becomes $\bar c_2 + \delta = -7 + \delta$. The basis remains optimal if $\delta \le -\bar c_2 = 7$, i.e., $c_2 \le 1 + 7 = 8$.
Example 2 · lp-g-sens-costo-fuori-base · level facile · notes
The LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 6\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 + x_3 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 20 \\ & 5\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 + 4\,x_3 \le 7 \\ & x_1, x_2, x_3 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with slack variables $x_4, x_5$, has the optimal dictionary $\begin{array}{rcl} x_2 & = & \frac{7}{4} - \frac{5}{4}\,x_1 - x_3 - \frac{1}{4}\,x_5 \\ x_4 & = & 13 + 4\,x_3 + x_5 \\ z & = & \frac{35}{4} - \frac{1}{4}\,x_1 - 4\,x_3 - \frac{5}{4}\,x_5 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). Only the objective-function coefficient $c_1$ is changed. For which values of $c_1$ does the basis of this dictionary remain optimal?
  1. $c_1 \le \frac{1}{4}$
  2. $c_1 \ge \frac{25}{4}$
  3. $c_1 \le \frac{25}{4}$
  4. $c_1 \le \frac{23}{4}$
Explanation. The variable $x_1$ is non-basic: with $c_1 + \delta$ only its reduced cost changes, and it becomes $\bar c_1 + \delta = -\frac{1}{4} + \delta$. The basis remains optimal if $\delta \le -\bar c_1 = \frac{1}{4}$, i.e., $c_1 \le 6 + \frac{1}{4} = \frac{25}{4}$.
Example 1 · lp-g-sens-costo-in-base · level media · notes
The LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & x_1 + 7\,x_2 + x_3 \\ \text{subject to} & 3\,x_2 + 5\,x_3 \le 10 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 + 3\,x_3 \le 17 \\ & x_1, x_2, x_3 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with slack variables $x_4, x_5$, has the optimal dictionary $\begin{array}{rcl} x_1 & = & \frac{31}{6} + \frac{1}{6}\,x_3 + \frac{1}{3}\,x_4 - \frac{1}{2}\,x_5 \\ x_2 & = & \frac{10}{3} - \frac{5}{3}\,x_3 - \frac{1}{3}\,x_4 \\ z & = & \frac{57}{2} - \frac{21}{2}\,x_3 - 2\,x_4 - \frac{1}{2}\,x_5 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). Only the objective-function coefficient $c_1$ is changed. For which values of $c_1$ does the basis of this dictionary remain optimal?
  1. $0 \le c_1 \le 7$
  2. $0 \le c_1 \le 64$
  3. $-1 \le c_1 \le 6$
  4. $-5 \le c_1 \le 2$
Explanation. $x_1$ is basic: with $c_1 + \delta$ the reduced costs become $\bar c_j - \delta \bar a_{1j}$. They remain non-positive if $\max\{-1\} \le \delta \le \min\{63,~ 6\}$, i.e., $-1 \le \delta \le 6$; then $c_1 = 1$ is added.
Example 2 · lp-g-sens-costo-in-base · level media · notes
The LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 7\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 + 4\,x_3 \\ \text{subject to} & 3\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 6 \\ & 3\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 + 4\,x_3 \le 15 \\ & x_1, x_2, x_3 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with slack variables $x_4, x_5$, has the optimal dictionary $\begin{array}{rcl} x_2 & = & 3 - \frac{3}{2}\,x_1 - \frac{1}{2}\,x_4 \\ x_3 & = & \frac{9}{4} + \frac{1}{4}\,x_4 - \frac{1}{4}\,x_5 \\ z & = & 24 - \frac{1}{2}\,x_1 - \frac{3}{2}\,x_4 - x_5 \end{array}$ (in the row of $x_i$ the coefficient of $x_j$ is $-\bar a_{ij}$; the row of $z$ shows the value $\bar z$ and the reduced costs $\bar c_j$). Only the objective-function coefficient $c_3$ is changed. For which values of $c_3$ does the basis of this dictionary remain optimal?
  1. $c_3 \ge 0$
  2. $0 \le c_3 \le 10$
  3. $-4 \le c_3 \le 6$
  4. $-2 \le c_3 \le 8$
Explanation. $x_3$ is basic: with $c_3 + \delta$ the reduced costs become $\bar c_j - \delta \bar a_{3j}$. They remain non-positive if $\max\{-4\} \le \delta \le \min\{6\}$, i.e., $-4 \le \delta \le 6$; then $c_3 = 4$ is added.
Example 1 · lp-g-sens-termine-noto · level media · notes
The LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 6\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 3\,x_1 + x_2 \le 20 \\ & x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 5 \\ & x_1 + 3\,x_2 \le 16 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$, has the optimal basis $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 3, 5\}$ with ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & -3 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = \begin{pmatrix} 5 \\ 5 \\ 11 \end{pmatrix}$ (values of the basic variables, in the order of $\pazocal{B}$). Only the right-hand side $b_2$ is changed. For which values of $b_2$ does this basis remain optimal?
  1. $b_2 \ge 0$
  2. $-5 \le b_2 \le \frac{5}{3}$
  3. $\frac{10}{3} \le b_2 \le 10$
  4. $0 \le b_2 \le \frac{20}{3}$
Explanation. With $b_2 + \delta$ we need $\bar{\boldsymbol b} + \delta \, {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1} {\boldsymbol e}_2 \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, where ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1} {\boldsymbol e}_2 = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -3 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ is column 2 (not the row). It follows that $-5 \le \delta \le \frac{5}{3}$, and $b_2 = 5$ is added.
Example 2 · lp-g-sens-termine-noto · level media · notes
The LP problem $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 5\,x_1 + x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 6 \\ & 5\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \le 15 \\ & 4\,x_1 + x_2 \le 14 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$, with slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$, has the optimal basis $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 4, 5\}$ with ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1} = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{1}{5} & 0 & 0 \\ -1 & 1 & 0 \\ -\frac{4}{5} & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\bar{\boldsymbol b} = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{6}{5} \\ 9 \\ \frac{46}{5} \end{pmatrix}$ (values of the basic variables, in the order of $\pazocal{B}$). Only the right-hand side $b_1$ is changed. For which values of $b_1$ does this basis remain optimal?
  1. $b_1 \ge 0$
  2. $-6 \le b_1 \le 9$
  3. $0 \le b_1 \le 15$
  4. $-3 \le b_1 \le 12$
Explanation. With $b_1 + \delta$ we need $\bar{\boldsymbol b} + \delta \, {\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1} {\boldsymbol e}_1 \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$, where ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}^{-1} {\boldsymbol e}_1 = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{1}{5} \\ -1 \\ -\frac{4}{5} \end{pmatrix}$ is column 1 (not the row). It follows that $-6 \le \delta \le 9$, and $b_1 = 6$ is added.

15. The geometry of Linear Programming (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-dimensione-poliedro · level facile · notes
Consider the polyhedron $F = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^{4} : x_1 + x_2 + 3\,x_3 - 2\,x_4 = 3,~ 3\,x_1 - x_2 - x_3 - 2\,x_4 = -1,~ 4\,x_1 + 2\,x_3 - 4\,x_4 = 2,~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, which contains the point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = (3, 3, 1, 3)$ with all entries positive. The dimension $\dim(F)$ is the maximum number of affinely independent points of $F$ minus one. What is $\dim(F)$?
  1. $3$
  2. $2$
  3. $4$
  4. $1$
Explanation. Since $\bar{\boldsymbol x} > {\boldsymbol 0}$, no constraint $x_j \ge 0$ holds with equality on the whole of $F$: the only implicit equalities are those of the system, of rank $2$ (the third equation is the sum of the first two). Hence $\dim(F) = n - \rank({\boldsymbol A}) = 4 - 2 = 2$.
Example 2 · lp-g-dimensione-poliedro · level facile · notes
Consider the polyhedron $F = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^{4} : 2\,x_1 + x_2 + 3\,x_3 + 2\,x_4 = 15,~ x_1 + 3\,x_2 + 2\,x_3 - 2\,x_4 = 2,~ 3\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 + 5\,x_3 = 17,~ {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}\}$, which contains the point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = (1, 1, 2, 3)$ with all entries positive. The dimension $\dim(F)$ is the maximum number of affinely independent points of $F$ minus one. What is $\dim(F)$?
  1. $3$
  2. $2$
  3. $1$
  4. $4$
Explanation. Since $\bar{\boldsymbol x} > {\boldsymbol 0}$, no constraint $x_j \ge 0$ holds with equality on the whole of $F$: the only implicit equalities are those of the system, of rank $2$ (the third equation is the sum of the first two). Hence $\dim(F) = n - \rank({\boldsymbol A}) = 4 - 2 = 2$.
Example 1 · lp-g-base-vertice · level media · notes
Let $F = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 5,~ -x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 10,~ 3\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 6,~ x_1, x_2 \ge 0\}$ be the feasible region of an LP problem in canonical maximization form, brought to standard form with the slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$. Consider the basis $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 3, 4\}$ (basic variables) and its basic solution, with the non-basic variables at $0$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $(\bar x_1, \bar x_2) = (2, 0)$: it is a vertex of $F$
  2. $(\bar x_1, \bar x_2) = (-10, 0)$: it is infeasible, hence not a vertex of $F$
  3. $(\bar x_1, \bar x_2) = (0, 2)$: it is a vertex of $F$
  4. $(\bar x_1, \bar x_2) = (2, 0)$: it is infeasible, hence not a vertex of $F$
Explanation. The non-basic variables are $0$ and the basic ones solve ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol b}$: we get $(2, 0, 1, 12, 0)$. All the entries are non-negative: it is a basic feasible solution, hence a vertex of $F$.
Example 2 · lp-g-base-vertice · level media · notes
Let $F = \{{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : 2\,x_2 \le 4,~ 4\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 9,~ 2\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 5,~ x_1, x_2 \ge 0\}$ be the feasible region of an LP problem in canonical maximization form, brought to standard form with the slack variables $x_3, x_4, x_5$. Consider the basis $\pazocal{B} = \{1, 2, 5\}$ (basic variables) and its basic solution, with the non-basic variables at $0$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $(\bar x_1, \bar x_2) = (2, \frac{1}{4})$: it is a vertex of $F$
  2. $(\bar x_1, \bar x_2) = (\frac{1}{4}, 2)$: it is a vertex of $F$
  3. $(\bar x_1, \bar x_2) = (2, \frac{1}{4})$: it is infeasible, hence not a vertex of $F$
  4. $(\bar x_1, \bar x_2) = (\frac{1}{4}, 2)$: it is infeasible, hence not a vertex of $F$
Explanation. The non-basic variables are $0$ and the basic ones solve ${\boldsymbol A}_{\pazocal{B}}{\boldsymbol x}_{\pazocal{B}} = {\boldsymbol b}$: we get $(\frac{1}{4}, 2, 0, 0, -\frac{7}{2})$. Some entry is negative: the basic solution is infeasible and does not correspond to a vertex of $F$.

16. Linear Programming modeling techniques (2)

Example 1 · lp-g-modelli-dimensioni · level facile · notes
A company has to satisfy the demand $d_t$ of a product over $n = 8$ periods, with maximum production $p_t$ in every period. In the multi-period LP model, with variables $x_t$ (production of period $t$) and $y_t$ (inventory at the end of period $t$, with $y_0 = 0$ given), one inventory balance constraint and one capacity constraint per period, how many variables and how many constraints (excluding non-negativity) are there?
  1. $8$ variables and $16$ constraints
  2. $16$ variables and $16$ constraints
  3. $16$ variables and $8$ constraints
  4. $17$ variables and $16$ constraints
Explanation. The variables are $x_t$ and $y_t$ for $t = 1, \dots, n$: $2n = 16$ ($y_0 = 0$ is a datum). The constraints are $n$ inventory balance constraints and $n$ capacity constraints: $2n = 16$.
Example 2 · lp-g-modelli-dimensioni · level facile · notes
A company has to satisfy the demand $d_t$ of a product over $n = 11$ periods, with maximum production $p_t$ in every period. In the multi-period LP model, with variables $x_t$ (production of period $t$) and $y_t$ (inventory at the end of period $t$, with $y_0 = 0$ given), one inventory balance constraint and one capacity constraint per period, how many variables and how many constraints (excluding non-negativity) are there?
  1. $23$ variables and $22$ constraints
  2. $22$ variables and $11$ constraints
  3. $22$ variables and $22$ constraints
  4. $11$ variables and $22$ constraints
Explanation. The variables are $x_t$ and $y_t$ for $t = 1, \dots, n$: $2n = 22$ ($y_0 = 0$ is a datum). The constraints are $n$ inventory balance constraints and $n$ capacity constraints: $2n = 22$.
Example 1 · lp-g-modello-produzione · level media · notes
A company makes two products using two resources, available in quantities $13$ (resource 1) and $20$ (resource 2). Profits and consumptions per unit of product are: $\begin{array}{l|cc} & \text{product 1} & \text{product 2} \\ \hline \text{unit profit} & 9 & 2 \\ \text{resource 1 per unit} & 5 & 1 \\ \text{resource 2 per unit} & 2 & 1 \end{array}$. With $x_j \ge 0$ units of product $j$, which LP model maximizes the total profit?
  1. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 9\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 13 \\ & x_1 + x_2 \le 20 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  2. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 2\,x_1 + 9\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 + x_2 \le 13 \\ & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 20 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  3. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 9\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 + x_2 \le 13 \\ & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 20 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  4. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 9\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 + x_2 \ge 13 \\ & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \ge 20 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
Explanation. Objective function: total profit $9\,x_1 + 2\,x_2$ to maximize. One constraint per resource: the consumption $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j$ (row $i$ of the table, not column) does not exceed the availability $b_i$.
Example 2 · lp-g-modello-produzione · level media · notes
A company makes two products using two resources, available in quantities $34$ (resource 1) and $30$ (resource 2). Profits and consumptions per unit of product are: $\begin{array}{l|cc} & \text{product 1} & \text{product 2} \\ \hline \text{unit profit} & 5 & 8 \\ \text{resource 1 per unit} & 2 & 1 \\ \text{resource 2 per unit} & 2 & 5 \end{array}$. With $x_j \ge 0$ units of product $j$, which LP model maximizes the total profit?
  1. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 5\,x_1 + 8\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 34 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \le 30 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  2. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 5\,x_1 + 8\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 34 \\ & x_1 + 5\,x_2 \le 30 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  3. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 5\,x_1 + 8\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \ge 34 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \ge 30 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  4. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 8\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 34 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \le 30 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
Explanation. Objective function: total profit $5\,x_1 + 8\,x_2$ to maximize. One constraint per resource: the consumption $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j$ (row $i$ of the table, not column) does not exceed the availability $b_i$.
